# Painting Traditions - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to read an Indian painting as four things at once - a **support** (rock face, plastered wall, palm leaf, paper, cloth, glass, canvas), a **technique** (what was done to that support), a **patron** (who paid, and therefore who was meant to look), and a **subject** (what the patron wanted shown). It runs from ochre on quartzite in a Vindhyan rock shelter to a group of six painters who formed themselves into a modern collective after 1947, and it ends with the living traditions still being painted this year. Architecture owns the buildings that carry murals; sculpture and iconography own carved images and the doctrine of attributes; crafts and textiles own the fabric trades that Kalamkari sits inside; heritage policy owns law, status and institutions. What is owned here is the **painted surface** - who painted it, on what, with what, for whom, and how safely a claim about it can be made.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each opens with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates before it names, and only then hands over the technical word. Five habits are built deliberately, because they are the five places this topic is actually lost.

1. **Name the format before you describe the style.** Mural, miniature, scroll, album page, glass panel and printed oleograph are different objects with different economics. A sentence that begins "Indian painting was colourful and religious" has said nothing; a sentence that begins "a portable album page made for one viewer" has already begun an argument.
2. **Keep technique words honest.** *Fresco secco* is not *true fresco*, a petroglyph is not a painting, and *gesso* is not *gold leaf*. Technique vocabulary is the cheapest place to lose a mark and the cheapest place to earn one.
3. **Separate what survives from what was made.** Only two Pallava mural locations and two Chola mural temples survive. That is a statement about preservation. It is not a statement about how much was painted.
4. **Refuse the school stereotype.** "Mughal is naturalistic, Rajput is devotional, Pahari is lyrical" is a revision slogan that collapses on contact with the record: Mewar has court *tamasha* scenes, Bundi has Mughal and Deccani influence, Kota is noted for realism, and Mandi uses dark muted colour. Schools are families of workshops, not personality types.
5. **Never pair an artist with a work you cannot evidence.** The single commonest factual accident in this topic is inventing an attribution that sounds right. Where the record gives a name, use it; where it gives only a school, say only the school.

Everything is drawn in text. No plate, photograph or colour reproduction is needed to follow any lesson here.

**Boundary map.** Architecture topics own the excavation, plan and structural history of Ajanta, Ellora, Bagh, Sittanavasal, Badami, the Kailasanatha at Kanchi, Brihadeswara, Lepakshi's Veerbhadra temple and every fort and palace named here; this session uses the building only as the support, the patronage and the viewing context of a painted surface. Sculpture and iconography own carved images, *mudra* and attribute doctrine, and the Chola bronze; where a painted figure is identified by the same attribute system, the identification is used but the doctrine is not re-taught. Languages, scripts and manuscripts own the textual history of the works that were illustrated; this session owns the illustration, not the text's transmission. Crafts and textiles own the dyeing and printing trades, and Kalamkari sits on both sides of that line - it is taught here as a painted narrative, with its textile process routed. Heritage and conservation own protection law, institutional structure and every changeable status claim; geographical-indication facts are named here only where they identify a tradition, and their current registry status is routed. Modern Indian history owns Swadeshi politics and the national movement; this session owns what the Bengal School did to technique. Indian society owns the living conditions of practising painter communities; this session owns their material, motif and method.

## Lesson 1 - What this topic owns, and the four questions to ask of any painting

### Visual: four questions, and the specific error each one prevents

 every wall   wall painting    painting of    religious

*Four questions, four separate factual risks. In this topic the second and third cause the most damage: a technique word carried to the wrong site, and a court name carried to the wrong workshop.*

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

✅ **Fact.** This topic covers painting from prehistoric rock art through mural painting, miniature painting, modern painting, and folk and tribal painting. Its classificatory spine is explicitly five-fold: **prehistoric rock art**; **mural painting**; **miniature painting**; **modern painting**; and **folk and tribal painting**.

That five-fold list is not decoration. It is a format classification, and each format carries its own patronage structure and its own viewing public. Everything in the nineteen lessons hangs on it, which is why the dependency map above is organised by format rather than by century.

⚠️ **Analytical inference on why format beats chronology as the organising axis.** Several of these formats run simultaneously. Kerala murals were being painted in the sixteenth century while Mughal albums were being assembled and while Jain manuscript painting continued; Warli painting is being done now. A chronological account would interleave them into a single stream and lose the one thing that explains them - who was paying and what the object was for.

### 2. The six limbs: the classical vocabulary you are expected to be able to name

✅ **Fact.** In the third century AD, **Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra*** outlined the six principles - ***Shadanga*** - of Indian art. The record gives them as:

| Term | What it names |
|---|---|
| **Roopbheda** | Looks and appearance |
| **Pramana** | Proportion, measurement and structure |
| **Bhava** | Expression |
| **Lavanya Yojana** | Aesthetic composition |
| **Sadrishya** | Resemblance |
| **Varnikabhanga** | Use of brush and colours |

Read them as a checklist a trained painter was expected to satisfy, not as a theory of beauty. Two of the six - *Pramana* and *Varnikabhanga* - are about craft discipline; two - *Bhava* and *Lavanya Yojana* - are about effect; and two - *Roopbheda* and *Sadrishya* - are about correspondence to the thing depicted.

> 🔑 Recall aid: **R-P-B-L-S-V** - *Roop, Pramana, Bhava, Lavanya, Sadrishya, Varnikabhanga*. Appearance, proportion, expression, composition, resemblance, colour.

⚠️ **Where the six limbs are worth deploying.** In any question that asks what made Indian painting a *tradition* rather than a sequence of accidents. A codified six-part standard, attributable to a named third-century text, is the strongest single piece of evidence that painting was taught, transmitted and judged against agreed criteria.

### 3. The named painting forms, and the named painting styles

Two further vocabularies are recorded, and they are separate from each other.

✅ **Fact - painting forms named in ancient texts.**

| Form | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Lepya Chitra** | Isolated framed drawings |
| **Lekhya Chitra** | Line drawings or sketches |
| **Dhuli Chitra** | Floor paintings |
| **Pata Chitra** | Painting on cloth or palm leaves |

✅ **Fact - styles named in Vishakhadatta's *Mudrarakshasa*.**

| Style | Major feature |
|---|---|
| **Cauka Pitaka** | Isolated framed drawings |
| **Dighala Pitaka** | Long scrolls of paintings |
| **Yama Pitaka** | Isolated paintings |

⚠️ **The connection worth making, and the one not to make.** The *Pata Chitra* of the ancient list and the living Odisha **Pattachitra** of Lesson 18 share a name and a support - cloth or palm leaf - and the continuity of the word is worth one sentence. ❌ Do **not** convert that into a claim that the modern tradition descends unbroken from the ancient form; the record supplies the name in both places, not the lineage between them.

⚠️ Similarly, *Dighala Pitaka* - the long scroll - names a format that reappears in Phad, Patua and Cheriyal scroll painting. The format recurs. The claim that one caused the other is not supported and should not be written.

### 4. The three chronologies this topic keeps apart

Almost every painting here has more than one date attached, and collapsing them is the standard accident.

```text
  DATE 1: when the painting was made
          e.g. Lepakshi murals: 16th century AD
                    |
  DATE 2: when the school, style or phase it belongs to ran
          e.g. Pahari School: 17th-19th century
                    |
  DATE 3: when the site or tradition was found, recognised or protected
          e.g. Bhimbetka: noticed 1957; World Heritage 2003
                    |
  DATE 4 (traditions only): when the tradition received institutional
          recognition, e.g. Mysore Ganjifa GI status in 2008
```

✅ **Fact.** The **first discovery of rock paintings in India** was made in **1867-68** by the archaeologist **Archibald Carlyle**. ✅ **Fact.** The earliest prehistoric paintings were discovered at the **Bhimbetka Caves, Madhya Pradesh, in 1957**. Those two dates belong to date-line 3. They are facts about archaeology, not about painting.

### 5. The boundaries, stated so they never have to be re-argued

- **Architecture owns the building.** The excavation sequence at Ajanta, the vesara temple at Lepakshi, the plan of Kota Fort - all of that belongs elsewhere. Here the building is the wall the paint is on.
- **Sculpture and iconography own the carved image.** Sittanavasal is famous for Tirthankara sculpture *and* for murals; this session takes the murals.
- **Crafts and textiles own the fabric trades.** Kalamkari is taught here because it is drawn and painted narrative; its dye chemistry and its market belong there.
- **Heritage owns status.** Geographical-indication facts appear here only where they identify a tradition, and the registry's current position is not asserted from this session.
- **Modern history owns the politics.** The Bengal School's Swadeshi context is named; the movement's political history is not retold.
- **Indian society owns living communities.** Their painting - material, motif, method, transmission - is owned here.

⚠️ **Why the boundary is protective.** A painting answer that drifts into temple plan or into colonial politics loses the marks it was earning. A painting answer that stays with support, technique, patron and subject can afford to be short, because every sentence is evidence.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies three things an examiner can test directly and one thing that is worth more than all three. Directly testable: the six limbs and their source text; the two named form-and-style vocabularies; and the discovery dates of 1867-68 and 1957. Worth more: the format classification itself, which is the skeleton of every long answer on painting. An evolution question is answered by moving through formats and naming what changed about the payer at each step, and a candidate who has that skeleton does not need to remember a chronological list of schools.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating the *Shadanga* as an aesthetic theory rather than a six-part craft standard, and therefore being unable to say what each limb governs.
- Equating the ancient *Pata Chitra* with modern Odisha Pattachitra as a demonstrated lineage.
- Using the 1957 Bhimbetka discovery date as a date for the paintings.
- Writing "Indian painting" as one tradition when the record's own first move is to split it into five formats.
- Importing architectural description into a painting answer because the site is famous as a building.

### Revision notes

1. Five-format classification: prehistoric rock art; mural; miniature; modern; folk and tribal.
2. Four reading questions: support, technique, patron, subject - style comes last.
3. *Shadanga*, six limbs, from Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra*, third century AD: Roopbheda, Pramana, Bhava, Lavanya Yojana, Sadrishya, Varnikabhanga.
4. Painting forms from ancient texts: Lepya Chitra (framed drawings), Lekhya Chitra (line drawings), Dhuli Chitra (floor paintings), Pata Chitra (cloth or palm leaf).
5. Styles from *Mudrarakshasa*: Cauka Pitaka (isolated framed), Dighala Pitaka (long scrolls), Yama Pitaka (isolated paintings).
6. Archibald Carlyle, 1867-68: first discovery of rock paintings in India.
7. Bhimbetka, 1957: earliest prehistoric paintings discovered; the site was declared a World Heritage Site in 2003.
8. Four date-lines: made / phase / found or protected / institutionally recognised.
9. Boundaries: building to architecture; carved image to sculpture; dye trade to crafts; status to heritage; politics to modern history; community condition to society.
10. The mechanism sentence to reuse: when the payer changes, the size, portability and subject of painting change with them.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Indian painting began at Bhimbetka in 1957 and developed through the Shadanga aesthetic theory into the great fresco tradition of Ajanta." Identify every error and give the corrected sentence.

**Model answer:** There are four errors. First, 1957 is the year the Bhimbetka paintings were discovered, not made; the paintings there belong to the Upper Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Chalcolithic and early historic periods, and the first discovery of rock paintings anywhere in India was in 1867-68 by Archibald Carlyle. Second, the *Shadanga* is a set of six craft principles - appearance, proportion, expression, composition, resemblance and use of brush and colours - outlined in Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra* in the third century AD, so it is a codified standard rather than an aesthetic theory, and it cannot be the mechanism by which rock art "developed" into cave murals separated from it by tens of thousands of years. Third, there is no evidenced developmental line from rock art to mural painting at all; they are two of five separately classified formats. Fourth, Ajanta is not true fresco: it is fresco secco, painted on an already dried surface. A corrected sentence: "Rock painting, the earliest of five format-classes of Indian painting, is attested at sites such as Bhimbetka, discovered in 1957; separately, mural painting on prepared plaster - executed at Ajanta in fresco secco - represents a later and distinct format whose classical craft standard is recorded as the six-limbed *Shadanga*."

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a chronological list of famous sites is the same thing as a developmental account. It is not. The five formats coexist, overlap and, in the case of folk and tribal painting, continue into the present; the argument comes from patronage and support, not from a single line of descent.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The history of Indian painting is better told as a sequence of formats than as a sequence of styles." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is largely sound, and the examination turns on what each account can explain.

A style account arranges Indian painting as a parade of named manners - Ajanta's fluid line, Mughal naturalism, Kangra lyricism - and explains nothing about why they differ. A format account begins with the object. Rock art has no patron and no commission. A mural requires a prepared wall, an endowment large enough to build it, and a public admitted to see it. A miniature, restricted to not more than twenty-five square inches on palm leaf or paper, requires only a patron, an artist and portable material - which is why miniature centres multiplied where mural centres structurally could not. Company painting and oleograph print add a market. Folk traditions add a household and a ritual occasion.

The qualification is that format does not determine subject: the same miniature format carried courtly portraiture and Krishna devotion.

Format therefore explains the distribution; style remains necessary to describe it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = stating what a style account cannot explain 2 + the format ladder from rock to market with the portability mechanism 4 + the twenty-five-square-inch technical fact used as evidence rather than trivia 2 + the qualification that format does not fix subject 2.**

## Lesson 2 - Rock art: painting, engraving and cupule, and the three periods

CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three kinds of mark, distinguished by what happens to the rock

```text
                     A MARK ON A ROCK SURFACE
                               |
      +------------------------+------------------------+
      |                        |                        |
  PIGMENT ADDED          ROCK REMOVED            ROCK HOLLOWED
  ROCK PAINTING          ROCK ENGRAVING          CUPULE
                         (= PETROGLYPH)          small round
                         carved, incised         artificial
                         or pecked               depression,
                                                 hammered or pecked
      |                        |                        |
  mainly Upper           mainly Mesolithic        mainly Lower
  Palaeolithic and       and Neolithic            Palaeolithic
  Mesolithic in India    in India                 in India
      |                        |                        |
  Bhimbetka (MP)         Konkan Geoglyphs         Daraki-Chattan (MP)
  Lakhudiyar (UK)          (Maharashtra, Goa)     Bhimbetka (MP)
  Adamgarh Hills (MP)    Kupgal Hill (Karnataka)  Kupgal Hill (Karnataka)
  Gudahandi (Odisha)     Vikramkhol (Odisha)
                         Daraki-Chattan (MP)
```

*The distinction is physical, not stylistic: a painting adds material to the rock, an engraving takes material away, and a cupule is the simplest and oldest known form of rock art. Note that three sites appear in more than one column - a site is not restricted to one technique.*

### 1. What rock painting is, and the three periods

✅ **Fact.** Rock painting is a form of prehistoric art in which early humans created images by applying natural pigments or colours directly onto the surfaces of rock shelters, cave walls or open rock faces. In India, rock paintings generally belong to the **Upper Palaeolithic and Mesolithic** periods.

✅ **Fact.** The earliest Indian paintings have been reported from Upper Palaeolithic times. **Cockburn, Anderson, Mitra and Ghosh** were the early archaeologists who discovered a large number of sites in the Indian subcontinent.

The three-period sequence is the most testable content in this lesson, because each period is characterised by a *palette* and a *subject*, and the two shift together.

| Period | Dates given in the record | Colour | Subject and scale |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Upper Palaeolithic** | 40,000-10,000 BC | Ochre or *geru* mixed with lime and water is the commonest mineral; minerals produce red, white, yellow and green. White, dark red and green for large animals - bison, elephant, rhino, tigers. **Red for hunters, green mostly for dancers** (human figurines) | Large animals dominate; the walls of the rock-shelter caves were made of **quartzite** |
| **Mesolithic** | 10,000-4000 BC | **Red is the main colour** | Paintings are **smaller** than Upper Palaeolithic ones; commonest scenes are group hunting, grazing activity and riding |
| **Chalcolithic** | 2500-700 BC | **More use of green and yellow** | Mostly battle scenes of men riding horses and elephants; some figures carry bows and arrows, said to indicate preparedness for skirmishes |

⚠️ **Read the table as an argument, not a list.** The palette narrows in the Mesolithic to a dominant red and the images shrink; the subject shifts from the single large animal to the group activity - hunting, grazing, riding. By the Chalcolithic the subject is organised conflict between mounted men. Painting is tracking a change in what mattered to the people painting it, which is exactly the point a good answer makes.

### 2. Two further Chalcolithic facts that carry weight

✅ **Fact.** Paintings and samples of writing in the **Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi scripts** at these cave sites establish that the sites were **inhabited in the late historical periods**. This matters because it destroys the assumption that a rock-shelter site has a single prehistoric date: occupation and marking continued.

✅ **Fact.** Another set of Chalcolithic paintings at **Narsinghgarh, Madhya Pradesh**, shows **skins of spotted deer left for drying**, which gives credence to the theory that the art of **tanning skins** for shelter and clothing had by then been perfected.

⚠️ That second fact is the cleanest example in the whole topic of painting used as *technological* evidence rather than as religious or aesthetic evidence. It is worth carrying into any answer about what art can evidence.

### 3. Engravings and cupules, stated precisely

✅ **Fact.** **Petroglyphs** are images, symbols or patterns carved, pecked or engraved into rock surfaces by prehistoric or historic peoples; unlike paintings, which use pigments, petroglyphs are made by **physically removing part of the rock's surface**. Rock engraving is the same thing under its other name, and in India engravings belong mainly to the **Mesolithic and Neolithic** periods. Named examples: **Konkan Geoglyphs (Maharashtra and Goa), Kupgal Hill (Karnataka), Vikramkhol (Odisha), Daraki-Chattan (Madhya Pradesh)**.

✅ **Fact.** A **cupule** is the simplest and oldest known form of rock art - a small, round, artificial depression hammered or pecked into a rock surface by humans, mainly during the **Lower Palaeolithic** period in India. Named examples: **Daraki-Chattan (Madhya Pradesh), Bhimbetka Rock Shelters (Madhya Pradesh), Kupgal Hill (Karnataka)**.

❌ **The trap this closes.** "Rock art" is not a synonym for "rock painting". A question that lists Konkan or Vikramkhol among "rock paintings" is testing whether you know that those are engraving sites. The technical word for the removal technique is *petroglyph*, and it is the single most likely one-word answer in this lesson.

### 4. Two contextual facts about the wider corpus

✅ **Fact.** Many of the rock sites in the **Deccan** are dominated by depictions of **cattle** and seem to belong to a stage of **agro-pastoralism**. This is a regional pattern statement and it complements the period table above: where the Vindhyan sites move from big game to hunting groups to battle, the Deccan sites sit with the herd.

✅ **Fact.** To date rock art, the techniques used are **Carbon-14 dating, thermoluminescence, OSL and uranium/thorium techniques**.

⚠️ **Why the dating list is examinable and not merely technical.** It answers the question a sceptical examiner is really asking - on what basis is any of this dated at all? Naming four independent physical methods converts the period table from assertion into evidence.

✅ **Fact, recorded alongside the rock-art corpus.** **Dolmens, cairns, menhirs and cists** are kinds of megalithic burial located in various places in India. ⚠️ They are burial monuments, not paintings, and are noted here only because the record places them next to the rock-art material; do not describe them as rock art.

### 5. Bhimbetka, in the detail a question can reach

✅ **Fact - location and discovery.** The **Bhimbetka Rock Shelters** are located **south of Bhopal** in the **Vindhyan Range of Madhya Pradesh**, inside the **Ratapani Wildlife Sanctuary**. In **1957** the archaeologist **V. S. Wakankar** noticed the Bhimbetka rocks **out of a train window** while travelling from **Bhopal to Itarsi**, got off at the nearest railway station to explore, and so discovered one of the most magnificent rock art sites in the world.

✅ **Fact - status and scale.** Bhimbetka was declared a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2003**. It is **one of seven hills**. There are **642 rock shelters**, of which **nearly 400 have paintings, engravings and bruisings**.

✅ **Fact - periods and dominance.** The paintings belong to the **Upper Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Chalcolithic and early historic** periods; **most belong to the Mesolithic**, and as at most Mesolithic rock-art sites, **animals dominate**. Mesolithic paintings are also found at other Madhya Pradesh sites - **Kharwar, Jaora, Kathotia and Lakhajoar**.

✅ **Fact - what is depicted.** The paintings generally portray the everyday life of prehistoric humans, often represented as **stick-like figures**. **Twenty-nine species of animals** are depicted, **chital most often**, with elephants, bison, deer, peacocks, snakes, lizards, frogs and crabs. **Sixteen colours or shades** can be identified, **white and light red used most often**. A few paintings are in the **x-ray style**, showing inner organs in the body. The famous **Bhimbetka Boar** has a body like a boar but a **snout like a rhinoceros**.

✅ **Fact - four named shelters.**

| Shelter | What it contains |
|---|---|
| **No. 3, designated "Auditorium"** | So named for its long shape; the composition shows bulls, buffaloes, deer, a peacock, a tiger, **a left hand print of a child**, and an unfinished basket-like object |
| **No. 4** | **More than 400 figures** - animals, birds, humans and decorative designs; most are prehistoric, a few historical, i.e. from the **6th century BC onwards** |
| **No. 8** | Bands of **armour-clad soldiers and cavaliers** |
| **No. 9** | Paintings that differ from the rest of the site, executed in **red, also green and yellow**; a **flower pot, a horse and an elephant with a rider** on a **deep white base** |

⚠️ **The one interpretive line worth writing about Bhimbetka.** A single site holds four periods, sixteen shades, twenty-nine animal species, a child's handprint and armoured cavalry. That is not a "prehistoric site"; it is a continuously used surface, and the strongest thing to say about it is that it records **occupation over time**, not a moment.

### 6. Lakhudiyar

✅ **Fact.** **Lakhudiyar**, meaning **"one lakh caves"**, is a group of prehistoric caves on the banks of the **Suyal River** in **Almora district, Uttarakhand**. The paintings are mostly of **humans, animals and the weapons used by early humans**, and they are created **with the fingers** in **red, white and black**.

⚠️ **The comparison to make, in one line.** Bhimbetka is a brush-and-pigment site with sixteen identifiable shades; Lakhudiyar is a three-colour finger-painted site. The contrast is about technique and resource, and it is a better answer than "both are rock art sites".

### Examination use

This is one of the two highest-density objective lessons in the topic. The reliable items are: the painting-versus-petroglyph-versus-cupule distinction with its period attachments; the three-period palette and subject table, especially **red for hunters and green for dancers** in the Upper Palaeolithic and **red as the main Mesolithic colour**; the four dating techniques; and the Bhimbetka numbers - 642 shelters, nearly 400 painted, 29 animal species, 16 shades, World Heritage in 2003, discovered 1957 by V. S. Wakankar. For a written answer, the period table converts into a short argument about how subject matter tracks subsistence, and the Narsinghgarh drying skins and the Brahmi-script samples give two pieces of evidence that painting documents technology and occupation rather than only belief.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling an engraving site a painting site, or using "rock art" and "rock painting" as synonyms.
- Attaching the cupule to the Mesolithic; the record places cupules mainly in the **Lower** Palaeolithic.
- Reversing the Upper Palaeolithic colour code - it is **red for hunters, green mostly for dancers**.
- Saying the Bhimbetka paintings are Upper Palaeolithic; **most** are **Mesolithic**, though four periods are present.
- Treating 642 and 400 as interchangeable; 642 is the number of shelters, nearly 400 the number with marks.
- Supplying an absolute date for any individual Bhimbetka painting. The record gives period attributions and dating **methods**, not painting-level dates.

### Revision notes

1. Three kinds of rock art: painting (pigment added), engraving or petroglyph (rock removed), cupule (hollow pecked); India: paintings mainly Upper Palaeolithic and Mesolithic, engravings mainly Mesolithic and Neolithic, cupules mainly Lower Palaeolithic.
2. Engraving sites: Konkan Geoglyphs, Kupgal Hill, Vikramkhol, Daraki-Chattan. Cupule sites: Daraki-Chattan, Bhimbetka, Kupgal Hill.
3. Upper Palaeolithic, 40,000-10,000 BC: quartzite shelter walls; ochre/*geru* with lime and water; red, white, yellow, green; white, dark red and green for large animals; red for hunters, green mostly for dancers.
4. Mesolithic, 10,000-4000 BC: main colour red; smaller paintings; group hunting, grazing, riding.
5. Chalcolithic, 2500-700 BC: more green and yellow; battle scenes with horses and elephants; bows and arrows.
6. Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi script samples prove late-historical habitation of the cave sites.
7. Narsinghgarh, MP: spotted-deer skins drying - evidence for perfected tanning.
8. Deccan rock sites dominated by cattle, suggesting agro-pastoralism.
9. Dating methods: Carbon-14, thermoluminescence, OSL, uranium/thorium.
10. Bhimbetka: south of Bhopal, Vindhyan Range, inside Ratapani Wildlife Sanctuary; V. S. Wakankar, 1957, from a train between Bhopal and Itarsi; World Heritage 2003; one of seven hills; 642 shelters, nearly 400 painted; four periods, most Mesolithic; 29 animal species, chital commonest; 16 shades, white and light red commonest; x-ray style; Bhimbetka Boar with rhinoceros snout.
11. Shelters 3 (Auditorium, child's handprint), 4 (400+ figures), 8 (armour-clad soldiers and cavaliers), 9 (flower pot, horse, elephant with rider on deep white base).
12. Other Mesolithic sites in MP: Kharwar, Jaora, Kathotia, Lakhajoar.
13. Lakhudiyar: "one lakh caves", Suyal River, Almora, Uttarakhand; humans, animals, weapons; finger-painted in red, white and black.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the rock-art record can be used to support a claim about prehistoric subsistence and technology, and state precisely where that use must stop.

**Model answer:** Three separate strands support such a claim. First, the period-wise subject shift: Upper Palaeolithic paintings are dominated by large animals - bison, elephant, rhino, tiger - while Mesolithic paintings, executed mainly in red and at smaller scale, show group hunting, grazing and riding, and Chalcolithic paintings show men riding horses and elephants, some carrying bows and arrows. That progression is consistent with a shift from individual big-game encounter towards organised and finally mounted activity. Second, a regional pattern: many Deccan rock sites are dominated by cattle and appear to belong to a stage of agro-pastoralism. Third, direct technological evidence: the Chalcolithic paintings at Narsinghgarh in Madhya Pradesh show skins of spotted deer left for drying, which gives credence to the view that skin-tanning for shelter and clothing had been perfected. The use must stop at three points. The images are selections, not censuses, so absence of a subject is not absence of a practice. The dating is by physical technique - Carbon-14, thermoluminescence, OSL, uranium/thorium - applied to a site, not to each image. And the presence of Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi writing at these sites proves continued habitation into late historical periods, so a shelter's contents cannot be assumed to be of one date.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a rock-shelter site as a sealed prehistoric time capsule. Bhimbetka alone carries Upper Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Chalcolithic and early historic work on the same hill, and Shelter 4 mixes prehistoric figures with work from the sixth century BC onwards. The correct instinct on seeing a rock site is to ask *which layer*, not *which period*.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Prehistoric rock art is better evidence of how people lived than of what they believed." Critically examine with reference to the Indian record. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is defensible on the Indian evidence, and the criticism needed is about the limits of both halves.

The livelihood case is strong and specific. The subject matter changes with subsistence: Upper Palaeolithic work, in ochre and *geru* mixed with lime and water, is dominated by large animals - bison, elephant, rhino, tiger - with red reserved for hunters and green mostly for dancers; Mesolithic work, mainly red and smaller in scale, shows group hunting, grazing and riding; Chalcolithic work uses more green and yellow and shows battle scenes with horses and elephants, some figures carrying bows and arrows. Regionally, many Deccan sites are dominated by cattle and appear to belong to a stage of agro-pastoralism. At Narsinghgarh, spotted-deer skins shown drying give credence to the view that skin-tanning had been perfected. At Bhimbetka, twenty-nine animal species and stick-like human figures record everyday life directly.

The belief case is weaker but not absent. Green is used mostly for dancers, which implies an occasion rather than a subsistence act; the x-ray style at Bhimbetka, showing inner organs, is a convention rather than an observation; and a child's left handprint in Shelter 3 is a marking act with no economic content.

Two limits must be stated. Interpretation of belief rests on convention and analogy, not on text. And the same shelters carry Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi writing, so their contents span periods.

The proposition therefore holds as a statement about evidential security rather than about what the painters cared for.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the livelihood case built from the period palette-and-subject shift with named colours 5 + the Deccan agro-pastoral pattern and the Narsinghgarh tanning evidence 3 + a genuine counter-case from dancers, x-ray style and the handprint 4 + both limits, interpretive and stratigraphic 3.**

## Lesson 3 - What a mural is, what its technique word commits you to, and where they are

Progress: 3/19 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the definition and date span of Indian murals, the fresco secco discipline, the complete named site list, and the disclosed boundaries within it

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the mural definition and date span, the named mural-site list, the fresco secco technique statement and the six-colour palette record
CA search: "Indian mural painting technique fresco secco conservation 2026"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the technique ladder, and the one step Indian murals do not take

```text
   TRUE FRESCO (buon fresco)
   pigment in water applied to WET lime plaster;
   pigment binds chemically as the plaster carbonates
                    X   <---- the Indian mural record does NOT
                    |         make this claim for its major sites
                    |
   FRESCO SECCO  <--+---- THIS is the recorded Indian technique
   pigment applied to an ALREADY DRIED plaster surface;
   binder is in the paint, not in the wall
                    |
   TEMPERA-type handling on dry ground
                    |
   ------------------------------------------------
   what it means in practice:
     - the wall is prepared, then allowed to dry
     - the paint sits ON the surface, not IN it
     - the paint layer is therefore MORE vulnerable
       to flaking, damp and abrasion
     - which is one reason so little survives
```

*The technique word is not a label; it is a prediction about survival. A paint film sitting on a dried surface is more fragile than pigment bound into the wall, and every survival statement later in this session is downstream of this diagram.*

### 1. The definition and the span

✅ **Fact.** **Artworks on the walls or a solid structure are referred to as murals.** They have existed in India since ancient times and can be dated **between the 10th century BC and the 10th century AD**.

⚠️ **Two cautions on that span, both necessary.** First, the span is the record's statement about the *ancient* mural tradition, and mural painting plainly continues far beyond it - Lepakshi is sixteenth century, Kerala's tradition evolved in the sixteenth century, and Rajput and Pahari wall painting runs into the eighteenth and nineteenth. Treat the 10th-century-BC-to-10th-century-AD span as the ancient bracket, not as the end of murals. Second, the lower end of that bracket is far earlier than any surviving painted wall discussed in this session; it is a statement about the antiquity of the practice.

### 2. The site list, complete and as given

✅ **Fact.** Mural paintings are found across India at sites including **Ajanta and Ellora Caves, Armamalai Cave, Ravan Chhaya Rock Shelter, Bagh Caves, Tirumalaipuram Caves, Chithannavasal (Sittanavasal) Cave, and Badami Cave Temples**. They are **mostly in caves or rock-cut chambers**, and they **mainly depict Hindu, Buddhist and Jain religious themes**.

| Site | Region | Status in this session |
|---|---|---|
| **Ajanta** | Maharashtra | Taught in full, Lesson 4 |
| **Ellora** | Maharashtra | Taught in full, Lesson 5 |
| **Bagh** | Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh | Taught in full, Lesson 5 |
| **Sittanavasal (Chithannavasal)** | Tamil Nadu | Taught in full, Lesson 6 |
| **Armamalai** | Tamil Nadu | Taught in full, Lesson 6 |
| **Ravan Chhaya** | Keonjhar district, Odisha | Taught in full, Lesson 6 |
| **Tirumalaipuram** | Tamil Nadu | Named, with the record's own statement that **nothing significant remains now** - Lesson 6 |
| **Badami Cave Temples** | Karnataka | ❌ **Disclosed boundary.** Named in the mural-site list, but the record supplies **no description of a Badami painted programme** - no theme, date, cave number or technique. Nothing is supplied here from memory |

⚠️ **How to use the Badami row in an answer.** Name it as a site in the mural list and stop. If a question demands mural content, the safe move is to name Badami as a listed site while building the answer on Ajanta, Bagh, Sittanavasal, Armamalai and Ravan Chhaya, where described material exists. A candidate who invents a Badami mural subject loses more than the sentence is worth.

### 3. The technique discipline, stated once for the whole session

✅ **Fact.** Of the rock-cut Buddhist caves, the record states that **originally most of the caves had mural paintings - paintings done on already dried surfaces - but a few survive today**, and that the **fresco secco technique** is used **with six colours: white, red, yellow, black, green and blue**.

✅ **Fact.** For **Ajanta** the technique is given explicitly as **fresco secco, painted on a dry plaster surface**, and the Ajanta colours are recorded as **earth pigments - red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime, black from lamp soot**.

✅ **Fact.** For **Ellora**, the technique used was **fresco secco, like Ajanta, painted on dry plaster over rock walls and ceilings**.

✅ **Fact.** For **Sittanavasal**, the record states that **like Ajanta and Bagh, the mural paintings here were also painted using fresco technique with many mineral colours**.

⚠️ **The disclosed wording problem, and how to handle it.** The record uses "**fresco secco**" for Ajanta and Ellora explicitly, and the unqualified word "**fresco technique**" for Sittanavasal in a sentence that groups it with Ajanta and Bagh. Two readings are possible - that the word is used loosely there for the same dry-surface method, or that a different method is meant. The evidence does not settle it. ✅ The disciplined formulation for an answer: *"Sittanavasal is recorded as painted in the fresco technique with mineral colours, grouped with Ajanta and Bagh, whose method is specifically fresco secco - painting on an already dried surface."* That reports both statements and asserts neither beyond the record.

❌ **Absolute prohibition for this whole session.** Do not describe any Indian mural site as **true fresco** or **wet-plaster fresco**. The record's word for the method is dry-surface painting.

### 4. Six colours, and what a palette statement is worth

The two palette statements are worth putting side by side, because they are not the same kind of statement.

| Source of the statement | Palette given | What kind of claim it is |
|---|---|---|
| The rock-cut cave tradition generally | **Six colours: white, red, yellow, black, green, blue** | A count. Useful because it is checkable and because six is a small number - these are not unlimited palettes |
| Ajanta specifically | **Earth pigments: red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime, black from lamp soot** | A materials list. Useful because it names the *substance* behind each colour |

⚠️ **The one inference worth drawing.** Blue is the expensive colour in that list: **lapis lazuli**, a mineral, against ochres, lime and soot that are locally obtainable. A palette in which five colours are cheap and one is a traded mineral is itself patronage evidence. ❌ Do not extend this into claims about trade routes, quantities or cost, none of which the record supplies.

### 5. Three things a mural is, that a miniature is not

This is the comparison the rest of the session depends on, so it is set out now and used repeatedly.

| Property | Mural | Miniature |
|---|---|---|
| **Support** | A prepared wall or ceiling, fixed to a building | Palm leaf, vellum or paper; portable |
| **Who commissions it** | Whoever can build or endow the structure - a monastic establishment, a temple trust, a ruler | Anyone who can pay an artist and buy material |
| **Who sees it** | Whoever is admitted to the space; viewing is collective and in situ | One or a few viewers at a time, held in the hand |
| **What happens when the patron fails** | The painting stays where it is and decays with the building | The artist takes the skill elsewhere and paints for a new patron |

⚠️ **That last row is the engine of the whole miniature half of this topic.** It is why the contraction of one court's patronage seeded schools in several others, and why mural traditions could not multiply in the same way. Hold it.

### Examination use

Three examinable things come out of this lesson. First, the technique vocabulary - fresco secco against true fresco - which is testable directly and is also the most common silent error in written answers. Second, the mural site list, which supplies the named examples for any "where are Indian murals found" demand, together with the honest boundary at Badami and the record's own statement that nothing significant remains at Tirumalaipuram. Third, the mural-versus-miniature property table, which is the comparison an evolution question is really asking for. The six-colour palette and the Ajanta materials list are high-value supporting detail because they are specific enough to be checked.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "fresco" unqualified and letting a reader infer wet-plaster technique.
- Treating the 10th-century-BC-to-10th-century-AD span as the full life of Indian mural painting, when Lepakshi, Kerala, Rajasthani and Pahari murals all fall outside it.
- Supplying a subject, date or cave number for Badami paintings.
- Claiming surviving murals at Tirumalaipuram; the record says nothing significant remains.
- Treating the six-colour list and the Ajanta pigment list as competing accounts. They are a count and a materials list.
- Explaining poor mural survival by neglect alone, when the technique itself - paint on a dried surface - is part of the explanation.

### Revision notes

1. Mural = artwork on walls or a solid structure; recorded ancient span 10th century BC to 10th century AD; the practice continues well beyond it.
2. Named mural sites: Ajanta, Ellora, Armamalai, Ravan Chhaya, Bagh, Tirumalaipuram, Chithannavasal/Sittanavasal, Badami Cave Temples; mostly caves or rock-cut chambers; mainly Hindu, Buddhist and Jain themes.
3. Technique: fresco secco - painting on already dried surfaces - stated for the rock-cut caves generally, and for Ajanta and Ellora specifically. Never write "true fresco".
4. Rock-cut cave palette: six colours - white, red, yellow, black, green, blue.
5. Ajanta pigments: red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime, black from lamp soot.
6. Sittanavasal: "fresco technique with many mineral colours", grouped with Ajanta and Bagh; report both statements, resolve neither.
7. Badami is a named mural site with no described programme in the evidence - a boundary, not a gap to be filled.
8. Tirumalaipuram: nothing significant remains now.
9. Lapis lazuli is the one traded mineral in the Ajanta list; the rest are locally obtainable.
10. Mural versus miniature on four properties: fixed versus portable support; institutional versus individual commission; collective versus private viewing; decay in place versus artist mobility.
11. The mobility row explains why miniature schools multiplied and mural schools did not.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the phrase "the great frescoes of Ajanta" a technically wrong description, and what does the correct description predict about the condition of the paintings?

**Model answer:** It is wrong because "fresco" without qualification suggests *buon fresco*, in which pigment is applied to wet lime plaster and binds chemically into the wall as it carbonates. The recorded Indian technique is different: the murals are paintings done on **already dried surfaces**, and Ajanta's technique is specifically named as **fresco secco**, painted on a dry plaster surface, using earth pigments - red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime, black from lamp soot. The correct description carries a prediction. In fresco secco the paint film sits on the surface rather than within it, held by a binder in the paint rather than by the chemistry of the wall, so the paint layer is intrinsically more vulnerable to flaking, damp and abrasion. That is consistent with what the record reports: most of the rock-cut caves originally had mural paintings, but only a few survive. Technique is therefore part of the explanation of scarcity, alongside neglect, exposure and later overpainting.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking the fresco secco point is a vocabulary quibble. It is a causal claim. If you write "true fresco" you have implicitly claimed a durable chemically bound paint layer, and you then have no technical explanation for why so little survives - which forces you into the weaker argument that Indian murals were simply neglected.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Technique, not neglect, is the first explanation for the scarcity of surviving Indian mural painting." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The statement is a useful corrective that should be accepted with qualification.

The technical argument is genuine. Indian murals are recorded as paintings done on already dried surfaces, with Ajanta and Ellora named specifically as fresco secco. In that method the pigment layer rests on the plaster rather than binding into it, so it is intrinsically susceptible to flaking, damp and abrasion. The record's own summary fits: most of the rock-cut caves originally had mural paintings, and only a few survive. Support compounds it - a mural cannot be removed to safety when its building fails.

But "first" is not "only". Vijaya Cholishwaram retains only faintly visible lines; Tirumalaipuram retains nothing significant; Sittanavasal's paintings are not in their best-preserved state; and at Brihadeswara the Rajaraja-period work carries Nayaka-period superimpositions, which is loss by overpainting rather than by decay.

Technique sets the baseline fragility; exposure, abandonment and later repainting determine the actual outcome.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the fresco secco mechanism explained rather than named 3 + the immovability of a wall-bound support 2 + three named survival cases 3 + superimposition identified as a distinct, non-decay loss mechanism 2.**

## Lesson 4 - Ajanta: patrons, phases, programme and technique

Progress: 4/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Ajanta murals - dating, patronage, the two painting phases, the named paintings, the Bodhisattva trio, style, colour and technique

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: Ajanta in five columns, so the facts cannot be mixed up

```text
+-------------+------------------+---------------+---------------+-------------+
|  WHEN       |  WHO             |  WHERE        |  WHAT         |  HOW        |
+-------------+------------------+---------------+---------------+-------------+
| carved      | inscribed by     | approx. 29    | life of the   | FRESCO      |
| between the | Buddhist monks;  | caves, set    | Buddha -      | SECCO       |
| 2nd century | patronised by    | out in a      | historical    | painted on  |
| BC and the  | the VAKATAKA     | HORSE-SHOE    | and Jataka;   | DRY plaster |
| 7th century | kings, especially| shape, in     | Bodhisattvas; |             |
| AD          | King HARISENA    | volcanic rock | kings, queens,| earth       |
|             |                  |               | dancers;      | pigments:   |
| PAINTING    |                  |               | animals;      | red ochre   |
| PHASES:     |                  |               | floral and    | yellow ochre|
| Caves 9, 10 |                  |               | geometric     | lapis lazuli|
| = SHUNGA    |                  |               | designs       |  (blue)     |
| the rest    |                  |               |               | lime (white)|
| = GUPTA     |                  |               | STYLE: fluid  | lamp soot   |
|             |                  |               | lines,        |  (black)    |
|             |                  |               | graceful      |             |
|             |                  |               | figures,      |             |
|             |                  |               | naturalistic  |             |
|             |                  |               | expressions,  |             |
|             |                  |               | symbolic      |             |
|             |                  |               | gesture       |             |
|             |                  |               | (mudras)      |             |
+-------------+------------------+---------------+---------------+-------------+
```

*Five columns, five separable facts. The one most often merged is the first with the second: the excavation span runs from the second century BC to the seventh century AD, but the named royal patronage is Vakataka, and the painting phases are Shunga for two caves and Gupta for the rest.*

### 1. The frame: date, patron, form

✅ **Fact.** The Ajanta Caves have **one of the oldest surviving murals of the Indian subcontinent**. They were **carved between the 2nd century BC and the 7th century AD out of volcanic rocks**, and consist of a set of **approximately 29 caves set out in a horse-shoe shape**. They were **inscribed by Buddhist monks and patronised by the Vakataka kings, especially King Harisena**.

✅ **Fact.** The caves are **most popular for their mural paintings**. **Murals in Caves 9 and 10 belong to the Shunga period, while the rest belong to the Gupta period.**

⚠️ **How to deploy the two-phase fact.** It is the cleanest chronological handhold in the entire mural half of the topic, because it splits one site into two datable groups. In an answer it does two jobs: it shows Ajanta is not one moment, and it lets you use Ajanta as evidence for both an early and a classical phase without contradiction.

❌ **The error to avoid.** Do not compress "carved between the 2nd century BC and the 7th century AD" into a single date, and do not attach the Vakataka patronage to the whole span. The excavation span and the named royal patronage are different claims.

### 2. Theme and style, in the record's own terms

✅ **Fact - themes.** Life of the Buddha, **historical and Jataka tales**; Bodhisattvas; kings, queens, dancers; animals; floral and geometric designs.

✅ **Fact - style.** **Fluid lines, graceful figures, naturalistic expressions, and symbolic use of gesture (*mudras*).**

⚠️ **What the style list is really telling you.** Three of the four items - fluid line, graceful figure, naturalistic expression - are observations about handling. The fourth, symbolic gesture, is a statement that the painting uses a **code**. A viewer who does not read *mudra* sees a hand; a viewer who does, reads a meaning. This is the link to iconography, and the single sentence worth writing is that at Ajanta naturalistic handling and coded gesture operate together rather than against each other.

### 3. The named paintings, grouped so they can be recalled

✅ **Fact - Jataka scenes.** Scenes from the **Jataka stories**, including **Vessantara, Sibi, Shaddanta and Mahajanaka Jataka**, and the life of Gautama Buddha.

✅ **Fact - what a Jataka is.** The **Jataka Tales relate to the previous births of Gautama Buddha in both human and animal form**. The future Buddha may appear as **a king, an outcast, a god, or an elephant** - but in whatever form he **exhibits some virtue that the tale thereby inculcates**.

✅ **Fact - two Jataka scenes described.**

| Scene | What is shown |
|---|---|
| **Shibi Jataka** | **King Shibi offers his own flesh to save the pigeon** |
| **Matriposhaka Jataka** | **The ungrateful person saved by an elephant discloses the elephant's whereabouts to the king** |

✅ **Fact - the Bodhisattva trio in Cave No. 1**, all in the ***tribhanga* pose**:

| Bodhisattva | What he manifests |
|---|---|
| **Vajrapani** | **Protector and guide, a symbol of Buddha's power** |
| **Manjusri** | **Manifestation of Buddha's wisdom** |
| **Padmapani (Avalokiteshvara)** | **Symbol of Buddha's compassion** |

✅ **Fact - three further named paintings and groups.** **The Dying Princess in Cave 16**; **Caves 19 and 26** have **chaitya hall paintings with Buddha in a preaching posture, surrounded by devotees and celestial beings**; and a **reception given to a Persian embassy by the Chalukyan ruler King Pulakeshin II**.

✅ **Fact - a supplementary description of the Padmapani.** Bodhisattva Padmapani is described as **depicting the compassionate Buddha holding a lotus**, and the most notable scenes are recorded as being from the **Vessantara Jataka** and of **Padmapani and Vajrapani**; other notable paintings there include **The Dying Princess (Cave 16)** and **The King Janaka**.

✅ **Fact - important images or sculptures at Ajanta**, recorded alongside the paintings: **Mahaparinirvana of Buddha**; **the assault of Mara during Buddha's penance**; **seated Buddha in Dharmachakrapravartana mudra**. ⚠️ These are named as images or sculptures; keep them out of a list of paintings.

> 🔑 Recall aid for Cave 1's trio: **V-M-P = power, wisdom, compassion.** Vajrapani, Manjusri, Padmapani.

### 4. The Pulakeshin II panel, and why it is disproportionately useful

✅ **Fact.** Among the Ajanta paintings is the **reception given to a Persian embassy by the Chalukyan ruler King Pulakeshin II**.

⚠️ **Analytical significance, stated carefully.** This is the one Ajanta subject that is neither devotional nor Jataka: it is a **court and diplomatic** scene involving a named ruler of a dynasty that is not the site's recorded patron. Three uses follow. It shows that a Buddhist monastic mural programme could carry secular political content. It is a rare instance of painting being used as evidence of external contact. And it complicates any neat claim that Ajanta's subject matter is exclusively religious.

❌ **Three things not to do with it.** Do not date the panel. Do not assign Ajanta's patronage to the Chalukyas on the strength of it. Do not build an argument about Indo-Persian relations from a single painted reception.

### 5. Why Ajanta is the reference point for so much else

Three later claims in this session depend on Ajanta, and it is worth flagging them now so they are recognised when they arrive.

| Later claim | Its dependence on Ajanta |
|---|---|
| **Bagh's character** | Bagh's figures are described as **more tightly modelled, with stronger outlines, and more earthly and human** - a comparison whose implicit reference point is the Ajanta idiom |
| **Sittanavasal's place in a chain** | Sittanavasal's paintings are **said to provide a link between the Ajanta paintings (4th-6th century AD) and the Chola paintings of the 11th century at Thanjavur** |
| **Brihadeswara's scale** | Brihadeswara is described as **the biggest centre of wall painting in India after Ajanta** |
| **The Bengal School's idiom** | The Bengal School is recorded as drawing inspiration from **Ajanta art**, exhibiting its **rhythm, grace and harmony**, with lines that **mirror the Ajanta paintings** |

⚠️ **The observation worth one sentence in a long answer.** Ajanta is not merely the earliest major site; it is the **measuring stick** the rest of the tradition, and even a twentieth-century nationalist movement, is described against. That is a statement about the history of taste as well as about painting.

### Examination use

Ajanta is the single most heavily examined site in the topic and rewards precise recall rather than praise. The reliable facts are the horse-shoe layout and approximately twenty-nine caves; the second-century-BC-to-seventh-century-AD carving span; the Vakataka patronage and King Harisena; the Shunga phase in Caves 9 and 10 against the Gupta phase elsewhere; the Cave 1 Bodhisattva trio with each one's meaning and the *tribhanga* pose; The Dying Princess in Cave 16; the chaitya-hall paintings in Caves 19 and 26; the Pulakeshin II reception; and fresco secco with its five named pigments. For written answers, Ajanta supplies the classical benchmark against which Bagh, Sittanavasal, Brihadeswara and even the Bengal School are described, and the Pulakeshin panel supplies the evidence that a monastic programme carried secular subject matter.

### Traps introduced here

- Assigning all Ajanta murals to the Gupta period; Caves 9 and 10 are Shunga.
- Naming Padmapani as the Buddha; he is a Bodhisattva, the symbol of compassion, identified as Avalokiteshvara.
- Placing The Dying Princess in Cave 1; it is in Cave 16, which is also where the Cave 1 trio must not be relocated.
- Treating the Pulakeshin II panel as evidence of Chalukya patronage of Ajanta.
- Listing the Mahaparinirvana, the assault of Mara and the Dharmachakrapravartana Buddha among the paintings; they are recorded as images or sculptures.
- Calling the technique true fresco, or omitting lapis lazuli when the pigments are listed.

### Revision notes

1. Ajanta: one of the oldest surviving murals of the subcontinent; carved 2nd century BC to 7th century AD out of volcanic rocks; approximately 29 caves in a horse-shoe layout.
2. Inscribed by Buddhist monks; patronised by the Vakataka kings, especially King Harisena.
3. Painting phases: Caves 9 and 10 Shunga; the rest Gupta.
4. Themes: life of the Buddha, historical and Jataka; Bodhisattvas; kings, queens, dancers; animals; floral and geometric designs.
5. Style: fluid lines, graceful figures, naturalistic expressions, symbolic use of gesture - *mudras*.
6. Technique: fresco secco on dry plaster. Colours: red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime, black from lamp soot. The general cave palette is six colours - white, red, yellow, black, green, blue.
7. Jatakas named: Vessantara, Sibi, Shaddanta, Mahajanaka. Jataka = previous births of the Buddha in human and animal form, each inculcating a virtue.
8. Shibi Jataka: King Shibi offers his own flesh to save the pigeon. Matriposhaka Jataka: the ungrateful man saved by an elephant discloses its whereabouts to the king.
9. Cave 1 Bodhisattvas in *tribhanga*: Vajrapani - power and protection; Manjusri - wisdom; Padmapani/Avalokiteshvara - compassion, shown holding a lotus.
10. The Dying Princess - Cave 16. The King Janaka is also recorded among notable paintings.
11. Caves 19 and 26: chaitya hall paintings, Buddha in preaching posture with devotees and celestial beings.
12. The reception given to a Persian embassy by Chalukyan ruler Pulakeshin II - the secular and diplomatic subject in a monastic programme.
13. Recorded as images or sculptures, not paintings: Mahaparinirvana; the assault of Mara; seated Buddha in Dharmachakrapravartana mudra.
14. Ajanta is the comparison point for Bagh's modelling, Sittanavasal's chain to Thanjavur, Brihadeswara's scale and the Bengal School's line.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using Ajanta alone, construct the strongest possible case that a monastic mural programme is evidence for more than religion - and then state what that case cannot establish.

**Model answer:** The case rests on three groups of evidence within the site. First, the subject list itself extends beyond doctrine: alongside the life of the Buddha, the Jataka tales and Bodhisattvas, the programme includes kings, queens, dancers, animals, and floral and geometric designs. Second, one panel is explicitly political and external - the reception given to a Persian embassy by the Chalukyan ruler Pulakeshin II - which records diplomacy rather than devotion. Third, the material itself is economic evidence: the pigments are mostly local earths, lime and lamp soot, but the blue is lapis lazuli, a mineral that has to be obtained, so the programme implies resources beyond the immediate site. Patronage supports the same reading, since the caves were inscribed by Buddhist monks but patronised by Vakataka kings, especially Harisena, which makes the walls a meeting point of monastic and royal interests. What the case cannot establish is scale or frequency. One diplomatic panel is not a record of sustained contact; a single expensive pigment is not a trade route; and dancers and royal figures in a Jataka narrative may be there to furnish the story rather than to document society. The evidence proves that the programme was not narrowly doctrinal; it does not license quantitative claims.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Pulakeshin II panel as though it dates or re-attributes the site. It does neither. Ajanta's recorded patronage is Vakataka; the panel shows a Chalukyan ruler as a subject of a painting, which is a fact about what was painted, not about who paid.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"At Ajanta, naturalistic handling and a coded system of gesture work together rather than in tension." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The statement identifies something real about the Ajanta idiom, and the discussion needs to show why the two are compatible.

The naturalistic side is recorded directly. The style is described as fluid lines, graceful figures and naturalistic expressions, and the figures are handled with a suppleness that makes them read as bodies rather than as signs. The Dying Princess in Cave 16 depends entirely on that capacity: the subject is a physical state, and it can be shown only if the painter can render weight, slackness and grief.

The coded side is equally recorded. Style is described as including the symbolic use of gesture - *mudras* - and the Cave 1 Bodhisattvas are identified not by likeness but by attribute and meaning: Vajrapani as protector and symbol of the Buddha's power, Manjusri as the manifestation of wisdom, Padmapani or Avalokiteshvara as the symbol of compassion, shown holding a lotus, all in the *tribhanga* pose. Caves 19 and 26 show the Buddha in a preaching posture, which is a posture that means something.

They cooperate because the code operates on the hands and the attributes while naturalism operates on the body and the face. A gesture can be read without the figure ceasing to be a convincing human form, and the *tribhanga* itself is a naturalistic device - a shift of weight - recruited as a convention.

The qualification is that this is a descriptive judgement about surviving work, not a statement about every panel.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the recorded style list used as evidence rather than as praise 3 + The Dying Princess as the case that requires naturalism 3 + the Cave 1 trio with each attribute and meaning plus *tribhanga* 5 + the explicit division of labour between hand-code and body-naturalism 3 + the descriptive qualification 1.**

## Lesson 5 - Ellora and Bagh, and the one Bagh fact already examined

Progress: 5/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Ellora painting programme; the Bagh caves, their two recorded date ranges, Rang Mahal and the Hallisalasya identification
