CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two Gupta-period mural sites, and what distinguishes them

```text
              "THE TWO EXAMPLES OF GUPTA PERIOD MURAL PAINTINGS
                     ARE AJANTA CAVES AND BAGH CAVES"
                                  |
        +-------------------------+-------------------------+
        |                                                   |
     AJANTA                                               BAGH
  Maharashtra                                    Dhar district, MADHYA PRADESH
  volcanic rock                                  bank of the BAGHNI RIVER
  approx. 29 caves, horse-shoe                   NINE Buddhist caves
  Vakataka patronage, King Harisena              majority VIHARAS, a few chaitya halls
  2nd c. BC - 7th c. AD                          DATE: the record gives TWO ranges
  fresco secco                                     (i) around 5th-6th century AD
  figures: fluid, graceful                         (ii) between 5th and 7th centuries CE
                                                 both "mainly Gupta period"
                                                 called the "AJANTA OF CENTRAL INDIA"
                                                 emphasis on INTERIOR murals over
                                                   exterior architecture
                                                 figures: MORE TIGHTLY MODELLED,
                                                   STRONGER OUTLINES,
                                                   MORE EARTHLY AND HUMAN
                                                 CAVE 4 = RANG MAHAL,
                                                   murals of the Buddhist JATAKA TALES
                                                 ceiling panels: floral and
                                                   geometric motifs
                                                 also depicts HALLISALASYA DANCE
        |                                                   |
        +--------------------- ELLORA ----------------------+
                 6th-9th centuries AD, Rashtrakutas and others
                 continues the Ajanta mural heritage, SMALLER SCALE,
                 MORE DECORATIVE; fresco secco; murals in FIVE caves,
                 mostly Cave 16 (Kailasha Temple); all THREE religions;
                 NEWER than the Ajanta paintings
```

*Three sites, one technique family, three different profiles. The single most likely objective pairing in this lesson is the Gupta-period pair at the top - Ajanta and Bagh - and the single most likely identification demand is the Bagh block on the right.*

### 1. Ellora, stated completely

✅ **Fact - date and dynasty.** The Ellora paintings date **mostly to the 6th-9th centuries AD**, during the time of the **Rashtrakutas and other dynasties**.

✅ **Fact - relationship to Ajanta.** Ellora **continued the mural painting heritage of Ajanta**, though **in smaller scale and more decorative style**, and the **Ellora cave paintings are newer than the Ajanta cave paintings**.

✅ **Fact - technique.** The technique used was **fresco secco, like Ajanta, painted on dry plaster over rock walls and ceilings**.

✅ **Fact - distribution and religions.** The mural paintings at Ellora are found in **five caves, mostly limited to Cave 16, i.e. the Kailasha Temple**, and the paintings in those five caves relate to **all three religions - Buddhism, Jainism and Hinduism**.

✅ **Fact - the prominent murals.**

| Group | Content |
|---|---|
| Ceiling and wall fragments | **Divine couples, flying celestial beings - *apsaras*, *gandharvas*** |
| Traces of murals | **Vishnu's incarnations and courtly scenes** |
| Painted ceiling panels | **Floral motifs, Buddhas and Bodhisattvas** |
| Ceiling paintings | **Dancing figures, lotus medallions, flying couples** |
| Ceiling paintings | **Lotus flowers, celestial beings, and Tirthankaras with attendants** |

⚠️ **What that list proves, and it is the point of Ellora in this session.** Four of the five groups are **ceiling** work. Ellora's painting survives chiefly overhead, which is consistent with its being described as smaller in scale and more decorative than Ajanta - ceiling programmes are ornamental fields as much as narrative ones. And the presence of Buddhas, Vishnu's incarnations and Tirthankaras **in one site's painted programme** is the clearest painted evidence in this topic of a genuinely multi-religious excavation.

❌ **The confusion to prevent.** Ellora is far better known for its architecture and sculpture than for its paintings, and a question about Ellora *painting* is testing whether you know the painting facts specifically: five caves, mostly Cave 16, ceilings, three religions, sixth to ninth centuries, Rashtrakuta-period and later, newer than Ajanta.

### 2. Bagh: the location and form facts

✅ **Fact.** The **Bagh Caves** are located **on the bank of the Baghni river in the Dhar district of Madhya Pradesh**. They are a group of **nine Buddhist caves**, **majority viharas with only a few chaitya halls**, and the site is **architecturally very similar to the Ajanta Caves**, with **emphasis more on interior murals than on exterior architecture**.

✅ **Fact.** They are famous for their murals, are **often called the "Ajanta of Central India"**, and the paintings there are **among the best surviving examples of Indian classical mural art**.

⚠️ **The locational discriminator worth memorising.** Bagh is a **river-bank** site in **Madhya Pradesh** on the **Baghni**. Ajanta is a Maharashtra site in volcanic rock. If an objective item is testing river or district, the answer is Baghni and Dhar.

### 3. Bagh: the two date ranges, reported as two

✅ **Fact - first statement.** The caves were **developed around the 5th-6th century AD, i.e. mainly during the Gupta period**.

✅ **Fact - second statement.** Elsewhere, the caves are recorded as **excavated between the 5th and 7th centuries CE (mainly Gupta period)**.

⚠️ **The disciplined handling.** The record gives **two different ranges** for the same site, both qualified as mainly Gupta. Do not silently pick one. The safe formulation is: *"the Bagh excavations are dated to the Gupta period, with the record giving both a 5th-6th century and a 5th-7th century range."* This is a small point that carries a large habit: where a source disagrees with itself, an answer that reports the disagreement is stronger than one that conceals it.

### 4. Bagh: the painted programme

✅ **Fact - the general programme.** There are **painted walls and ceilings with Jataka tales, Bodhisattvas and divine figures**; the **ceiling panels have floral and geometric motifs**. The paintings **depict Buddhist religious themes in the light of the contemporary lifestyle of people** and are **also secular in nature**.

✅ **Fact - the cave that matters.** **The most significant cave here is Rang Mahal (Cave 04)**, and **Cave 4, known as Rang Mahal, has beautiful murals on the walls depicting the Buddhist Jataka Tales.**

✅ **Fact - the formal contrast with Ajanta.** **The main difference from the Ajanta School is that the figures are more tightly modelled, have stronger outlines, and are more earthly and human.**

✅ **Fact - the record's own summary formula.** **Mural paintings in the Bagh caves are more materialistic than spiritualistic.**

⚠️ **The qualification that must accompany that formula.** It is a **relative, descriptive** contrast in the record's own words, not a doctrinal classification. The same evidence records Bagh's subjects as **Jataka tales, Bodhisattvas and divine figures** - Buddhist religious themes, which cannot be "materialistic" in any doctrinal sense. ✅ Write it as *the record's descriptive contrast of emphasis*, attribute it, and immediately qualify it with the religious subject matter. ⚠️ "Secular in nature" is likewise the record's phrase about the **subject matter**, and is not a claim about who paid.

❌ **Two Rang Mahal confusions to block.** Do **not** extend the name "Rang Mahal" to the **Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort**, which is a Rajasthani mural site taught in Lesson 8, or to Shah Jahan's Rang Mahal. Three different structures share the name; only one is Bagh Cave 4.

❌ **Do not transfer Ajanta's technique term to Bagh.** The record does not supply a distinct technique word for Bagh, and Ajanta's fresco secco label is not automatically portable.

### 5. The Hallisalasya identification, handled exactly

This is the one point in the topic where an examiner has already gone, so it is worth being pedantic.

✅ **Fact, quoted in full.** **"Bagh Cave paintings also depict Hallisalasya Dance, which is a classical Indian dance form which used to be performed in royal courts, temples, and ceremonial gatherings. In the Gupta period, it was often depicted as a devotional or courtly dance in art and sculpture."**

That sentence contains five separable facts, and they are the whole of the evidence:

| Element | What the record says |
|---|---|
| Where | The **Bagh Cave paintings** - the site generally |
| What | **Hallisalasya Dance** |
| Its category | **A classical Indian dance form** |
| Its performance settings | **Royal courts, temples, and ceremonial gatherings** |
| Its Gupta-period depiction | **Often depicted as a devotional or courtly dance in art and sculpture** |

❌ **Three prohibitions that remain in force.** Do not assign the dance to a **specific Bagh cave**; the record attributes it to the Bagh paintings generally, not to Cave 4. Do not equate it with, or identify it as, any **named modern classical dance form**. Do not supply an iconographic reading beyond the record's own words - **devotional or courtly**.

✅ **The identification rule for this family of questions.** Answer with **site + river or district + period + cave number + cave name + subject**, in that order, and stop. The demand is factual recall against close options, and every additional interpretive sentence is a new place to be wrong.

### 6. The pairing that objective papers love

✅ **Fact.** **The two examples of Gupta period mural paintings are Ajanta Caves and Bagh Caves.**

⚠️ **Why this single sentence is worth isolating.** It is a closed pair. A closed pair is the ideal raw material for an objective item, because the examiner can offer three plausible alternatives - Ellora, Badami, Sittanavasal - and test whether the candidate knows that the Gupta-period mural pairing is specifically Ajanta and Bagh. Learn it as a pair, not as two separate facts.

### Examination use

Bagh carries a verified route through the most recent available Preliminary paper and is therefore the highest-priority identification block in this topic; the exact official item, its options and its verification status are set out in the final PYQ section, and no answer is inferred anywhere. What the lesson has to deliver is the fact set that any version of that demand would need: Baghni river, Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh; nine Buddhist caves; mostly viharas; Gupta period with the record's two date ranges; "Ajanta of Central India"; Cave 4 named Rang Mahal; Jataka tales on its walls; floral and geometric ceiling panels; the tightly modelled, strongly outlined, earthly and human figures; the materialistic-versus-spiritualistic formula with its qualification; and the Hallisalasya sentence in full with its three prohibitions. Ellora supplies a separate and smaller set - five caves, mostly Cave 16, ceilings, three religions, sixth to ninth centuries, newer than Ajanta - and the Ajanta-Bagh Gupta pairing is a closed-pair fact worth learning as one unit.

### Traps introduced here

- Placing Bagh in Maharashtra or on a gorge; it is Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh, on the Baghni river.
- Choosing one Bagh date range silently when the record supplies two.
- Attaching the Hallisalasya dance to Cave 4, or to any named modern classical dance form.
- Confusing Bagh's Rang Mahal with the Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort or Shah Jahan's Rang Mahal.
- Pushing "more materialistic than spiritualistic" into a doctrinal claim, when the same evidence records Jataka tales, Bodhisattvas and divine figures.
- Assigning fresco secco to Bagh as a recorded technique term.
- Naming Ellora or Sittanavasal as the second Gupta-period mural example; the recorded pair is Ajanta and Bagh.
- Treating Ellora's paintings as extensive; they survive in five caves, mostly Cave 16, and mostly on ceilings.

### Revision notes

1. Ellora paintings: mostly 6th-9th centuries AD; Rashtrakutas and other dynasties; fresco secco; five caves, mostly Cave 16, the Kailasha Temple; all three religions; newer than Ajanta; smaller scale and more decorative.
2. Ellora subjects: divine couples, *apsaras* and *gandharvas*; Vishnu's incarnations and courtly scenes; floral motifs with Buddhas and Bodhisattvas; dancing figures, lotus medallions, flying couples; lotus flowers, celestial beings and Tirthankaras with attendants - four of five groups are ceiling work.
3. Bagh: bank of the Baghni river, Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh; nine Buddhist caves; majority viharas, a few chaitya halls; architecturally very similar to Ajanta; emphasis on interior murals over exterior architecture.
4. Bagh dating: two recorded ranges - around 5th-6th century AD, and between the 5th and 7th centuries CE - both "mainly Gupta period". Report both.
5. Bagh epithet: "Ajanta of Central India"; paintings among the best surviving examples of Indian classical mural art.
6. Bagh programme: painted walls and ceilings with Jataka tales, Bodhisattvas and divine figures; ceiling panels with floral and geometric motifs; Buddhist themes shown in the light of contemporary lifestyle, and also secular in nature.
7. Cave 4 = Rang Mahal = the most significant cave = Buddhist Jataka Tales on its walls.
8. Bagh figures: more tightly modelled, stronger outlines, more earthly and human than the Ajanta School.
9. "More materialistic than spiritualistic" is the record's relative descriptive formula; attribute it and qualify it with the Buddhist subject matter.
10. Hallisalasya: a classical Indian dance form performed in royal courts, temples and ceremonial gatherings; in the Gupta period often depicted as a devotional or courtly dance in art and sculpture; attributed to the Bagh paintings generally, not to a named cave.
11. Closed pair: the two examples of Gupta period mural painting are Ajanta and Bagh.
12. Three Rang Mahals exist in this topic's vocabulary; only Bagh Cave 4 is the one meant here.

### Concept check

**Question:** A close-option identification item names the Bagh Caves. Set out, in order, exactly what you would state and where you would stop.

**Model answer:** State six things in this order and then stop. Site: the Bagh Caves. Location: on the bank of the Baghni river, Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh. Period: Gupta, with the record giving both a fifth-to-sixth-century and a fifth-to-seventh-century range for the excavations. Form: a group of nine Buddhist caves, mostly viharas with a few chaitya halls, architecturally very similar to Ajanta and emphasising interior murals over exterior architecture. Cave number and name: the most significant cave is Cave 4, known as Rang Mahal. Subject: its walls carry murals of the Buddhist Jataka tales, while the wider programme includes Bodhisattvas and divine figures with floral and geometric ceiling panels, and the paintings also depict the Hallisalasya dance, which the record describes as a classical Indian dance form performed in royal courts, temples and ceremonial gatherings and, in the Gupta period, often depicted as a devotional or courtly dance in art and sculpture. Stop there. Do not assign the dance to a specific cave, do not equate it with a named modern classical dance form, do not supply an iconographic reading beyond "devotional or courtly", and do not attach a technique term to Bagh.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a fuller answer is a safer answer in an identification demand. It is the opposite. Each additional interpretive sentence introduces a claim the record does not support, and in a close-option item the examiner is often testing precisely the boundary at which a well-prepared candidate over-reaches.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Where a source disagrees with itself, the historian's duty is to report the disagreement, not to resolve it." Examine this proposition using the evidence for the Bagh Caves. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds where the evidence cannot adjudicate, and Bagh is a clean test case.

The record gives two dates for the same excavations - around the fifth to sixth century AD, and between the fifth and seventh centuries CE - both qualified as mainly Gupta. Nothing in the evidence privileges either, so a writer who picks one silently converts an open question into a false certainty and forfeits the one thing that could be demonstrated: that the site's chronology is bracketed rather than fixed.

A second instance is interpretive. The record calls the Bagh murals more materialistic than spiritualistic, while recording their subjects as Jataka tales, Bodhisattvas and divine figures. Reported together, the pair yields a defensible reading - a relative contrast of emphasis, not a doctrinal classification.

The limit is that reporting is not abdication: where one statement is demonstrably an error, it should be named as such rather than balanced.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the two Bagh date ranges quoted and left unresolved with the reason 4 + the materialistic-versus-religious-subject pair handled as emphasis rather than doctrine 4 + the stated limit that irreconcilable is not the same as erroneous 2.**

```text
                                |
                     "said to provide a LINK between"
                     AJANTA (4th-6th c. AD) ------> CHOLA PAINTINGS
                                                    11th c., THANJAVUR
                                |
                     ARMAMALAI CAVE (Tamil Nadu), 8th-9th c. AD
                     Pallava period, later patronised by the CHOLAS
                     = the bridge, and a Jain-to-Shaiva conversion

  ELSEWHERE, SAME CENTURY:  RAVAN CHHAYA ROCK SHELTER, Keonjhar, ODISHA
                            royal procession, 7th century AD
```

*Read the diagram as a chain of evidential strength, not just of sites. The fifth-century entry is literary; the seventh-century entry is material; and the link between Ajanta and Thanjavur is explicitly hedged in the record as something "said to" exist.*

### 1. Where Tamil wall painting starts, and what the literature proves

✅ **Fact.** The **wall painting tradition in Tamil Nadu seems to have existed since the 7th century AD**. Though **literary mention of painting is found in *Silappadikaram* and *Manimekalai* in the 5th century AD**.

⚠️ **The precise inference the literary mention licenses.** It proves that painting was **known and worth writing about** in the fifth century. It does **not** prove the scale, quality, distribution or survival of painting at that date. The correct sentence is *"textual evidence supplies what material evidence has lost"* - and then a stop.

### 2. Mahendravarman: a patron with contemporary epithets

✅ **Fact.** The Pallava ruler **Mahendravarman was the first great patron of art and architecture**. He was given appellations like ***Vichitra Chitta* (curious mind)**, ***Chettakri* (temple builder)**, and ***Chitrakarppulli* (a tiger among painters)**. He **introduced rock-cut cave temples in Tamil Nadu**, and **in the rock-cut caves of Mamandur, wall paintings can be seen**.

⚠️ **How to use the epithets, and how not to.** They are **honorific titles**, and their value is that they are contemporary evidence that a ruler wanted to be described in terms of painting. "A tiger among painters" tells you what the court thought worth boasting about. ❌ It does not tell you that the king painted, and it must not be read as biography.

⚠️ **A cross-paper note.** The same cluster of Pallava-related titles has been used as objective material in other examinations, where the tested point is simply that terms of this kind are **titles** rather than places, markets or institutions. The safe reading is the one given here: epithets of a named ruler.

### 3. Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, and the two surviving locations

✅ **Fact.** **Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha (700-720 CE)** has the honour of being the patron of **the best Pallava temple and the best Pallava wall painting**.

✅ **Fact - location one.** The **wall of the ambulatory passageway around the main shrine in the Kailashnath Temple of Kanchi** carries beautiful wall paintings, whose **theme is mainly based on the legends of Lord Shiva and Goddess Parvati**.

✅ **Fact - location two.** The **temple at Panamalai** has a beautiful painting of **the goddess Parvati watching the dance of Lord Shiva**.

✅ **Fact - a third reference.** Besides these, **paintings of royal figures and a Jain Muni have been found in the Jain caves of Annamalai Hill**.

⚠️ **The survival statement, and the rule that follows from it.** These are recorded as **the only two surviving examples of Pallava mural painting**. That is a statement about the **survival record**. ❌ It is not proof that the tradition was thin. The sentence to write explicitly in any answer touching Pallava painting: *"Only two Pallava mural locations survive. This constrains what can be claimed about the scale of the tradition, not what can be claimed about its quality."*

⚠️ **Why the Annamalai Hill reference is useful anyway.** It adds a third find-spot and, more importantly, a **Jain** context with **royal figures** in it - evidence that Pallava-period painting was not confined to one sect or to temple walls.

### 4. The Pandya record: Sittanavasal in full

✅ **Fact - patronage.** **The Pandyas were also patrons of mural art.** The paintings of the **rock-cut Jain temple at Sittanavasal** and the **Jain cave temple of Tirumalaipuram** were **patronised by the Pandyas**.

✅ **Fact - what survives where.** At **Sittanavasal**, paintings with **floral creepers and dancing celestial nymphs, birds** can be seen, **but not in their best-preserved state**. At **Tirumalaipuram**, **nothing significant remains now**.

✅ **Fact - the site itself.** The **Sittanavasal Jain caves** are located in **Tamil Nadu** and their development **can be traced from the 1st century BC to the 10th century AD**. The Jain cave shrine is mainly famous for the **sculptures of Tirthankaras** and the **mural fresco paintings on the walls, ceilings and pillars** of the rock-cut cave temple, which is known as ***Arivar Koil*, the temple of the arihants**.

✅ **Fact - technique.** **Like Ajanta and Bagh, the mural paintings here were also painted using fresco technique with many mineral colours.**

✅ **Fact - the famous mural and its meaning.** The paintings are **based on Jain themes**. A famous mural here is **a lotus pond with abundant nature and some human figures**; the **pond has a green hue where fish swim between the leaves and flowers**. The scene is **associated with the Jain tradition of *Samavasarana* - when a Tirthankara attains *keval gyan* and gives the first sermon**.

✅ **Fact - the Samavasarana explained.** In Jain tradition there are **five important events in the life of a Jain Acharya for attaining enlightenment: birth, renunciation, realisation (*keval gyan*), first sermon, and finally Nirvana or the liberation of the soul**. The first sermon is given in an audience hall called the ***Samavasarana***, where **celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish all come as an audience**; this broad spectrum of audience is called ***Bhavyas*** - those fortunate people who become entitled to attend the divine discourse in the Samavasarana.

⚠️ **Why the lotus pond is the best-taught mural in the southern group.** Without the doctrine, it is a decorative pond. With the doctrine, the **fish, the birds, the plants and the human figures are the audience** - the painting is a depiction of the *Samavasarana*'s universal congregation. This is the topic's clearest single case of iconographic knowledge converting description into meaning, and it is exactly the move a fifteen- or twenty-mark answer needs.

✅ **Fact - two further site features.** On the **eastern side of the complex** there is a natural cavern called ***Ezhadippattam*** with **17 polished rock beds with Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions**, where Jaina ascetics practised extreme penance like ***Sallekhana*** or ***Kayotsarga*** for more than a thousand years since the 1st century BC, with **their names inscribed on these beds**. The ***Navach-chunai*** is a submerged rock-cut temple, a small mountain lake on the eastern slope of the rocky hillock.

✅ **Fact - the two penances defined.** ***Sallekhana*** is an ancient extreme form of fasting in which a person keeps reducing the amount of food consumed until they die. ***Kayotsarga*** requires **staying still in one meditative posture**; many Tirthankaras are believed to have attained salvation by standing for prolonged periods, and these acts of penance are believed to free one from birth and death.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** The rock beds, the inscriptions and the penance vocabulary belong to religious and epigraphic history; they are carried here because they establish the **continuous ascetic occupation** of the site that the painting programme sits inside. Do not expand them into a doctrinal account.

### 5. The chain claim, and the record's own hedge

✅ **Fact.** The Sittanavasal paintings **are also said to provide a link between the Ajanta paintings (4th-6th century AD) and the Chola paintings of the 11th century at Thanjavur**.

⚠️ **"Said to" is the record's own hedge and it must be preserved.** ✅ The safe formulation: *"Sittanavasal is said to link the Ajanta paintings of the fourth to sixth centuries with the eleventh-century Chola paintings at Thanjavur."* ❌ The unsafe formulation: *"Sittanavasal transmitted the Ajanta style to Thanjavur."* The first reports a proposed chain; the second asserts a mechanism of transmission that is not evidenced.

⚠️ **Why the chain is still worth carrying.** It converts a list of unconnected sites into a **narrative with a direction**, which is what a Mains answer needs. State it as a proposed link, use it as your organising thread, and let the hedge do the work of protecting you.

### 6. Ravan Chhaya, and Armamalai as the bridge

✅ **Fact - Ravan Chhaya.** Located in the **Keonjhar district of Odisha**, these **ancient fresco paintings on a rock shelter** are **in the shape of a half-opened umbrella**. It is **believed that this shelter acted as the royal hunting lodge**, and the **most noticeable painting is that of a royal procession that dates back to the 7th century AD**. ⚠️ "Fresco" here is the record's own word for the paintings; no wet-plaster method is asserted, and the technique discipline of Lesson 3 applies - the recorded Indian method is painting on already dried surfaces.

⚠️ **Its use in an answer.** Ravan Chhaya is the counterweight to the assumption that seventh-century painting is a southern temple phenomenon. It is a **rock shelter**, in **eastern India**, with a **secular royal subject**, contemporaneous with the Pallava material. One sentence on it prevents a regionally lopsided answer. ⚠️ Note the record's own hedge - "it is believed" - on the hunting-lodge function.

✅ **Fact - Armamalai.** The **Armamalai Cave in Tamil Nadu** is a **rock-cut cave temple dedicated to Lord Shiva** that preserves important mural paintings from the **8th-9th century AD, the Pallava period, later patronised by the Cholas**. It is described as **a key link in the history of South Indian temple painting, leading to later Chola frescoes at Brihadisvara Temple, Thanjavur**. ⚠️ "Frescoes" is again the record's own word for those paintings and carries no wet-plaster claim.

✅ **Fact - the conversion.** It was **originally a Jain monastery (8th century AD) before being taken over by Shaiva devotees later**, as the murals depict **Jain Tirthankaras, monks, devotees and possibly scenes from Jain stories**. **One of the colourful paintings on the walls and roof depicts the tales of the *Astathik Palakas*, the deities protecting eight corners.**

✅ **Fact - why it matters.** The paintings are **crucial for understanding the transition of cave use from Jainism to Shaivism, and the continuity of mural traditions from Ajanta to Bagh to South India**.

⚠️ **The two arguments Armamalai supplies, both unusually strong.** First, a **sectarian re-use argument**: a single cave holds a Jain painted programme and a Shaiva dedication, so the building's religious identity changed while the paintings stayed. Second, a **chronological bridge**: Pallava-period work later patronised by the Cholas is a painted instance of the dynastic handover. ❌ Attribution of individual panels between the two phases is not settled, and should not be asserted.

### Examination use

The southern group supplies the topic's richest set of named evidence for any dynastic-contribution demand, and it is where the survival-bias argument has to be deployed. High-value recall: the seventh-century start with fifth-century literary mention in *Silappadikaram* and *Manimekalai*; Mahendravarman's three epithets and Mamandur; Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, 700-720 CE, with Kanchi's Kailashnath ambulatory and Panamalai's Parvati-watching-Shiva; the Annamalai Hill Jain caves; Sittanavasal's *Arivar Koil*, its lotus-pond *Samavasarana* reading, *Ezhadippattam*'s seventeen inscribed rock beds and *Navach-chunai*; Tirumalaipuram's nil survival; Ravan Chhaya's seventh-century royal procession in Keonjhar; and Armamalai's eighth-to-ninth-century Pallava work under later Chola patronage with its Jain-to-Shaiva conversion and the *Astathik Palakas*. The two argumentative moves are the survival-bias rule and the hedged Ajanta-Sittanavasal-Thanjavur chain.

### Traps introduced here

- Using the fifth-century literary mention as a date for surviving painting.
- Reading Mahendravarman's epithets as biography rather than as honorific titles.
- Presenting the paucity of Pallava murals as proof of a minor tradition.
- Dropping the record's "said to" from the Ajanta-to-Thanjavur link and asserting transmission.
- Claiming surviving significant painting at Tirumalaipuram.
- Describing the Sittanavasal lotus pond as decorative nature study when its recorded association is the *Samavasarana*.
- Assigning individual Armamalai panels confidently to the Jain or the Shaiva phase.
- Placing Ravan Chhaya in the south; it is in Keonjhar district, Odisha.

### Revision notes

1. Tamil wall painting: material tradition from the 7th century AD; literary mention in *Silappadikaram* and *Manimekalai* in the 5th century AD.
2. Mahendravarman: first great patron of art and architecture; *Vichitra Chitta*, *Chettakri*, *Chitrakarppulli*; introduced rock-cut cave temples in Tamil Nadu; wall paintings in the rock-cut caves of Mamandur.
3. Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, 700-720 CE: patron of the best Pallava temple and the best Pallava wall painting.
4. Kailashnath Temple, Kanchi: paintings on the ambulatory passageway wall around the main shrine; theme mainly Shiva and Parvati legends.
5. Panamalai: Parvati watching the dance of Shiva.
6. Annamalai Hill Jain caves: royal figures and a Jain Muni.
7. Survival rule: only two Pallava mural locations survive; this limits claims about scale, not about quality.
8. Pandya patronage: Sittanavasal and Tirumalaipuram; at Sittanavasal, floral creepers, dancing celestial nymphs and birds, not in best-preserved state; at Tirumalaipuram, nothing significant remains.
9. Sittanavasal: 1st century BC to 10th century AD; *Arivar Koil*, temple of the arihants; Tirthankara sculpture and murals on walls, ceilings and pillars; recorded as "fresco technique with many mineral colours", a phrase reported as the record's own and never read as a wet-plaster claim; Jain themes.
10. The lotus pond: green-hued pond, fish between leaves and flowers, human figures; associated with *Samavasarana*, the first sermon after *keval gyan*; audience of celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish, called *Bhavyas*; the five events are birth, renunciation, *keval gyan*, first sermon, Nirvana.
11. *Ezhadippattam*: natural cavern, 17 polished rock beds, Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions with ascetics' names, in use for more than a thousand years since the 1st century BC; penances *Sallekhana* (progressive fasting to death) and *Kayotsarga* (motionless meditative posture). *Navach-chunai*: submerged rock-cut temple, a small mountain lake.
12. The hedged chain: Sittanavasal is *said to* link Ajanta (4th-6th c. AD) with the 11th-century Chola paintings at Thanjavur.
13. Ravan Chhaya: Keonjhar, Odisha; rock shelter shaped like a half-opened umbrella; believed to be a royal hunting lodge; royal procession, 7th century AD; described in the record as "ancient fresco paintings", a word reported as the record's own with no wet-plaster claim attached.
14. Armamalai: Tamil Nadu; Shiva rock-cut cave temple; 8th-9th century AD murals, Pallava period, later Chola patronage; originally a Jain monastery; Jain Tirthankaras, monks and devotees; tales of the *Astathik Palakas* on walls and roof; evidences the Jain-to-Shaiva transition and the Ajanta-Bagh-to-South-India continuity.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the Sittanavasal lotus-pond mural changes meaning when the Jain doctrinal context is supplied, and state what the change demonstrates about reading paintings generally.

**Model answer:** Described formally, the mural is a lotus pond with abundant nature and some human figures, the pond given a green hue with fish swimming between leaves and flowers - an attractive nature scene in a rock-cut Jain shrine known as *Arivar Koil*, the temple of the arihants. Supplied with the doctrine, the same elements become a congregation. The scene is associated with the Jain tradition of *Samavasarana*, the audience hall in which a Tirthankara delivers the first sermon after attaining *keval gyan*, and the tradition specifies that celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish all come as an audience, that whole spectrum being called the *Bhavyas*. The fish and birds are therefore not scenery; they are listeners, and the human figures are part of the same universal audience. What this demonstrates generally is that in Indian painting the subject is frequently not recoverable from the depicted objects alone. The painter is using a shared textual convention, and a viewer without that convention will describe accurately and identify wrongly. This is the same structural point as the Ajanta *mudras*: the surface is naturalistic, the meaning is coded.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a painting containing plants, water and animals is a nature study. In a shrine context the presumption should run the other way - that the natural elements are performing a doctrinal function - and the question to ask is which text or tradition the composition is illustrating.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The southern mural record is small in quantity and large in significance." Critically examine, with named evidence. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The formulation is accurate and the examination must establish both halves and then their relation.

The smallness is a matter of record. Only two Pallava mural locations survive. At Tirumalaipuram nothing significant remains. At Sittanavasal the floral creepers, dancing celestial nymphs and birds are not in their best-preserved state. Chola wall painting survives in only two temples, one of which retains only faint lines.

The significance is disproportionate to that scale for four reasons. First, patronage is nameable: Mahendravarman, the first great patron, carries the contemporary epithets *Vichitra Chitta*, *Chettakri* and *Chitrakarppulli*, and Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha of 700-720 CE is recorded as patron of the best Pallava wall painting. Second, the surviving subjects are specific enough to argue from - the Shiva-Parvati legends on the Kanchi ambulatory, Parvati watching Shiva's dance at Panamalai, royal figures and a Jain Muni at Annamalai Hill. Third, one site carries doctrine: the Sittanavasal lotus pond is associated with the *Samavasarana*, whose audience of celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish is the composition itself. Fourth, the group supplies a chronology: Armamalai's eighth- to ninth-century Pallava work, later patronised by the Cholas, bridges to Thanjavur, and Sittanavasal is said to link Ajanta to the eleventh-century Chola paintings.

The critical limits are two. The chain is explicitly hedged as something said to exist. And scarcity of survivals cannot be read as scarcity of production.

Quantity here measures preservation; significance measures evidence, and the two must not be confused.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four separate quantitative survival statements 4 + nameable patronage with the three epithets and the 700-720 CE reign 4 + three named surviving subjects 3 + the *Samavasarana* reading as the doctrinal payload 4 + the Armamalai bridge and the hedged Ajanta-Thanjavur chain 3 + both critical limits, hedge and survival bias, stated as limits rather than apologies 2.**

## Lesson 7 - Four kingships, four programmes: Chola, Vijayanagara, Nayaka and Kerala

Progress: 7/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Chola wall painting and superimposition; Vijayanagara and Lepakshi; the Nayaka concept of kingship and its painting; the economic base of the Kerala school

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Chola mural passage; the Vijayanagara mural passage with its travellers' notices; the Lepakshi description; the Nayaka mural passage with its named patrons; the Kerala mural passage with its trade context and style features
CA search: "Lepakshi Veerbhadra temple murals Kerala mural revival 2026"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four kingships, and what each one's idea of kingship did to its painting

```text
 CHOLA  ------------------------------------------------------------------
   idea of kingship: divine king, imperial temple
   painting: survives in only TWO temples
     Vijaya Cholishwaram - FIRST Chola temple with wall paintings;
                           only the LINES faintly visible
     Brihadeswara - MOST OUTSTANDING centre of Chola wall painting;
                    "the biggest centre of wall painting in India AFTER AJANTA"
        subject: Shiva in Kailash; the devotee Sundarar; Shiva as
        Tripurantaka, Nataraja, Dakshinamurti, Lingodbhava;
        PLUS a portrait of RAJARAJA CHOLA I with his guru KARUVUR DEVAR
        NOTE: Rajaraja-period work WITH NAYAKA-PERIOD SUPERIMPOSITIONS

 VIJAYANAGARA ------------------------------------------------------------
   idea of kingship: NOT the pride of one linguistic group; protectors of
   the Hindu religion; prosperity -> migration -> cosmopolitan culture
   painting: noticed by ABDUR RAZZAK (ambassador) and DOMINGO PAES
             (Portuguese traveller) in their memoirs
     Tirupparutikanram Jain temple - a beautiful damsel; Indra with Airavat
     LEPAKSHI, Veerbhadra Temple, 16th c. AD - the finest example

 NAYAKA ------------------------------------------------------------------
   idea of kingship: NOT divine kings; a policy of BHOGA (self-gratification);
   Rama admired for PHYSICAL CHARM, not moral norms
   -> temples, sculptures and paintings were OPULENT
   estates at Thanjavur, Madurai, Mysore, Gingee

 KERALA ------------------------------------------------------------------
   idea of kingship: not stated; the driver is ECONOMIC
   spice trade (Greeks, Romans, Persians, Arabs; Marco Polo; Ibn Batuta)
   -> solid economic base -> temple architecture AND wall painting
   tradition evolved in the 16th century with the PALLIMANA TEMPLE, THRISSUR
```

*Four programmes, four different causes. Chola painting follows imperial temple-building, Vijayanagara painting follows a deliberate redefinition of kingship, Nayaka painting follows a philosophy of enjoyment, and Kerala painting follows the spice trade. A dynastic-contribution question is answered by naming the cause, not by listing the sites.*

### 1. Chola wall painting, and the superimposition problem

✅ **Fact - the dynastic frame.** Under the Cholas, **Dravida temple art reached its most glorious height**; they **built over a thousand temples until their rule in 1279**, and are **still more famous for their extraordinary temples and bronze sculptures**. **The art of wall painting also flourished, though surviving remains are visible in only two temples.**

✅ **Fact - temple one.** **Vijaya Cholishwaram is the first Chola temple with wall paintings**, but **only the lines of the painting are faintly visible**.

✅ **Fact - temple two.** **Brihadeswara Temple is the most outstanding centre of Chola wall painting.** **In the passage of the temple, Shiva is painted in his full glory.** The theme is **Lord Shiva in Kailash, his devotee Sundarar, and Lord Shiva in various forms - Tripurantaka, Nataraja, Dakshinamurti, Lingodbhava**. **A portrait of Rajaraja Chola I with his spiritual guru Karuvur Devar has also been depicted there.** **Brihadeswara is the biggest centre of wall painting in India after Ajanta.**

✅ **Fact - the layering.** **Paintings in Brihadeswara temple were originally done in Rajaraja's time, though there are also superimpositions from the Nayaka period.**

⚠️ **Why the royal portrait is unusually strong evidence.** A named king shown **with his named guru, inside his own imperial temple**, is not decorative: it records a relationship the patron wanted permanently visible. It is the strongest single piece of painted patronage evidence in the southern group, and it is worth naming in any answer about Chola art.

⚠️ **Why the superimposition is worth more than it looks.** A single wall carrying **Rajaraja-period work under Nayaka-period overpainting** means that dynastic painting phases are not neatly separable, and it poses a real conservation dilemma: presenting one layer means suppressing the other. That dilemma belongs to heritage policy, but the *fact of layering* is owned here and is itself examinable.

### 2. Vijayanagara: a redefinition of kingship, and what it did to art

✅ **Fact - the political frame, as recorded.** **Vijayanagara became the largest state ever founded in south India.** It **framed a new concept of kingship**: kings **did not identify themselves with the pride of one linguistic group**; they **changed the narrative**, and people of **different linguistic groups started to identify themselves with Vijayanagara rulers**. The rulers **emerged as protectors of the Hindu religion**, and **with the development of this notion they also shaped their policy, which transformed the course of art production**.

✅ **Fact - the economic frame.** One focal point was **economic prosperity**, which **promoted migration from different parts of the Indian subcontinent**; those migrations **created prosperity**, helped develop a **cosmopolitan culture**, and **profoundly influenced art**.

✅ **Fact - the external notices.** The ambassador **Abdur Razzak** and the Portuguese traveller **Domingo Paes** mention **the magnificent wall paintings of Vijayanagar** in their memoirs.

✅ **Fact - two named sites.** The **Tirupparutikanram Jain temple** includes wall paintings of **a beautiful damsel and Indra with Airavat**. Another famous example is the **Lepakshi paintings**.

⚠️ **The argument to extract.** The record explicitly makes art production **downstream of a political decision about identity**. That is a rare and usable causal claim: a state that defines itself as trans-linguistic and as protector of a religion will fund an art that is legible across language groups - which is what monumental narrative mural painting is. ⚠️ Mark it as the record's own causal statement and do not extend it into claims about specific commissions.

### 3. Lepakshi, in the detail it deserves

✅ **Fact - identity and date.** The famous **Lepakshi mural paintings of Andhra Pradesh** are part of the famous **Veerbhadra temple** belonging to the **Vijayanagara empire**. The temple is **dedicated to Lord Veerabhadra, a fierce emanation of Lord Shiva**. The murals **belong to the 16th century AD** and are **one of the finest and most significant examples of Vijayanagar mural art**.

✅ **Fact - the architectural setting, named because the paintings sit in it.** The temple premises have a **Rang Mandapa with 70 richly carved pillars**; the famous **"hanging pillar"**, slightly detached from the floor, showcases engineering ingenuity; the **Kalyana Mandapa**, a hall for wedding events, is **incomplete** but intricately sculpted showing **the divine wedding of Shiva and Parvati**; there are sculptures of episodes from the **Ramayana, Mahabharata and Puranas**, the most notable being a **giant naga hood sheltering a linga**; and the complex has the **largest monolithic Nandi in the country**.

✅ **Fact - the paintings themselves.** The **temple ceiling has a famous mural painting of Veerabhadra Swamy** of a size **more than 300 square feet**, **considered the largest single-figure fresco in India**. ⚠️ "Fresco" is the record's own word in that phrase; quote the claim as a **size** claim about a single-figure mural and do not read it as a statement that a wet-plaster method was used. Other prominent Lepakshi murals include **Ravana shaking Mount Kailash, Shiva's marriage with Parvati, Shiva as Dakshinamoorthy, Arjuna's penance, Vishnu's Dashavatara, and a mural depicting Arjuna winning Draupadi's hand**.

✅ **Fact - the forward link.** **The Lepakshi murals are seen as the progenitor or precursor of the famous Kalamkari paintings.**

⚠️ **Handle the Kalamkari link as the record frames it** - as a **seen-as** relationship, a proposed line of descent from a sixteenth-century Andhra mural programme to a textile painting tradition of the same region. It is genuinely useful because it connects the mural half of this topic to the folk half in Lesson 18, and because Kalamkari itself is recorded as dating to the Vijayanagara era. ❌ Do not upgrade "seen as the progenitor" into a demonstrated derivation.

⚠️ **Two boundary notes.** The hanging pillar, the monolithic Nandi and the Kalyana Mandapa are **architecture and sculpture**; they are named here because a painting answer that cannot place the ceiling mural in its building sounds unanchored. The **300-square-foot** figure is a size claim carried from the record and should be written with the record's own "more than".

### 4. Nayaka painting, and the philosophy that explains it

✅ **Fact - who they were.** The **Nayakas were the governors appointed by Vijayanagara rulers to oversee the provinces**; after the fall of the Vijayanagara empire, **the feudatories became full-fledged rulers**. The **Nayaka estates existed in Thanjavur, Madurai, Mysore, Gingee**.

✅ **Fact - the idea of kingship, and its consequence.** Their concept of kingship **differed from that of the Cholas and Vijayanagara**. They **did not picture themselves as divine kings**; instead they **believed in a policy of *bhoga* - self-gratification**. **To them, Rama was ideal for his physical charm, not his moral norms.** This philosophy **was clearly reflected in their art**: their **temples, sculptures and paintings were opulent**.

⚠️ **This is the sharpest cause-and-effect statement in the mural half of the topic.** A stated ideology of enjoyment, a stated aesthetic preference for physical charm over moral exemplarity, and a stated artistic consequence of opulence. Use it whenever a question asks how patronage shapes art; it is more specific and better evidenced than the usual generalities.

✅ **Fact - the named Nayaka sites and patrons.**

| Place | Site | Patron and date | Theme |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kanchipuram** | **Tirupparutikanram Jaina temple** - where Nayaka paintings are **best preserved** | - | Derived from the **Jain *Adipurana***, based on the lives of **Vardhaman Mahavira and Lord Krishna** |
| **Madurai** | **Meenakshi Sundareshwar Temple** wall paintings | **Tirumala Nayaka (1623-1659 AD)** | **Shaivite tradition**, **highly ornamental** |
| **Tiruchirappalli** | **Sri Ranganatha Temple** | - | Wall paintings recorded |
| **Thanjavur** | **Brihadeswara Temple Parikrama** wall paintings **added during the Nayaka period** | **Raghunath Nayaka (1600-1633 AD)**, a great patron of art and literature | - |
| **Mysore** | **Prasanna Venkataramana Temple** | **Raja Wodeyar (1578-1617 AD)**, credited with the **beginning of wall painting**; a **Nayaka feudatory of the Vijayanagar kingdom** | Lives of **Lord Rama and Lord Krishna** |
| **Shravanabelagola** | **Jain shrine** | - | **Best wall paintings of Jainism from the Nayaka period** |

⚠️ **Two observations worth making.** First, the **Tirupparutikanram** temple appears under both Vijayanagara and Nayaka - a single Jain temple carrying successive programmes, which is the same layering lesson as Brihadeswara in a different form. Second, the Nayaka programme is **conspicuously plural**: Shaiva at Madurai, Vaishnava at Tiruchirappalli and Mysore, Jain at Kanchipuram and Shravanabelagola. An ideology of enjoyment was not sectarian.

### 5. Kerala: an economic explanation of a painting school

✅ **Fact - the economic base.** It was **the strong economic resource base that made Kerala a strong centre of flourishing art**. In ancient times **Greeks, Romans, Persians and Arabs traded spices taken from Kerala**; **Marco Polo mentions trade between China and Kerala**; and **Ibn Batuta also wrote about the prosperity of Kerala from the spice trade**. The **rich spice trade created a solid economic base and, in turn, it led to the development of temple architecture and wall paintings**.

✅ **Fact - the start and the sites.** The **Kerala mural tradition evolved in the 16th century with the construction of the Pallimana Temple in Thrissur**. A vast number of sites in Kerala have mural paintings, **most in temples and palaces**. Important sites: **Trivanchikulam Shiva Temple; Rama Temple in Triparayar; Padmanabhaswamy Temple in Thiruvananthapuram; Pundareekapuram Krishna Temple**. **Guruvayur Temple, also known as Dwarka of the South**, represents a **very sophisticated wall painting based on the childhood of Lord Krishna**. Some of the **richest paintings** are found in **Padmanabhapuram Palace, Krishnapuram Palace and Matancheri Palace**.

✅ **Fact - the style.** In Kerala, wall paintings have **a good deal of movement, rhythm and colour**. The standard features include **prominent, voluminous male figures**, and **modelled muscular and highly ornamented bodies**. The **Kerala School is distinct and unique in itself**; it **represents a great deal of emotion on the wall**, and **there is never a dull moment in their art**.

⚠️ **The methodological point Kerala makes better than any other school here.** The explanation offered is **entirely economic** and is evidenced by **three independent external witnesses across centuries** - classical Mediterranean and West Asian traders, Marco Polo, Ibn Batuta. This is the topic's clearest case of a painting tradition being explained by trade rather than by dynasty, religion or style, and it is a genuinely different kind of argument to have available.

⚠️ **Note also the palace list.** Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram and Matancheri are **palaces**, not temples. Kerala is the one southern school in which secular royal residences are named among the richest painting sites, which qualifies any claim that South Indian mural painting is exclusively temple-bound.

### Examination use

This lesson is where a dynastic-contribution question is won, because each of the four programmes comes with a stated cause. Chola: imperial temple art, two surviving mural temples, Brihadeswara as the biggest centre after Ajanta, the Rajaraja-Karuvur Devar portrait, and Nayaka superimposition. Vijayanagara: a redefined trans-linguistic kingship, prosperity and migration, two external witnesses in Abdur Razzak and Domingo Paes, Tirupparutikanram, and Lepakshi's sixteenth-century programme with the 300-plus-square-foot Veerabhadra ceiling. Nayaka: the *bhoga* ideology and its opulent consequence, with six named sites and four dated patrons. Kerala: the spice-trade base with three external witnesses, the sixteenth-century Pallimana start, and a style described in terms of volume, musculature and ornament. Any of the four can carry a ten-mark answer alone; together they carry the twenty-mark "contribution of a dynasty" demand.

### Traps introduced here

- Saying Chola wall painting survives widely; it survives in two temples, one only as faint lines.
- Presenting the Brihadeswara paintings as purely Rajaraja-period work and omitting the Nayaka superimpositions.
- Attributing the Nayaka *Parikrama* paintings at Brihadeswara to the Chola period.
- Calling Lepakshi a Nayaka programme; it is Vijayanagara, sixteenth century, at the Veerbhadra temple.
- Upgrading "the Lepakshi murals are seen as the progenitor of Kalamkari" into a demonstrated descent.
- Treating the Vijayanagara kingship argument as your own inference; it is the record's own causal statement.
- Describing Kerala murals as temple-only when Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram and Matancheri palaces are named among the richest sites.
- Mixing up Raja Wodeyar (1578-1617), Raghunath Nayaka (1600-1633) and Tirumala Nayaka (1623-1659).

### Revision notes

1. Chola: over a thousand temples until 1279; Dravida temple art at its height; wall painting survives in only two temples.
2. Vijaya Cholishwaram: first Chola temple with wall paintings; only faint lines visible.
3. Brihadeswara: most outstanding centre of Chola wall painting; biggest centre of wall painting in India after Ajanta; Shiva in Kailash, the devotee Sundarar, Shiva as Tripurantaka, Nataraja, Dakshinamurti and Lingodbhava; portrait of Rajaraja Chola I with his guru Karuvur Devar; Rajaraja-period work with Nayaka-period superimpositions.
4. Vijayanagara kingship: largest state ever founded in south India; not the pride of one linguistic group; protectors of the Hindu religion; prosperity, migration and cosmopolitan culture transformed art production.
5. External witnesses to Vijayanagara wall painting: Abdur Razzak, ambassador; Domingo Paes, Portuguese traveller.
6. Tirupparutikanram Jain temple: a beautiful damsel; Indra with Airavat. It also holds the best-preserved Nayaka paintings, themed on the Jain *Adipurana*.
7. Lepakshi: Andhra Pradesh; Veerbhadra temple; Vijayanagara; 16th century; Rang Mandapa with 70 carved pillars; hanging pillar; incomplete Kalyana Mandapa; giant naga hood over a linga; largest monolithic Nandi in the country; ceiling mural of Veerabhadra Swamy, more than 300 sq ft, considered the largest single-figure fresco in India - a size claim in the record's own wording, carrying no wet-plaster technique claim; Ravana shaking Kailash, Shiva's marriage, Dakshinamoorthy, Arjuna's penance, Dashavatara, Arjuna winning Draupadi; seen as the progenitor of Kalamkari.
8. Nayakas: Vijayanagara provincial governors who became rulers; estates at Thanjavur, Madurai, Mysore, Gingee; not divine kings; policy of *bhoga*; Rama admired for physical charm not moral norms; opulent temples, sculptures and paintings.
9. Nayaka sites: Tirupparutikanram at Kanchipuram, best preserved, *Adipurana* themes on Vardhaman Mahavira and Krishna; Meenakshi Sundareshwar at Madurai under Tirumala Nayaka, 1623-1659, Shaivite and highly ornamental; Sri Ranganatha at Tiruchirappalli; Brihadeswara *Parikrama* added under Raghunath Nayaka of Thanjavur, 1600-1633; Prasanna Venkataramana at Mysore under Raja Wodeyar, 1578-1617, credited with beginning wall painting there; Shravanabelagola for the best Nayaka-period Jain wall paintings.
10. Kerala: spice trade with Greeks, Romans, Persians and Arabs; Marco Polo on China-Kerala trade; Ibn Batuta on Kerala's prosperity; economic base led to temple architecture and wall painting.
11. Kerala tradition evolved in the 16th century with the Pallimana Temple, Thrissur; sites include Trivanchikulam Shiva, Rama Temple Triparayar, Padmanabhaswamy, Pundareekapuram Krishna, and Guruvayur - "Dwarka of the South" - with Krishna's childhood; richest paintings at Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram and Matancheri palaces.
12. Kerala style: movement, rhythm and colour; prominent voluminous male figures; modelled muscular and highly ornamented bodies; a great deal of emotion; distinct and unique.

### Concept check

**Question:** Take the Nayaka case and show precisely how a stated theory of kingship becomes a visible property of paintings. Then name one thing this explanation cannot account for.

**Model answer:** The chain has four links, and each is recorded. First, a political fact: the Nayakas were Vijayanagara provincial governors who became full-fledged rulers after the empire fell, with estates at Thanjavur, Madurai, Mysore and Gingee. Second, an ideological fact: their concept of kingship differed from that of the Cholas and Vijayanagara in that they did not picture themselves as divine kings, and believed instead in a policy of *bhoga*, self-gratification. Third, an aesthetic preference that follows from it: to them Rama was ideal for his physical charm and not for his moral norms, which relocates value from exemplarity to the body. Fourth, the artistic consequence: this philosophy was clearly reflected in their art, and their temples, sculptures and paintings were opulent - visible in the highly ornamental Shaivite paintings of the Meenakshi Sundareshwar temple under Tirumala Nayaka between 1623 and 1659. What the explanation cannot account for is the sectarian range. An ideology of enjoyment does not predict why the best-preserved Nayaka paintings are in a Jain temple at Kanchipuram, drawn from the *Adipurana*, or why the best Nayaka-period Jain wall paintings are at Shravanabelagola, alongside Shaiva work at Madurai and Vaishnava work at Tiruchirappalli and Mysore. Patronage ideology explains the manner; it does not explain the distribution across sects.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting "opulent" into "decadent" or treating the *bhoga* philosophy as a judgement on Nayaka art. The record states a stated policy and a stated consequence. Turning a described preference into a verdict about artistic decline substitutes a slogan for the evidence, and it is contradicted by the same record's note that these are the best-preserved paintings of their kind.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"South Indian mural painting after the Cholas is best explained by economics rather than by religion." Comment, using the Vijayanagara, Nayaka and Kerala evidence. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is half right, and the comment should separate the cases it fits from the case it does not.

Kerala fits it almost exactly. The record explains the school by its resource base: Greeks, Romans, Persians and Arabs traded Kerala spices, Marco Polo notices China-Kerala trade and Ibn Batuta writes of the prosperity that came from it, and the rich spice trade created a solid economic base which in turn led to the development of temple architecture and wall paintings. The tradition then evolves from the sixteenth century with the Pallimana Temple at Thrissur, and its richest works are in palaces - Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram, Matancheri - as much as in temples.

Vijayanagara fits it partly. Prosperity promoted migration, which created a cosmopolitan culture and profoundly influenced art. But the record's primary explanation is political and religious: a new concept of kingship that refused identification with one linguistic group, and rulers who emerged as protectors of the Hindu religion, shaping policy that transformed art production.

The Nayaka case resists the claim outright. Their painting is explained by an ideology - not divine kings, a policy of *bhoga*, Rama admired for physical charm rather than moral norms - producing opulent temples, sculptures and paintings.

Economics is therefore the best single explanation for Kerala, a contributing one for Vijayanagara, and largely irrelevant for the Nayakas, whose programme follows a stated theory of rule.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the Kerala economic chain with all three external witnesses 5 + Vijayanagara split correctly between its economic and its political-religious causes 4 + the Nayaka counter-case stated through its own ideology 4 + a graded verdict that assigns a different weight to each case rather than one overall answer 2.**

### Visual: two northern wall traditions, and the different engines behind them

```text

     -> the JAIN COMMUNITY also patronised wall art
```
