```text
    Ayodhya, Bundelkhand, Rajasthan    paintings; elaborates 312 SADHANAS
    to Jammu from MP, Bihar, Bengal  Hevajratantra - the FIVE DHYANI BUDDHAS
  under Akbar many became              as celestial, not historic, beings
    MANSABDARS -> contact with       MANDALA - a mystic circle, sphere of
    Mughal art and culture             divinity; Dhyani Buddha at centre;
                                       sometimes 16 Bodhisattvas + 8 goddesses
  Himachal: Raja Sansarchand;        SIDDHAS painted to motivate followers
    Gauri Shankar Temple,            Alchi (Sumstek: Maitreya, Vairochana,
    Sujanpur Tira; also                Manjushri), Hemis (Dukhang), Tabo
    Dharamshala, Kangra, Kulu,         (Spiti; Mahayana and Vajrayana)
    Chamba                           parallel development: Nepal, Sikkim,
  Jammu: temple walls, Rama;           Bhutan, Arunachal; Sinon, Namchi
    Dogra dynasty; Gulab Singh         and Lachung monasteries in Sikkim
```

*Two traditions that look similar on a map and are driven by different things. Rajasthani wall painting is explained by alliance, commerce and devotion; Ladakhi wall painting is explained by texts that specify what must be painted and why.*

### 1. Why Rajput courts began painting walls, in the record's own causal order

✅ **Fact - the political setting.** Rajasthan was previously called **Rajputana, the land of Rajputs and the sons of Rajas**; Rajput associations with Rajasthan **emerged between the middle of the fifth and eighth centuries**. The **nature of the climate and the difficult terrain** resulted in people becoming **hard-working, resilient and sturdy**, which further resulted in Rajputs becoming **warlike people** who **conceptualised the ethos of living and dying by the sword**. In the **Delhi Sultanate period the position of Rajputs was not seriously challenged**; later, when **Mughal rule was established, Akbar was able to bind Rajputs through matrimonial alliance and mansabdari**.

✅ **Fact - the artistic consequence.** **In the course of their association with the Mughals, Rajputs learned to appreciate Mughal art and were also exposed to Persian and Arabic traditions of art. It proved to be a catalyst for the development of the Rajasthani tradition of painting.**

✅ **Fact - the economic driver.** **The rise of the Marwari class and increased economic prosperity led to the patronage of wall paintings.** The **patrons of wall art were Maharajas, their nobles, and the business community.**

✅ **Fact - the religious driver.** **The most powerful inspiration for wall art came from religion.** **With the popularisation of Lord Krishna as the deity of love, his presentation on walls increased. The Jain community also patronised wall art.**

⚠️ **Why the three-driver structure is worth more than the site list.** It answers the real question - why did a warrior aristocracy suddenly commission painted walls? - with a political catalyst, an economic base and a devotional subject, each separately recorded. ⚠️ Note also that the **patron list includes the business community**. That is unusual and worth a sentence: wall painting in Rajasthan is not exclusively a court art.

### 2. Harauti: Bundi and Kota

✅ **Fact - the region.** **Kota and Bundi**, two of the regions of **Harauti**, are known for **lush green landscapes of lakes and forests**. Harauti is **the land of the Hada Rajputs**, and is **strategically located on the route to the Deccan from Delhi**; **hence it was an important area for the Mughals, which further fostered the painting tradition**.

✅ **Fact - Bundi.** Bundi has **some of Rajasthan's oldest and most beautiful wall paintings**. **Bundi Fort** is the abode of the **most extensive wall paintings**, providing a hint of its evolution; the **earliest wall painting here is in Badal Mahal**. The paintings have an **early Rajasthani style with Mughal and Deccani influences**, with remarkable illustrations of **Rukmini's abduction by Lord Krishna, Sita's rescue by Lord Rama after defeating Ravana, the Nala-Damayanti legend from the Mahabharata, flute-playing Krishna, and Krishna Ras-Leela**. The **Adinatha Jain Temple in Bundi contains the earliest and most beautiful Jain wall paintings in Rajasthan**.

✅ **Fact - Kota.** The **Kingdom of Kota was carved out of Bundi State by Jahangir as a reward for Madho Singh's military service to the Mughals in 1614**. **Soon Kota was to outshine Bundi in its size, prosperity and grandeur.** The most important wall paintings are **inside Kota Fort**: the **front wall of Rajmahal** depicts **scenes from Krishna's childhood and the forests of Vrindavan**, and **Bada Mahal** on the top of Kota Fort has **the royal hunt, a durbar scene, Krishna playing Holi, and Kaliya Mardan**. **The paintings of Kota are noted for their realism**, and **the predominance of red colour can also be seen.**

⚠️ **The stereotype this breaks, and it is the most important one in the topic.** Bundi wall painting carries **Mughal and Deccani influence**; Kota wall painting is noted for **realism**. Neither is the "flat, bold, devotional Rajput" of the revision slogan. A route that carried Mughal armies to the Deccan also carried artists, models and compositions, and the paintings show it. ⚠️ Note that **1614** is a dated political fact - Jahangir's grant to Madho Singh - which is why Kota exists as a separate painting centre at all.

### 3. Marwar, Bikaner and Jaisalmer

✅ **Fact - Marwar.** The **Rathores** were the rulers of Marwar, **descended from the Rashtrakutas**; their large empire **extended from Jaisalmer in the north-west to Ajmer in the south**, and it is **also known as the house of Jodhpur or Marudesh**. The **rulers of Marudesh were exceptional soldiers and Marwaris were astute businessmen**, and **this combination brought immense prosperity**, which in turn **fostered the wall painting tradition**. The **history of wall painting in Marwar started in the 18th century**. A large number of Marwar murals are **inside the Jodhpur Fort**, and the **earliest wall paintings are in Phool Mahal**. Themes are based on **Raga-Ragini, Ragamalas, Krishna-Radha love, and Nayaka-Nayika Bheda**. The **most remarkable wall paintings are in Teeja Mata Mandir Gas Mandi**, on a **Rama-Sita** theme.

✅ **Fact - Bikaner.** **Bikaner state was established by Rao Bika**, son of **Rao Jodha**, a descendant of the **Rathore clan**. The kingdom **flourished under the Mughals**, with whom it had a **close military association**. The **earliest wall paintings were created inside Chandra Mahal and later in Anup Mahal, Shivabari Temple**. Themes: **Ramayana, Mahabharata, royal scenes, the life of Lord Krishna**.

✅ **Fact - Jaisalmer.** Jaisalmer is **the true heartland of the Rajasthan Desert**, inhabited by **Rajputs of the Bhatti clan**, who **also entered into a matrimonial alliance with the Mughals**. The **Rang Mahal of the Jaisalmer Fort embodies the wall paintings**; keeping with the Rajasthani tradition, themes are drawn from **Krishna's life**, and also from **Shiva-Parvati, Lord Ganesh, and Riddhi-Siddhi**.

❌ **Reminder.** The **Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort** is not **Bagh Cave 4, Rang Mahal**. Keep them apart.

### 4. Mewar and Amber

✅ **Fact - Mewar's political distinctiveness.** **Mewar claimed descent from Kusha, the second son of Lord Rama**, and the state **existed at least from 734 AD**. Mewar **always firmly adhered to Hinduism** and was **Rajasthan's foremost state**; its rulers **carried on a relentless war against the Mughals at any cost**, and **though they became Mughal mansabdars by 1614, they never submitted to the Mughals and refused matrimonial alliances**.

✅ **Fact - Mewar's wall painting.** The tradition **has existed since the early 18th century**. During the tenure of **Maharana Bhim Singh**, the **Pichola Lake Palace, Udaipur**, was created, **recording the grand lifestyle of the Maharaja**. **Maharaja Bhim Singh poisoned his own daughter, Krishna Kumari, when two bridegrooms arrived warring for her and threatened destruction if she was married to one of them**; a **room in the palace is dedicated to Krishna Kumari, but it does not mention her sacrifice**, and **the paintings in Krishna Kumari's room are among the best in Rajasthan**. Themes are mostly **Nayak and Nayika bhava and Radha-Krishna**. **Udaipur's beautiful villas** also contain wall paintings - **Govardhan Vilas, Mamaji Ki Haveli, and Shiv Rati Ki Haveli** - themed on **Radha-Krishna and Nayak-Nayika Bheda**.

⚠️ **The interpretive point that room offers, stated carefully.** A room commemorates a person, and the paintings in it are among the finest in Rajasthan, while the **event that caused her death is not represented**. That is a precise example of what royal painting does: it **commemorates without narrating**. ⚠️ Write it as an observation about the record's own statement, not as a psychological claim about the patron.

✅ **Fact - Amber.** The **rulers of Amer were the first to enter into a matrimonial alliance with the Mughals**: **Raja Bharmal married his daughter to Akbar in 1562**, which **had a powerful impact on the fortunes of the state**. **Raja Man Singh was the major patron of wall painting in Amber**; another major patron was **Maharaja Jai Singh II, who founded the city of Jaipur**. **During the reign of Madho Singh (1777-1802), wall paintings were commissioned in the city palace of Jaipur.** Themes range from **Krishna Leela in Vrindavan, flute-playing Krishna, Lord Ganesh, to Vishnu's Dashavatara**.

⚠️ **The comparison worth drawing between Mewar and Amber, in one line.** Amber allies first, in **1562**, and paints; Mewar resists longest, becomes mansabdar only **by 1614** and refuses matrimonial alliance, and paints from the **early eighteenth century**. Political posture correlates with the **date** at which court wall painting begins - the earlier the accommodation, the earlier the programme. ⚠️ Mark this as an inference drawn from dated facts, not as a recorded causal claim.

### 5. Pahari wall painting in Himachal and Jammu

✅ **Fact - the population movement.** **Pahari murals are mainly found in Himachal Pradesh and Jammu and Kashmir.** **Rajputs migrated to Himachal Pradesh from Prayagraj, Ayodhya, Bundelkhand and Rajasthan**; similarly, **Rajputs migrated to Jammu from Madhya Pradesh, Bihar and Bengal**. **During the reign of Akbar, many of them became mansabdars, which brought them into contact with Mughal art and culture.**

✅ **Fact - Himachal.** **Raja Sansarchand was the most important patron of wall painting.** The paintings inside the **Gauri Shankar Temple in Sujanpur Tira** are **based on paintings of hills with the Raja in the background**; **Krishna Leela and the Ramayana** also formed important themes. Wall paintings of Himachal are also found in **Dharamshala, Kangra, Kulu and Chamba**.

✅ **Fact - Jammu.** **Most of the wall paintings are executed on temple walls and dedicated to Lord Rama**; some are devoted to **Krishna and Lord Shiva**. The **Dogra dynasty was the major patron**, and **Gulab Singh Dogra had a more extraordinary taste for paintings**.

⚠️ **Note the overlap you will meet again in Lesson 15.** *Sansar Chand* is both the patron of Himachal **wall** painting here and the patron under whom the **Kangra miniature school** reached its zenith. One patron, two formats. That is a genuinely useful fact, because it shows the same court commissioning both a fixed and a portable art.

### 6. Ladakh and the monastic belt: painting specified by text

✅ **Fact - the setting.** The **northern part of India was more exposed to visitors and invaders from the north-west and was subjected to many influences**, some of which **have not percolated down south**. There is **greater variety in style**, and **at the same time it is uniquely Indian**. The **cultural connection of Ladakh with India is evident from Kailash Mountain and Mansarovar Lake**; the **racial composition of Ladakh is mixed, with Mongoloid and Indo-Sanskrit groups**; and it is **believed that the spur of Buddhism further spurred the development of art in Ladakh**.

✅ **Fact - the two texts.** **Sadhanmala** and the **Hevajratantra** are **prominent texts in iconography**. **Sadhanmala also explains most of the wall paintings and further elaborates on 312 *sadhanas*, or spiritual exercises.** **Hevajratantra conceptualises the five Dhyani Buddhas as celestial beings, not historic beings**; the five are **to be visualised during meditation**, and **form an important part of Ladakh wall paintings**. The **female counterparts of the Dhyani Buddhas are also represented**.

✅ **Fact - the mandala.** **Another instrument of *sadhana* is the mandala.** A **mandala is a mystic circle and a sphere of divinity**; it **carries a Dhyani Buddha in the centre and other Dhyani Buddhas surrounding him in a circle**, and **sometimes 16 Bodhisattvas and 8 goddesses are also arranged around the Dhyani Buddha**. **Mandala forms an important theme in wall paintings.**

✅ **Fact - the siddhas.** **To attain *sadhana*, the assistance of a guru, a Siddha, is necessary.** **Siddhas have attained the *siddhis*, and they are often painted on the wall to motivate followers on the path.**

✅ **Fact - the function of the monastic hall.** The **Buddhist monasteries of Ladakh consisted of several halls, which provided ideal space for wall paintings to promote the message of the Buddha and as an object of concentration and meditation for the Buddhist monks.**

✅ **Fact - the named monasteries.**

| Monastery | What is recorded |
|---|---|
| **Alchi** | A **tiny village in Ladakh** consisting of a **monastery, stupa and chortens**; the most important building is the **three-storey Sumstek Temple**, with **some of the most magnificent wall paintings**; the **three main images belong to Maitreya, Vairochana and Manjushri** |
| **Hemis** | Like Alchi, it **represents some of the finest Buddhist wall paintings**; the paintings in the ***Dukhang*, or assembly hall**, are **executed with great attention**, drawing themes from **Jataka, Buddhist Tantricism, and Dhyani Buddha with mantras** |
| **Tabo** | **Nestled in the Spiti valley of Himachal Pradesh**; its wall paintings are **inspired by Mahayana and Vajrayana philosophies** |
| **Others named** | **Lamayuru, Mang Gyu, Saspol, Phiyang, Tsemo, Basgo, Shey, Thiktse and Spituk** monasteries also include many wall paintings |

✅ **Fact - the wider belt.** Apart from Ladakh and Himachal Pradesh, there seems to be a **parallel development of monastic culture in the Himalayan kingdoms of Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan and Arunachal Pradesh**. The **Sinon, Namchi and Lachung monasteries in Sikkim** represent wall paintings based on Buddhism.

⚠️ **The argument this section uniquely supplies.** Everywhere else in this topic, a painting is explained by a patron. Here it is explained by a **text and a practice**: the *Sadhanmala* explains most of the wall paintings, the *Hevajratantra* specifies the five Dhyani Buddhas as visualisation objects, the mandala is an instrument of *sadhana*, and the hall itself is described as an **object of concentration and meditation**. The painting is not illustration of doctrine; it is **equipment for practice**. That single sentence is the highest-value line in the lesson.

### Examination use

Two different kinds of demand are served here. For a question on Rajput or Rajasthani art, the three-driver explanation - Mughal contact through matrimonial alliance and mansabdari, Marwari commercial prosperity, and Krishna devotion with Jain patronage - plus six named regions with their forts, palaces and themes, will carry a full answer, and the Bundi-and-Kota evidence is the specific material that refuses the school stereotype. For a question on Buddhist or Himalayan art, the Ladakh section supplies a rare text-driven explanation with two named iconographic texts, the 312 *sadhanas*, the five Dhyani Buddhas, the mandala's structure and the named monasteries. The dated political facts - 1562, 1614, 1777-1802, 734 AD - are the anchors that keep a Rajasthani answer from becoming a list of palaces.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing Rajasthani wall painting as purely courtly; the recorded patrons include the business community, and Jain communities patronised it too.
- Treating Bundi as an unmixed Rajput idiom; its paintings carry Mughal and Deccani influence.
- Calling Kota flat and stylised; its paintings are noted for realism and a predominance of red.
- Using 1614 for Kota's foundation without the reason - Jahangir's reward to Madho Singh for military service.
- Confusing the Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort with Bagh Cave 4.
- Making Mewar's early-eighteenth-century wall painting contemporaneous with Amber's, when the recorded alliance dates differ by half a century.
- Treating the Ladakh mandala as decoration rather than as an instrument of *sadhana*.
- Describing the Dhyani Buddhas as historical figures; the *Hevajratantra* conceptualises them as celestial beings.

### Revision notes

1. Three drivers of Rajasthani wall painting: Mughal contact through Akbar's matrimonial alliances and mansabdari, plus exposure to Persian and Arabic traditions, as catalyst; the rise of the Marwari class and prosperity, with patrons being Maharajas, nobles and the business community; and religion as the most powerful inspiration, with Krishna as the deity of love and Jain community patronage.
2. Harauti/Hadoti = Kota and Bundi, land of the Hada Rajputs, on the Delhi-to-Deccan route, which fostered the painting tradition.
3. Bundi: oldest and most beautiful wall paintings in Rajasthan; Bundi Fort most extensive; earliest in Badal Mahal; early Rajasthani style with Mughal and Deccani influences; Rukmini's abduction, Sita's rescue, Nala-Damayanti, flute-playing Krishna, Krishna Ras-Leela; Adinatha Jain Temple has the earliest and most beautiful Jain wall paintings in Rajasthan.
4. Kota: carved out of Bundi by Jahangir in 1614 as a reward to Madho Singh; outshone Bundi; Kota Fort - Rajmahal front wall with Krishna's childhood and Vrindavan, Bada Mahal with royal hunt, durbar, Krishna playing Holi, Kaliya Mardan; noted for realism and predominance of red.
5. Marwar: Rathores descended from the Rashtrakutas; Jaisalmer to Ajmer; house of Jodhpur or Marudesh; soldiers plus Marwari businessmen produced prosperity; wall painting from the 18th century; Jodhpur Fort, earliest in Phool Mahal; Raga-Ragini, Ragamala, Krishna-Radha, Nayaka-Nayika Bheda; Teeja Mata Mandir Gas Mandi on Rama-Sita.
6. Bikaner: Rao Bika, son of Rao Jodha, Rathore; close military association with the Mughals; Chandra Mahal earliest, then Anup Mahal and Shivabari Temple; Ramayana, Mahabharata, royal scenes, Krishna.
7. Jaisalmer: Bhatti clan; matrimonial alliance with the Mughals; Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort; Krishna, Shiva-Parvati, Ganesh, Riddhi-Siddhi.
8. Mewar: descent claimed from Kusha; state from at least 734 AD; relentless war with the Mughals; mansabdars by 1614 but never submitted and refused matrimonial alliances; wall painting from the early 18th century; Pichola Lake Palace under Maharana Bhim Singh; Krishna Kumari's room, among the best in Rajasthan, which does not mention her sacrifice; Nayak-Nayika bhava and Radha-Krishna; Govardhan Vilas, Mamaji Ki Haveli, Shiv Rati Ki Haveli.
9. Amber: first to ally; Raja Bharmal's daughter married Akbar in 1562; Raja Man Singh the major patron; Jai Singh II founded Jaipur; Madho Singh, 1777-1802, commissioned paintings in the Jaipur city palace; Krishna Leela, flute-playing Krishna, Ganesh, Dashavatara.
10. Pahari murals: Himachal and Jammu and Kashmir; Rajput migration from Prayagraj, Ayodhya, Bundelkhand and Rajasthan into Himachal, and from MP, Bihar and Bengal into Jammu; mansabdari under Akbar brought contact with Mughal art.
11. Himachal: Raja Sansarchand the most important patron; Gauri Shankar Temple, Sujanpur Tira - hills with the Raja in the background; Krishna Leela and Ramayana; also Dharamshala, Kangra, Kulu, Chamba. Jammu: temple walls, mainly Rama, also Krishna and Shiva; Dogra dynasty; Gulab Singh Dogra.
12. Ladakh: *Sadhanmala* explains most of the wall paintings and elaborates 312 *sadhanas*; *Hevajratantra* makes the five Dhyani Buddhas celestial, not historic, beings, to be visualised in meditation, with female counterparts also represented.
13. Mandala: mystic circle and sphere of divinity; Dhyani Buddha at the centre, others around; sometimes 16 Bodhisattvas and 8 goddesses. Siddhas painted to motivate followers. Monastic halls served as space for wall painting and as objects of concentration and meditation.
14. Alchi - Sumstek, three storeys, images of Maitreya, Vairochana, Manjushri. Hemis - *Dukhang*, Jataka, Buddhist Tantricism, Dhyani Buddha with mantras. Tabo - Spiti valley, Mahayana and Vajrayana. Also Lamayuru, Mang Gyu, Saspol, Phiyang, Tsemo, Basgo, Shey, Thiktse, Spituk. Parallel development in Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan, Arunachal; Sinon, Namchi and Lachung in Sikkim.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Ladakh mural programme is better described as equipment for practice than as illustration of doctrine, and identify what would have to be true for the opposite description to be correct.

**Model answer:** Four recorded features make the practice reading the better one. First, the governing texts are prescriptive rather than narrative: the *Sadhanmala* explains most of the wall paintings and elaborates 312 *sadhanas* or spiritual exercises, so the paintings sit inside a manual of practice. Second, the principal subjects are defined by their function in meditation: the *Hevajratantra* conceptualises the five Dhyani Buddhas as celestial rather than historic beings, and they are to be visualised during meditation. Third, the mandala is explicitly described as an instrument of *sadhana* - a mystic circle and sphere of divinity carrying a Dhyani Buddha at the centre with others arranged around, sometimes with sixteen Bodhisattvas and eight goddesses. Fourth, the architecture is described in the same terms: monastic halls provided ideal space for wall paintings to promote the message of the Buddha and as an object of concentration and meditation for monks. For the illustration reading to be correct instead, the subjects would need to be narrative episodes with a story order, the figures would need to be historical rather than visualised, and the paintings would need to be positioned for viewing rather than for the sustained attention of a practitioner in a hall.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that religious wall painting everywhere does the same job. Ajanta narrates Jataka stories to a viewing public; Ladakh's programme supplies visualisation objects to practitioners. Both are Buddhist wall painting; their function, and therefore the right way to describe them, differ.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Rajput wall painting is better explained by political accommodation than by Rajput tradition." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition captures the recorded catalyst but overstates it, and the examination should weigh three drivers rather than two.

The accommodation case is strong and dated. The Rajput position was not seriously challenged under the Delhi Sultanate; Akbar then bound the Rajputs through matrimonial alliance and mansabdari, and in the course of that association Rajputs learned to appreciate Mughal art and were exposed to Persian and Arabic traditions, which proved a catalyst for the Rajasthani painting tradition. The dates follow the politics. Amber allies first, with Bharmal's daughter married to Akbar in 1562, and its patrons run from Man Singh to Jai Singh II to Madho Singh of 1777-1802. Kota exists as a centre only because Jahangir carved it out of Bundi in 1614 for Madho Singh's military service. Harauti's position on the Delhi-to-Deccan route is recorded as fostering the tradition, and Bundi's paintings carry Mughal and Deccani influence.

Two other drivers are equally recorded. Economically, the rise of the Marwari class and rising prosperity produced patronage in which Maharajas, nobles and the business community all commission. Religiously, the most powerful inspiration came from religion, with Krishna popularised as the deity of love and Jain communities also patronising wall art.

Mewar is the test: it refused matrimonial alliance and fought relentlessly, yet painted from the early eighteenth century on Radha-Krishna and Nayak-Nayika themes.

Accommodation set the timetable; commerce funded it; devotion supplied the subject.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the accommodation mechanism with at least two dated political facts 5 + the Marwari commercial driver and its unusual patron list 3 + the devotional driver with Krishna and Jain patronage 3 + Mewar used as a genuine test case rather than an illustration 3 + a three-part verdict that assigns each driver a distinct role 1.**

## Lesson 9 - What a miniature actually is, and where it began

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the word, the rule and the two beginnings

```text
   "MINIATURE" <- Latin MINIUM = red lead paint
                  used in illuminated manuscripts of the Renaissance
                  verb MINIARE = "to colour with minium"
   NOTE: the word is about a PIGMENT, not about smallness
         (though the Indian form is in fact small - see the rule)
                              |
   THE TWO TECHNICAL PRECONDITIONS, as recorded

        of the actual size
                              |
   THE FIGURAL CONVENTIONS

     bulging eyes | pointed nose | slim waist
     RAJASTHANI miniatures: BROWN skin
     MUGHAL miniatures: FAIRER tones
     divine beings such as Krishna: BLUE
     women: long black hair and eyes | men: traditional attire with turbans
                              |
   DEVELOPED BETWEEN THE 8TH AND 12TH CENTURIES
              |                              |
        EASTERN REGION                  WESTERN REGION
        PALA SCHOOL                     APABHRAMSA SCHOOL
        750-1150 AD                     11th-15th centuries
        palm leaf or vellum paper       Gujarat and the Mewar region
        illustrated manuscripts         predominant school of W. India
        sinuous lines                   JAIN themes first,
        subdued backgrounds               later VAISHNAVA too
        simple compositions             Gita Govinda + SECULAR LOVE
        single figures                  palm leaf -> paper
        patrons: rulers supporting      red, yellow, ochre (symbolic),
          BUDDHISM, esp. VAJRAYANA        later bright colours and GOLD
        painters: DHIMMAN and VITAPALA  fish-shaped bulging eyes,
                                          pointed nose, double chin,
                                          angular faces, stiff postures,
                                          rich detail
                                        KALPASUTRA and KALAKACHARYA KATHA,
                                          15th century
```

*One word, one size rule, one shared set of conventions - and then two regional beginnings that differ in religion, support, palette and face.*

### 1. The word and the rule

✅ **Fact.** The word **"miniature" is derived from *minium*, the old Latin word for red lead paint**, which was **often used in the illuminated manuscripts of the Renaissance period**; the corresponding verb meaning **"to colour with *minium*" was *miniare***. **In India, miniature painting has a long tradition, with several schools developing their own distinct styles of composition and perspective.**

✅ **Fact - the preconditions.** There are **several preconditions necessary for making a miniature painting**: the painting **should not be larger than 25 square inches**, and the **subject of the painting would be painted in not more than one-sixth of the actual size**.

⚠️ **Why the size rule is worth more than its face value.** It is a **definition by constraint**, and constraints have consequences. A painting that cannot exceed twenty-five square inches cannot be a public object; it can only be held, passed and looked at closely. Everything that follows about private viewing, portability, album assembly and artist mobility is implicit in that single number, which is why it appeared in the format argument of Lesson 1 and reappears in every patronage comparison in this session.

⚠️ **The etymological trap.** The word refers to a **pigment**, not to smallness. A candidate who explains "miniature" as "a small painting" has given a description rather than a derivation. Say both: the word derives from red lead paint used in Renaissance illuminated manuscripts, and the Indian form is separately defined by a size ceiling.

### 2. The conventions of the figure

✅ **Fact.** In most Indian miniature paintings, **human figures are usually shown in profile with bulging eyes, pointed nose, and slim waist**. **Rajasthani miniatures depict characters with brown skin, while Mughal miniatures show fairer tones.** The **colour of divine beings like Lord Krishna is painted blue**. **Women are shown with long black hair and eyes, while men wear traditional attire with turbans.**

⚠️ **How to handle the skin-tone statement.** It is recorded as a **convention of depiction** distinguishing two school families. Report it as such - a difference in the painted convention - and do not convert it into a claim about the people depicted or about the painters.

⚠️ **What the conventions tell you about reading a miniature.** Profile view plus stylised eye, nose and waist means the figure is **not a likeness** in the portrait sense. Identification therefore runs through **attribute, colour and costume** rather than through facial resemblance - Krishna is blue, a man is turbaned. This matters in Lesson 12, where Mughal painting develops something genuinely different: individual portraiture.

### 3. The Pala School

✅ **Fact - dates and support.** The **art of miniature painting developed between the 8th and 12th centuries**, attributable to the **eastern and western regions**, with **two prominent schools: the Pala School of Art and the Apabhramsa School of Art**. The **Pala School flourished during 750-1150 AD**. Its paintings are **generally found as a part of illustrated manuscripts** and were **generally executed on palm leaf or vellum paper**.

✅ **Fact - style and patronage.** The paintings are characterised by **sinuous lines, subdued backgrounds, simple compositions, and single figures**, and were **patronised by rulers supporting Buddhism, especially the Vajrayana school**. The **prominent painters were Dhimman and Vitapala**.

⚠️ **Four formal features that hang together.** Sinuous line, subdued background, simple composition, single figure - these are the properties of an image made to sit on a **narrow palm-leaf folio** beside text. The support is dictating the style. This is the first and clearest instance in the topic of format determining form, and it is worth stating explicitly rather than listing the four adjectives.

⚠️ **Two named painters at this date is unusual.** Dhimman and Vitapala are among the earliest painter names available in this topic, and naming them is a cheap way to demonstrate specificity in an answer about early miniature painting.

### 4. The Apabhramsa School

✅ **Fact - origin and span.** This school **traces its origin to Gujarat and the Mewar region in Rajasthan**, and was **the predominant school of painting in Western India during the 11th to 15th centuries**.

✅ **Fact - subject.** **Jain themes were the most common; later the Vaishnava School also used these themes.** The **artists brought in the concept of *Gita Govinda* and secular love into these paintings**, which were **otherwise dominated by Jain iconography**.

✅ **Fact - support and colour.** In the **early Jain phase the paintings were made on palm leaf**, but in the **later period they were executed on paper**. The **colours used had a symbolic meaning**, and artists **usually used red, yellow and ochre**; in the **later phase artists experimented with bright colours and the colour gold**.

✅ **Fact - the face.** **Human figures had fish-shaped bulging eyes, pointed nose, double chin, angular faces, with stiff postures and rich detail.**

✅ **Fact - the examples.** **The most famous example is of the *Kalpasutra* and the *Kalakacharya Katha* from the 15th century.**

⚠️ **The one word to be careful with.** "Apabhramsa" is a **language** name being used as a painting-school label. ❌ It is not the only or universally preferred label for western Indian Jain manuscript painting; the safer description in an answer is **Western Indian or Jain school**, with language, region, support and patronage spelled out. Use "Apabhramsa" as the recorded label, and then explain what it denotes.

### 5. Pala against Apabhramsa: the comparison that matters

| Axis | Pala | Apabhramsa |
|---|---|---|
| Region | Eastern India | Gujarat and the Mewar region of Rajasthan |
| Dates | 750-1150 AD | 11th-15th centuries; predominant in Western India |
| Religion | **Buddhist**, patronised by rulers supporting **Vajrayana** | **Jain** first, **Vaishnava** later |
| Support | Palm leaf or vellum paper | Palm leaf early, **paper** later |
| Line and composition | **Sinuous** lines, **subdued** backgrounds, **simple** compositions, **single** figures | **Angular** faces, **stiff** postures, **rich detail** |
| The eye | Not separately specified beyond the general convention | **Fish-shaped bulging eyes**, pointed nose, **double chin** |
| Colour | Not separately specified | Red, yellow, ochre with symbolic meaning; later bright colours and **gold** |
| Named names | Painters **Dhimman** and **Vitapala** | Manuscripts ***Kalpasutra*** and ***Kalakacharya Katha***, 15th century |

⚠️ **The single most useful contrast.** Pala is **restrained** - sinuous, subdued, simple, single; Apabhramsa is **emphatic** - angular, stiff, richly detailed, and eventually gold. ⚠️ Do not moralise the contrast into "refined" versus "crude". They are different conventions serving different religious traditions on different supports.

⚠️ **The shift that decides the future of the format.** Apabhramsa moves from **palm leaf to paper**. Paper permits a larger, less brittle, more flexible page than a leaf, which is the technical precondition for the album page of Lesson 12. The miniature's whole later history runs through that material change.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the definitional core of the entire miniature half of the topic, and its facts are unusually crisp. The high-value recall items are the *minium* etymology, the twenty-five-square-inch ceiling and the one-sixth rule, the profile convention with brown against fairer skin tones and blue divinity, the Pala dates of 750-1150 with Dhimman and Vitapala and the Vajrayana patronage, and the Apabhramsa span of the eleventh to fifteenth centuries with the *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharya Katha* of the fifteenth century. For written answers, the lesson supplies two arguments: that support determines form, evidenced by the Pala palm-leaf style; and that the palm-leaf-to-paper shift is the material precondition for everything the Mughal atelier later does.

### Traps introduced here

- Defining "miniature" as "small painting"; the word derives from *minium*, red lead paint.
- Quoting the size rule as twenty-five inches rather than twenty-five **square** inches, or forgetting the separate one-sixth rule.
- Treating the Pala School as a general early-Indian school; it is eastern, Buddhist and Vajrayana-patronised, 750-1150.
- Describing the Apabhramsa school as purely Jain; the record notes later Vaishnava use of the same themes, plus *Gita Govinda* and secular love.
- Using "Apabhramsa" as though it were the only acceptable label for western Indian Jain manuscript painting.
- Giving the fish-shaped eye and double chin to Pala painting; those are Apabhramsa features.
- Assigning the *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharya Katha* to a century other than the fifteenth.

### Revision notes

1. "Miniature" from Latin *minium*, red lead paint used in Renaissance illuminated manuscripts; verb *miniare*, to colour with *minium*.
2. Preconditions: not larger than 25 square inches; subject painted at not more than one-sixth of actual size.
3. Conventions: profile view; bulging eyes, pointed nose, slim waist; Rajasthani brown skin, Mughal fairer tones; divine beings such as Krishna blue; women with long black hair and eyes; men in traditional attire with turbans.
4. Miniature painting developed between the 8th and 12th centuries, in the eastern and western regions; two prominent early schools - Pala and Apabhramsa.
5. Pala: 750-1150 AD; part of illustrated manuscripts; palm leaf or vellum paper; sinuous lines, subdued backgrounds, simple compositions, single figures; patronised by rulers supporting Buddhism, especially Vajrayana; painters Dhimman and Vitapala.
6. Apabhramsa: origin in Gujarat and the Mewar region of Rajasthan; predominant in Western India, 11th-15th centuries; Jain themes commonest, later also Vaishnava; *Gita Govinda* and secular love introduced into an otherwise Jain-dominated iconography.
7. Apabhramsa support and colour: palm leaf in the early Jain phase, paper later; red, yellow and ochre with symbolic meaning; later bright colours and gold.
8. Apabhramsa figures: fish-shaped bulging eyes, pointed nose, double chin, angular faces, stiff postures, rich detail.
9. Apabhramsa examples: *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharya Katha*, 15th century.
10. Support determines form: the Pala style suits a narrow palm-leaf folio; the palm-leaf-to-paper shift is the material precondition for the later album page.
11. Prefer "Western Indian or Jain school" as the descriptive label, using "Apabhramsa" as the recorded term and explaining it.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the twenty-five-square-inch rule, taken seriously, predicts four later features of Indian miniature painting.

**Model answer:** The rule states that a miniature should not be larger than twenty-five square inches, with the subject painted at not more than one-sixth of actual size. Four consequences follow. First, viewing becomes private: an object of that size cannot address a congregation, so it must be held, and the audience shrinks to one or a few - which is why album and manuscript painting is connoisseurial where mural painting is collective. Second, patronage broadens downwards: because the object requires only a patron, an artist and portable material rather than a building and an endowment, many small courts can sustain workshops, which is why the Rajasthani and Pahari schools proliferate into sub-schools where mural traditions could not. Third, the work becomes mobile with its maker: when a court's patronage contracts, the skill walks, which is the mechanism by which artists trained in one style appear at other centres. Fourth, the handling must be fine and the identification must run through convention rather than scale: figures are in profile with stylised eye, nose and waist, and identity is carried by colour and attribute - Krishna blue, men turbaned - because at that size resemblance cannot do the work.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the size ceiling as a stylistic preference for delicacy. It is a technical definition, and its consequences are economic and social before they are aesthetic. The delicacy is downstream of the constraint, not the reason for it.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The early history of Indian miniature painting is a history of supports rather than of styles." Comment with reference to the Pala and Apabhramsa schools. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is a useful exaggeration and should be accepted in part.

The support case is genuine. Pala painting, flourishing between 750 and 1150, was executed on palm leaf or vellum as part of illustrated manuscripts, and its recorded features - sinuous lines, subdued backgrounds, simple compositions and single figures - are precisely what a narrow leaf beside text can carry. Apabhramsa painting begins on palm leaf in its early Jain phase and moves to paper later, and it is with paper that bright colours, gold and rich detail appear.

But style is not merely derived. The two schools differ in ways the support does not explain: Pala is Buddhist and Vajrayana-patronised, Apabhramsa Jain and later Vaishnava; Pala line is sinuous where Apabhramsa faces are angular with fish-shaped bulging eyes and double chins.

Support sets the limits; religion and region decide what is done inside them.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the support argument evidenced by matching Pala features to the palm-leaf folio 4 + the palm-leaf-to-paper shift tied to gold and bright colour 2 + the counter-case from religion, patronage and facial convention 3 + a limits-versus-choices verdict 1.**

*This lesson is short because the evidence is short, but it is structurally the most important junction in the topic: everything before it is regional and religious, everything after it is courtly and competitive.*

### 1. The transition period

✅ **Fact.** The **14th-century arrival of Muslims brought a cultural renaissance**, while **in the Vijayanagara South, a style akin to the Deccan school emerged**.

✅ **Fact - the four features.** **The colours were applied in a flat manner**; **the dress and human outlines were demarcated in black**; **the view of the face forms a three-quarter angle**; and the face **portrays a detached appearance**.

⚠️ **The one feature that matters most, and why.** The **three-quarter angle** is a departure from the strict profile that the general miniature convention prescribes. A face turned three-quarters towards the viewer is a step towards volume and towards individual presence - and it is therefore an early technical precondition for the portraiture that becomes a Mughal speciality. ⚠️ Mark this as an inference from the recorded feature, not as a recorded causal claim.

⚠️ **The southern half of that sentence should not be skipped.** The record places, at the same moment, an emergent **Deccan-akin style in the Vijayanagara South**. That is the earliest anchor available for the Deccani stream of Lesson 13, and it prevents the mistake of treating Deccani painting as a late offshoot of Mughal work.

### 2. Sultanate painting: the programme and the two named products

✅ **Fact - the aim.** These paintings **tried to create a synthesis of the Persian elements borrowed from the artists' origins and indigenous Indian traditional elements**.

✅ **Fact - the format preference.** There was **a preference for illustrating manuscripts**.

✅ **Fact - the finest example.** One of the finest examples from this period is the ***Nimatnama*, a book on cookery**, produced during the reign of **Nasir Shah, who ruled over Mandu (Malwa Sultanate)**. **This manuscript shows the synthesis of the indigenous and Persian styles.**

✅ **Fact - the second style.** **Another style called *Lodi Khuladar* was also prevalent during this period and was followed in many of the Sultanate-dominated regions between Delhi and Jaunpur.**

⚠️ **Why a cookery book is the best possible example, and worth saying so.** A manuscript on cookery is **secular**, **practical** and **courtly** all at once. Its illustration proves that by this date the illustrated book was no longer confined to religious transmission - Buddhist manuscripts in the east, Jain manuscripts in the west - but had become an instrument of **court life**. That is the change the transition period actually consists of, and the *Nimatnama* demonstrates it more economically than any doctrinal manuscript could.

⚠️ **Where Lodi Khuladar sits.** It is a **named style with a named geography** - the Sultanate-dominated regions **between Delhi and Jaunpur**. It is worth carrying precisely because it shows that Sultanate painting was not a single court phenomenon but had a regional spread.

### 3. What emerges, and the sentence that governs the next four lessons

✅ **Fact.** **Later, three major styles emerged that dominated the medieval landscape: Mughal, Rajput, Deccan.** **The artists borrowed elements from the Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles.**

⚠️ **Read that last clause as an instruction.** "Borrowed... but also developed their own individual styles" forbids two opposite errors. It forbids treating Mughal, Rajput and Deccani painting as three sealed traditions with separate ancestries; and it forbids treating the regional schools as derivative provincial Mughal work. The recorded position is **shared inheritance plus independent development**, and that is the formulation to carry into any comparison question.

### 4. The synthesis claim, examined rather than repeated

The word "synthesis" is doing a great deal of work in this lesson, so it is worth pinning down what is being synthesised.

| Element | Where it comes from, as recorded |
|---|---|
| Persian elements | **The artists' origins** - the painters themselves are the carriers |
| Indigenous elements | **Indian traditional elements** already present |
| The format | **Manuscript illustration** - a form already established in both the Pala and the Apabhramsa traditions |
| The occasion | **Court use**, evidenced by a cookery book made for a named Sultan of Mandu |

⚠️ **The analytical point.** The synthesis is carried by **people**, not by abstractions. The record says the Persian elements come from *the artists' origins* - that is, the style arrives because trained painters arrive. This is the first appearance of what becomes the organising mechanism of the whole later half of this topic: **styles move because artists move**. Hold it; it explains the Akbari workshop, the post-Aurangzeb dispersal, the Kangra school's foundation and the Rajasthani schools' formation.

### Examination use

The transition and Sultanate material is short but supplies three examinable specifics and one argument. The specifics are the four transition features - flat colour, black outlines, three-quarter face, detached appearance; the *Nimatnama* as a cookery manuscript under Nasir Shah of Mandu in the Malwa Sultanate; and Lodi Khuladar as a style followed between Delhi and Jaunpur. The argument is the mechanism of transfer: Persian elements arrive with the artists' origins, which establishes that style travels with people, and the record's own formulation - borrowing from Sultanate art while developing individual styles - is the correct frame for every later comparison between Mughal, Rajput and Deccani work.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling the *Nimatnama* a religious or literary manuscript; it is a book on cookery.
- Placing the *Nimatnama* at Delhi; it belongs to Nasir Shah's Mandu, in the Malwa Sultanate.
- Treating Lodi Khuladar as a Delhi-only style; it was followed between Delhi and Jaunpur.
- Describing transition-period faces as profiles; the recorded view is a three-quarter angle.
- Forgetting the southern half of the transition statement - a style akin to the Deccan school emerging in the Vijayanagara South.
- Presenting Mughal, Rajput and Deccani painting as unrelated traditions, when the record has them borrowing from Sultanate art and then developing individually.

### Revision notes

1. Fourteenth century: arrival of Muslims brings a cultural renaissance; in the Vijayanagara South a style akin to the Deccan school emerges.
2. Transition-period features: flat application of colour; dress and human outlines demarcated in black; face at a three-quarter angle; a detached appearance.
3. Sultanate miniature aim: a synthesis of Persian elements from the artists' origins with indigenous Indian traditional elements.
4. Format preference: illustrating manuscripts.
5. *Nimatnama*: a book on cookery; reign of Nasir Shah, ruler of Mandu, Malwa Sultanate; shows the synthesis of indigenous and Persian styles.
6. Lodi Khuladar: another style of the period, followed in many Sultanate-dominated regions between Delhi and Jaunpur.
7. Three major styles emerged later and dominated the medieval landscape: Mughal, Rajput, Deccan; the artists borrowed from Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles.
8. The transfer mechanism: Persian elements arrive through the artists' origins - styles travel with people.
9. The three-quarter face is the technical step away from strict profile and towards individual presence.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is an illustrated cookery book a better piece of evidence for the change happening in fourteenth- and fifteenth-century Indian painting than an illustrated religious manuscript would be?

**Model answer:** Because the change being evidenced is a change of occasion, not of technique alone. Before this period, the two prominent miniature schools are religious in their subject and in their patronage: Pala manuscripts are Buddhist, patronised by rulers supporting Vajrayana, and Apabhramsa manuscripts are dominated by Jain iconography. An illustrated religious manuscript from the Sultanate period would therefore demonstrate continuity, not change. The *Nimatnama*, a book on cookery produced under Nasir Shah of Mandu in the Malwa Sultanate, demonstrates that the illustrated book had been annexed to court life: its subject is practical and secular, its patron is a named ruler, and the record identifies it as one of the finest examples of the period precisely because it shows the synthesis of indigenous and Persian styles. It thus evidences three things simultaneously - a new patron type, a new subject range, and the stylistic fusion that defines the period - where a devotional manuscript would evidence only the third.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that "secular" here means irreligious or anti-religious. It means only that the occasion of the book is court life rather than doctrine. Religious manuscript painting continues throughout; what is new is that it is no longer the only use of the illustrated page.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In Indian painting, styles travel because artists travel." Discuss with reference to the Sultanate and its aftermath. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is the mechanism the record itself supplies, and it should be affirmed with a limit.

Sultanate painting is described as a synthesis of Persian elements borrowed from *the artists' origins* with indigenous Indian traditional elements. The carrier is therefore the painter, not an abstraction called influence: the *Nimatnama*, made for Nasir Shah at Mandu, is the product of that fusion, and Lodi Khuladar spreads across Sultanate-dominated regions between Delhi and Jaunpur rather than being confined to one court.

The same mechanism organises what follows. Three major styles - Mughal, Rajput and Deccan - emerge from artists who borrowed from Sultanate art and then developed their own individual styles, which is a description of trained people relocating and adapting rather than of traditions diverging by themselves.

The limit is that mobility explains transmission, not selection: why a receiving court adopted some elements and refused others requires its own patronage explanation.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the artists'-origins formulation identified as the recorded mechanism 3 + two named instances, the *Nimatnama* and Lodi Khuladar's geography 3 + the three-stream outcome stated in the record's own borrowing-plus-development terms 2 + the limit distinguishing transmission from selection 2.**

## Lesson 11 - The Mughal workshop to Akbar: how an institution made a style

Progress: 11/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Babur and Humayun, the two Persian masters, foreshortening, the Tasvir Khana, Akbar's painters and manuscripts, and the atelier as a collective

### Visual: four reigns, four different relationships to painting

```text
 BABUR      | established the dynasty after a series of wars
            | DID NOT HAVE MUCH TIME to commission paintings
            | is SAID TO HAVE PATRONISED the Persian artist BIHZAD,
            |   who made some illustrations of the MUGHAL FAMILY TREE
            v
 HUMAYUN    | a GREAT PATRON OF ARTS; came to the throne young
            | interested in paintings and in building monuments
            | ATELIER DISRUPTED when he lost the throne to Sher Shah Suri
            |   and was EXILED TO PERSIA
            | at SHAH TAHMASP'S court he acquired two main painters:
            |      ABDUS SAMAD  and  MIR SAYYID ALI
            | they RETURNED WITH HIM, brought PERSIAN INFLUENCE,
            |   created many beautifully illustrated albums
            | under AKBAR they created the TUTINAMA (Tales of a Parrot)
            v
 AKBAR      | established AN ENTIRE DEPARTMENT for illustration and scribing
            | formal artistic studio: TASVIR KHANA
            |   artists HIRED ON A SALARY, able to develop individual styles
            | looked upon paintings as a MEANS OF STUDY AND AMUSEMENT
            | recognised talented INDIAN artists who had worked for previous
            |   rulers and invited them in -> "INDIAN INFLUENCE" appears
            | features: 3-DIMENSIONAL figures; continued FORESHORTENING;
            |   artists encouraged the use of CALLIGRAPHY
            | THE defining change: POPULAR ART -> COURT ART
            |   (scenes of court life rather than the life of the masses)
            v
 TECHNIQUE ADDED IN THIS PERIOD: FORESHORTENING -
   "objects were drawn in a way that they look closer and smaller
    than they really are"
```

*Four reigns, and the decisive one is the third. Akbar's contribution is not a manner but an institution: a salaried department in which individual styles could develop.*

### 1. Babur and Humayun: interruption, exile, and two painters

✅ **Fact - Babur.** **Babur established the Mughal dynasty after fighting a series of wars.** **He did not have much time to commission paintings, but he is said to have patronised the Persian artist Bihzad, who made some illustrations of the Mughal family tree.**

⚠️ **Preserve the hedge.** "Is said to have patronised" is the record's own qualification. Report it as such.

✅ **Fact - Humayun.** **Humayun, who was a great patron of arts, came to the throne at a young age.** He was **interested in paintings and building beautiful monuments, but his atelier was disrupted when he lost the throne to Sher Shah Suri and was exiled to Persia.**

✅ **Fact - the acquisition.** **While he was at Shah Tahmasp's court in Persia, he acquired the services of two main painters called Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali, who came back with him after he won his throne back and established the Mughal dynasty in India.** **These artists were responsible for bringing Persian influence into the Mughal paintings and created many beautifully illustrated albums.** **During Akbar's reign, they created an illustrated manuscript called *Tutinama* (Tales of a Parrot).**

⚠️ **The structural point, and it is a good one.** A **military defeat** produced an **artistic acquisition**. Humayun's exile is the reason two Persian masters entered Indian service, and their presence is the reason Persian influence enters Mughal painting. Defeat, exile, recruitment, return, transmission - it is the clearest single chain in the topic of politics converting into style, and it is a more interesting opening for a Mughal-painting answer than any statement about naturalism.

⚠️ **Note the continuity across reigns.** Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali are recruited by **Humayun** and produce the *Tutinama* under **Akbar**. The workshop outlived the ruler who assembled it, which is the first evidence that the Mughal achievement is institutional.

### 2. Foreshortening: the one technique term of this lesson

✅ **Fact.** **During this period, the technique of foreshortening was added to the Indian painter's repertoire.** Under this technique, **"objects were drawn in a way that they look closer and smaller than they really are"**.

⚠️ **Use the record's own definition, exactly.** It is an unusual formulation - closer *and* smaller - and it should be quoted rather than paraphrased into a standard art-historical definition. ⚠️ What matters for an answer is that foreshortening is a **depth device**: it is how a flat page begins to imply recession. Together with the three-dimensional figures recorded under Akbar, it marks Mughal painting's move away from the flat colour application of the transition period.

### 3. Akbar's institution, feature by feature

✅ **Fact - the department.** **Akbar was responsible for the establishment of an entire department devoted to the illustration and scribing of his documents.** He **established a formal artistic studio called *Tasvir Khana* where the artists were hired on a salary and were able to develop their individual styles.** **Akbar looked upon paintings as a means of study and amusement.**

✅ **Fact - recruitment.** **Akbar also recognised the skills of talented Indian artists who had worked for previous rulers and invited them to work in his *Tasvir Khana*.** **Hence, "Indian influence" started to appear in the Mughal paintings.**

✅ **Fact - the defining features.** The defining features of paintings in Akbar's period are the **use of 3-dimensional figures** and the **continued use of foreshortening**. Furthermore, **the artists encouraged the use of calligraphy in the paintings**.

✅ **Fact - the change of subject.** **One of the distinguishing features of this period was the transformation of popular art to court art, i.e. the artist was more focused on depicting the scenes of court life than the life of the masses.**

✅ **Fact - the painters.** **Famous painters of this period include Abdus Samad, Mir Sayyid Ali, Daswanth, Basawan and Kesu Das.**

✅ **Fact - the manuscripts.** **Prominent illustrated manuscripts during Akbar's reign are *Tutinama*, *Razmnama*, *Baburnama*, *Hamzanama*, *Tarikh-i-Alfi*, *Anvar-i-Suhaili* and *Gulistan of Sadi*.**

> 🔑 Recall aid for the seven Akbari manuscripts: **T-R-B-H-T-A-G** - *Tutinama, Razmnama, Baburnama, Hamzanama, Tarikh-i-Alfi, Anvar-i-Suhaili, Gulistan of Sadi*.

⚠️ **Read the salary clause carefully, because it contains an argument.** Artists were **hired on a salary** and were **able to develop their individual styles**. A salaried studio removes the need to find a new commission for every work, which is the condition under which a painter can specialise. That single administrative fact explains why Akbar's period produces named individual painters rather than an anonymous court manner.

### 4. The atelier as a collective, and where the artists came from

✅ **Fact.** The imperial atelier assembled artists from **Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa**; **manuscripts were collaborative**; and the workforce was **mixed** rather than drawn from a single community.

⚠️ **The consequence for how you describe Mughal painting.** "Court style" was **not a single hand**. A large illustrated manuscript such as the *Hamzanama* or the *Razmnama* is the product of a division of labour across a workshop recruited from several regions. Two things follow, and both are examinable. First, "Mughal style" is a **workshop product**, so attributing a whole manuscript to one artist is an error of kind, not just of fact. Second, because the style lives in **trained people distributed across regions**, the contraction of imperial patronage does not destroy it - it disperses it. That is the mechanism Lesson 12 ends on and Lessons 14 and 15 depend on.

### 5. What Akbar's period actually changed, summarised as a table

| Before | Under Akbar | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Illustration attached to religious or regional manuscript traditions | A **salaried imperial department**, the *Tasvir Khana*, devoted to illustration and scribing | Recorded establishment of the department and the studio |
| Persian manner introduced by two recruited masters | Persian manner **plus** recruited Indian artists, producing recorded "Indian influence" | Named recruitment of talented Indian artists who had worked for previous rulers |
| Flat colour, black outlines, three-quarter faces | **Three-dimensional figures**, continued **foreshortening**, encouraged **calligraphy** | Recorded defining features |
| Subject drawn from religious narrative and popular life | **Court life** - the transformation of popular art to court art | Recorded distinguishing feature |
| Individual anonymous craftsmen | **Named painters** - Daswanth, Basawan, Kesu Das, alongside the two Persian masters | Recorded list of famous painters |

⚠️ **The sentence to carry out of this lesson.** Akbar's contribution to Indian painting is **administrative before it is aesthetic**: he built the institution inside which a style could be developed, taught and reproduced.

### Examination use

Akbar's reign is examined both for recall and for argument. Recall: Bihzad and the family-tree illustrations, with the record's hedge; Humayun's exile to Persia and the recruitment of Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali at Shah Tahmasp's court; the *Tutinama* as their work under Akbar; foreshortening in the record's own words; the *Tasvir Khana* as a salaried studio; three-dimensional figures and encouraged calligraphy; the transformation of popular art to court art; the five named painters; and the seven named manuscripts. Argument: the atelier as a collective recruited from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa, producing manuscripts collaboratively, which is the basis for refusing both the single-genius account of Mughal painting and the sealed-tradition account of the regional schools.

### Traps introduced here

- Attributing the recruitment of Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali to Akbar; they were acquired by Humayun in Persia and worked on into Akbar's reign.
- Dropping the record's hedge on Babur's patronage of Bihzad.
- Paraphrasing foreshortening loosely; the recorded definition is that objects look closer and smaller than they really are.
- Treating the *Tasvir Khana* as a style rather than as a salaried department.
- Assigning a complete Akbari manuscript to a single painter; production was collaborative.
- Describing Akbar's painting as religious; the recorded shift is from popular art to court art, with court life displacing the life of the masses as the subject.
- Omitting the recruitment of Indian artists, which is the recorded source of "Indian influence".

### Revision notes

1. Babur: established the dynasty after a series of wars; little time for commissions; said to have patronised the Persian artist Bihzad, who illustrated the Mughal family tree.
2. Humayun: great patron; atelier disrupted by the loss of the throne to Sher Shah Suri and exile to Persia; at Shah Tahmasp's court acquired Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali.
3. Those two brought Persian influence, created many beautifully illustrated albums, and under Akbar created the *Tutinama*, Tales of a Parrot.
4. Foreshortening added in this period: objects drawn so that they look closer and smaller than they really are.
5. Akbar: an entire department for illustration and scribing; the formal studio *Tasvir Khana*; artists on salary, able to develop individual styles; paintings as a means of study and amusement.
6. Akbar recruited talented Indian artists who had served previous rulers, which is the recorded origin of "Indian influence" in Mughal painting.
7. Akbari features: three-dimensional figures; continued foreshortening; encouraged calligraphy.
8. The defining change: popular art transformed into court art - scenes of court life rather than the life of the masses.
9. Akbari painters: Abdus Samad, Mir Sayyid Ali, Daswanth, Basawan, Kesu Das.
10. Akbari manuscripts: *Tutinama*, *Razmnama*, *Baburnama*, *Hamzanama*, *Tarikh-i-Alfi*, *Anvar-i-Suhaili*, *Gulistan of Sadi*.
11. The atelier assembled artists from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa; manuscripts were collaborative and the workforce mixed.
12. Consequence: Mughal painting is a workshop product; its style resides in trained people, so reduced imperial patronage disperses rather than destroys it.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Akbar's contribution to Indian painting was administrative before it was aesthetic." Justify this from the record, and say what the claim does not deny.

**Model answer:** Four recorded facts justify it. First, the institution: Akbar established an entire department devoted to the illustration and scribing of his documents, and a formal artistic studio called the *Tasvir Khana*. Second, the employment terms: artists were hired on a salary and were thereby able to develop their individual styles, which is an administrative arrangement producing an artistic outcome. Third, recruitment policy: he recognised the skills of talented Indian artists who had worked for previous rulers and invited them into the *Tasvir Khana*, which is what the record identifies as the origin of "Indian influence" in Mughal painting. Fourth, composition and method: the atelier assembled painters from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa, and manuscripts were produced collaboratively, so the seven great Akbari manuscripts are institutional outputs. What the claim does not deny is that aesthetic change occurred. Three-dimensional figures, the continued use of foreshortening, the encouragement of calligraphy and the transformation of popular art into court art are all real stylistic changes. The claim is about sequence and cause: the department, the salary and the recruitment are what made those changes possible, transmissible and durable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading "workshop product" as a judgement that the paintings are impersonal or mechanical. The record says the opposite - that salaried employment allowed artists to develop individual styles - and it names five of them. Collective production and individual manner are compatible; what is ruled out is attributing a whole collaborative manuscript to one hand.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Mughal atelier, not the Mughal emperor, is the right unit of analysis for Mughal painting." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition corrects a persistent habit and should be accepted with two qualifications.

The atelier case is strong. Akbar's contribution is an institution: an entire department for illustration and scribing, a formal studio called the *Tasvir Khana*, artists hired on a salary and thereby able to develop individual styles, and a deliberate recruitment policy that brought in talented Indian artists who had served previous rulers, producing the recorded "Indian influence". The workforce was assembled from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa and was mixed; manuscripts were collaborative. The seven great Akbari manuscripts - *Tutinama*, *Razmnama*, *Baburnama*, *Hamzanama*, *Tarikh-i-Alfi*, *Anvar-i-Suhaili*, *Gulistan of Sadi* - are institutional outputs, and the studio outlived the ruler who assembled it, since Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali were recruited by Humayun in exile and worked on under Akbar. Decisively, when imperial patronage later contracted under Aurangzeb, artists moved to provincial courts, which is only possible if the style resided in people rather than in a person.

Two qualifications are necessary. Imperial taste demonstrably redirected the workshop: the recorded shift from illustrated manuscripts towards albums and individual portraiture, and the preference for flora and fauna, belong to a named reign. And the emperor supplied the resources without which no atelier exists.

The atelier is therefore the right unit for explaining how Mughal painting was made and how it survived; the emperor remains the right unit for explaining what it was made to show.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the institutional evidence with department, studio, salary and recruitment 5 + the multi-regional mixed workforce and collaborative production 4 + the seven manuscripts used as institutional outputs 3 + the cross-reign continuity of the two Persian masters 3 + dispersal under reduced patronage as decisive proof 3 + both qualifications, taste and resources, with a split verdict 2.**

### Visual: three reigns, read as three decisions about what a painting is for

```text
    unlike father and grandfather who liked NATURALISTIC depictions,
      he liked creating ARTIFICIAL elements
```
