```text
      bring in UNNATURAL STILLNESS, as he was INSPIRED BY THE EUROPEAN STYLE"
    ESCHEWED CHARCOAL; encouraged drawing and sketching with a PENCIL
    ordered artists to INCREASE THE USE OF GOLD AND SILVER
    liked BRIGHTER COLOUR PALETTES than his predecessors
 ================================ AURANGZEB ===============================
    "Aurangzeb's DISINTEREST led to a DECLINE in Mughal painting
      as artists moved to PROVINCIAL COURTS"
                               |
                               v
             the trained workforce carries the style outward:
             Bikaner, Jodhpur, Kishangarh, the Kangra region ...
```

*Three reigns, three different answers to the question of what a painting is for: a record of the observed world; a display of imperial magnificence; and, finally, not a priority at all.*

### 1. Jahangir and the format shift

✅ **Fact.** **The Mughal paintings reached their zenith in the period of Jahangir.** **Jahangir was a naturalist by nature and preferred paintings of flora and fauna, i.e. birds, animals, trees and flowers.** **There was a shift from illustrated manuscripts to albums and emphasis was given to bringing naturalism to portrait (individual) painting.** **One of the unique trends that developed in this period was the use of decorated margins around the paintings that were sometimes as elaborate as the paintings themselves.**

⚠️ **Why this is the single most important sentence in the Mughal half of the topic.** It records a change of **object**, not of manner. An illustrated manuscript is a book: its images are subordinate to a text and are seen in a reading sequence. An album is a **collection of independent pages**: each image is complete in itself and is chosen, arranged and compared by a collector. The shift from one to the other is what makes individual portraiture worth doing at all, because a portrait needs no story around it.

⚠️ **The decorated margin belongs to the same change.** A margin as elaborate as the painting only makes sense on a page that is itself the object of attention. In a manuscript the margin is the edge of a text block; in an album it is part of the composition.

✅ **Fact - the emperor as artist and collector.** **Jahangir was himself considered to be a good artist and he had his own private workshop.**

### 2. Natural history, and the two named masters

✅ **Fact - the famous subjects.** **The most famous paintings were the naturalistic paintings of the zebra, the turkey and the cock.**

⚠️ **What the zebra and the turkey are evidence of, stated carefully.** They are **unfamiliar animals**, and the record presents them among the most famous naturalistic paintings of the reign. Their presence shows that the court's interest was in **the observed particular** - this animal, recorded - rather than in the conventional animal of narrative painting. ❌ Do not extend this into claims about how the animals reached the court, from where, or in what year; the record supplies none of that.

✅ **Fact - Ustad Mansur.** **One of the most famous artists from his period was Ustad Mansur, who was given the title *Nadir ul Asr* by Jahangir.** **He was an expert in drawing the features for the most complex faces.**

✅ **Fact - a manuscript still illustrated in this reign.** **An animal fable called *Ayar-i-Danish* (Touchstone of Knowledge) was illustrated during his reign.**

⚠️ **The *Ayar-i-Danish* is a useful corrective.** Manuscript illustration did not stop when albums rose; the record's word is a **shift of emphasis**. An answer that says manuscripts were abandoned overstates the change and can be corrected by this single title.

✅ **Fact - Abu'l Hasan.** **Abu'l Hasan (1569-1627) was a chief portrait painter in the court of Jahangir.** He was **honoured with the title *Nadir-al-Zaman*, "wonder of the age", by Jahangir for his excellence in painting.** **He experimented with allegorical paintings - a symbolic fictional narrative - which had a subtle agenda: they reinforced the supremacy, legitimacy and divine right to rule of his emperor Jahangir.** **Portraits painted by him showed Jahangir in positions of dominance, towering over a globe, which represented the world.**

✅ **Fact - the named works.** **Some of Abu'l Hasan's famous paintings on Jahangir include *Jahangir with portrait of Akbar in his hand*, *Jahangir Standing on a Globe Shooting Poverty*, *Squirrels in a Plane Tree*, and *Jahangir embracing Persian Safavid Emperor Shah Abbas (Jahangir's Dream)*.**

> 🔑 Two titles, two men, easy to swap: **Ustad Mansur = *Nadir ul Asr*. Abu'l Hasan = *Nadir-al-Zaman*.** Both contain "*Nadir*"; the second word differs - *Asr* for Mansur, *Zaman* for Abu'l Hasan.

⚠️ **The argument the allegories supply, and it is the strongest in the lesson.** Allegorical portraiture is recorded as carrying **a subtle agenda** - supremacy, legitimacy and divine right. Painting here is not decoration or record but **a political instrument**, and the record makes the claim itself rather than leaving it to inference. ⚠️ The dream-embrace of Shah Abbas and the globe imagery are the named instances; describe them as recorded and do not construct a diplomatic history from them.

### 3. Shah Jahan: a reversal, explained by the record

✅ **Fact.** **The tenor of the Mughal paintings changed rapidly in the period of Shah Jahan.** **Unlike his father and grandfather who liked naturalistic depictions, Shah Jahan liked creating artificial elements in the paintings.** **It is said that he tried to reduce the liveliness of the paintings and bring in unnatural stillness as he was inspired by the European style.** **He eschewed the use of charcoal for drawing and encouraged artists to draw and sketch using a pencil.** **He also ordered artists to increase the use of gold and silver in the paintings.** **He also liked brighter colour palettes as compared to his predecessors.**

⚠️ **Preserve "it is said".** The attribution of the stillness to European inspiration is hedged in the record, and the hedge should travel with the claim.

⚠️ **Why this reign is examinable out of proportion to its length.** It is a **counter-example to the story of steady progress towards naturalism**. The conventional narrative runs Akbar's three-dimensionality to Jahangir's zenith of naturalism to decline under Aurangzeb; Shah Jahan interrupts it with a deliberate turn to artificiality, stillness, gold, silver and brighter colour. Any answer that presents Mughal painting as a single upward curve can be corrected by this reign, and doing so demonstrates control of the material.

⚠️ **Note the two material changes.** Charcoal is replaced by **pencil**; gold and silver are **increased**. These are instructions to a workshop, and they are the kind of specific evidence that distinguishes a prepared answer from a general one.

### 4. Aurangzeb, and the dispersal that makes the rest of the topic possible

✅ **Fact.** **Aurangzeb's disinterest led to a decline in Mughal painting as artists moved to provincial courts.**

✅ **Fact - the wider mechanism.** The imperial atelier's later **dispersal helped Rajasthan and Pahari centres**; continuity therefore worked through **mobile artists**, not through sealed dynastic boxes.

⚠️ **This is the hinge of the entire regional-schools half of the topic**, and it needs to be stated as a mechanism rather than as an event. The style was held by **salaried, trained, regionally recruited people**. Withdraw the salary and the people remain, with their training intact, and go where there is a patron. Three consequences follow, and all three are visible in later lessons:

| Consequence | Where it shows up |
|---|---|
| Regional courts acquire Mughal-trained skill | **Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh**, recorded as one of the sources of Rajput painting's development (Lesson 14) |
| A new school can be founded by migration | After the **decline of the Mughal empire, especially following Nadir Shah's invasion**, many artists trained in the Mughal style **migrated to the Kangra region**, giving rise to the **Guler-Kangra School** (Lesson 15) |
| Influence runs both ways and is selective | The record's own formulation: artists **borrowed from Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles**; regional schools **selectively reworked shared resources** |

❌ **The error to avoid.** Do not describe post-Mughal regional painting as "provincial Mughal painting". The recorded position is that regional schools took trained artists and shared resources and then developed independently - which is why Kishangarh does not look like Kota and neither looks like Kangra.

### 5. The Mughal sequence, compressed into a table you can reproduce

| Reign | The object being made | The dominant subject | A named technical or material fact |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Babur** | Little commissioned | Illustrations of the Mughal family tree, said to be by Bihzad | - |
| **Humayun** | Beautifully illustrated albums | - | Persian influence brought by Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali, recruited at Shah Tahmasp's court |
| **Akbar** | Large **illustrated manuscripts** from a salaried department | **Court life** replacing the life of the masses | Three-dimensional figures; foreshortening; encouraged calligraphy |
| **Jahangir** | **Albums** and individual portraits | **Flora and fauna**; naturalistic portraiture; allegory | Decorated margins as elaborate as the paintings |
| **Shah Jahan** | Continued court production | Artificial elements; unnatural stillness | **Pencil replaces charcoal**; increased gold and silver; brighter palettes |
| **Aurangzeb** | Decline | - | Artists move to provincial courts |

### Examination use

Jahangir's reign is the most frequently tested point in Mughal painting, and the tested distinction is the format shift - from illustrated manuscripts to albums and individual portraiture - together with his preference for flora and fauna. Secure recall should include the zebra, turkey and cock; Ustad Mansur with the title *Nadir ul Asr*; Abu'l Hasan, 1569-1627, chief portrait painter, with the title *Nadir-al-Zaman* and his four named works; the *Ayar-i-Danish* as a manuscript still illustrated in the reign; and the decorated margins. Shah Jahan supplies the counter-example to a smooth naturalist narrative, with pencil, gold and silver and brighter palettes. Aurangzeb supplies the dispersal mechanism that every subsequent lesson needs.

### Traps introduced here

- Assigning the shift to albums and individual portraiture to Akbar or Shah Jahan; it is Jahangir's.
- Swapping the two titles: Mansur is *Nadir ul Asr*, Abu'l Hasan is *Nadir-al-Zaman*.
- Saying manuscript illustration ceased under Jahangir; the *Ayar-i-Danish* was illustrated in his reign.
- Presenting Mughal painting as a continuous rise to naturalism, ignoring Shah Jahan's recorded turn to artificial elements and unnatural stillness.
- Dropping "it is said" from the claim that Shah Jahan's stillness came from European inspiration.
- Treating Abu'l Hasan's allegories as neutral portraiture; the record states their political agenda explicitly.
- Calling post-Mughal regional schools provincial Mughal painting.

### Revision notes

1. Mughal painting reached its zenith under Jahangir; he was a naturalist by nature and preferred flora and fauna - birds, animals, trees, flowers.
2. The format shift: from illustrated manuscripts to albums, with emphasis on bringing naturalism to portrait, that is individual, painting.
3. Decorated margins around paintings, sometimes as elaborate as the paintings themselves.
4. Jahangir was himself considered a good artist and had his own private workshop.
5. Most famous paintings of the reign: naturalistic paintings of the zebra, the turkey and the cock.
6. Ustad Mansur: title *Nadir ul Asr* from Jahangir; expert in drawing the features for the most complex faces.
7. *Ayar-i-Danish*, Touchstone of Knowledge, an animal fable, illustrated in Jahangir's reign - manuscript illustration continued.
8. Abu'l Hasan, 1569-1627: chief portrait painter; title *Nadir-al-Zaman*, wonder of the age; allegorical paintings with a subtle agenda reinforcing supremacy, legitimacy and divine right to rule; Jahangir shown towering over a globe representing the world.
9. Abu'l Hasan's named works: *Jahangir with portrait of Akbar in his hand*; *Jahangir Standing on a Globe Shooting Poverty*; *Squirrels in a Plane Tree*; *Jahangir embracing Persian Safavid Emperor Shah Abbas*, also called *Jahangir's Dream*.
10. Shah Jahan: tenor changed rapidly; liked artificial elements; said to have reduced liveliness and brought unnatural stillness, inspired by the European style; eschewed charcoal for pencil; ordered increased gold and silver; preferred brighter palettes.
11. Aurangzeb: disinterest led to decline as artists moved to provincial courts.
12. The dispersal mechanism: the atelier's later dispersal helped Rajasthan and Pahari centres; continuity worked through mobile artists, not sealed dynastic boxes; Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh, and Mughal-trained artists migrated to Kangra after Nadir Shah's invasion.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the move from illustrated manuscripts to albums is a change in the *kind* of object being made, and identify the two later developments that depend on it.

**Model answer:** In an illustrated manuscript the image is bound into a book and subordinate to a text: it is seen in the order the text dictates, it illustrates an episode, and its meaning is supplied by the narrative around it. That is what the great Akbari manuscripts - *Tutinama*, *Razmnama*, *Baburnama*, *Hamzanama*, *Tarikh-i-Alfi*, *Anvar-i-Suhaili*, *Gulistan of Sadi* - are. An album is a different kind of object: a collection of independent pages assembled, arranged and compared by a collector, in which each image is complete in itself and requires no surrounding story. The record places this shift under Jahangir, together with an emphasis on bringing naturalism to portrait, that is individual, painting. Two developments depend on it. First, individual portraiture becomes worth commissioning, because a portrait has no episode to illustrate and needs the album page to exist as a self-sufficient object; the reign's chief portrait painter, Abu'l Hasan, works in exactly this space. Second, the decorated margin appears as a unique trend, sometimes as elaborate as the painting itself, which is intelligible only on a page that is the object of attention rather than the edge of a text block.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the shift as the end of manuscript illustration. The record describes a shift of emphasis, and the *Ayar-i-Danish*, an animal fable, was illustrated in the same reign. The album is added to the repertoire; it does not replace the book.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Under the later Mughals, painting became an instrument of rule rather than a record of the world." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The statement is true of one strand and false as a general account of the period, and the examination must keep the two apart.

The instrument-of-rule case is explicit in the record. Abu'l Hasan, chief portrait painter and honoured as *Nadir-al-Zaman*, experimented with allegorical painting - symbolic fictional narrative with a subtle agenda that reinforced the supremacy, legitimacy and divine right to rule of his emperor. The named works carry it: Jahangir towering over a globe representing the world, *Jahangir Standing on a Globe Shooting Poverty*, *Jahangir with portrait of Akbar in his hand*, and the dream-embrace of Shah Abbas. Under Shah Jahan, the material instructions point the same way - increased gold and silver, brighter palettes, artificial elements and an unnatural stillness said to be inspired by the European style. These are decisions about magnificence.

The record-of-the-world case is equally documented and contemporaneous. Jahangir was a naturalist by nature, preferred flora and fauna, and the most famous paintings of his reign are the naturalistic zebra, turkey and cock; Ustad Mansur, titled *Nadir ul Asr*, was an expert in drawing the features of the most complex faces; and the emphasis on naturalism in individual portraiture is recorded alongside the allegory.

The two strands sit in the same reign and often in the same workshop, which is the real finding: the album page could carry an observed bird and a political allegory equally well.

The statement therefore describes an added function, not a replaced one.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the allegorical evidence with the agenda stated in the record's own terms and at least two named works 5 + Shah Jahan's material instructions read as magnificence 3 + the simultaneous naturalist strand with the three animals and Mansur 4 + the finding that both functions coexist in one reign and one workshop 2 + a verdict of addition rather than replacement 1.**

## Lesson 13 - The Deccani stream, and how to write about a school when the evidence thins

Progress: 13/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: what is actually recorded about Deccani painting, what is commonly asserted about it, and the disciplined way to handle the gap

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - every reference to the Deccan and Deccani painting across the transition-period, Sultanate, regional-school, Ragamala and Bundi passages, together with the recorded characterisation of Deccani work
CA search: "Deccani painting Bijapur Golconda Ahmadnagar scholarship 2026"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what is owned, what is inferred, and what is missing

```text
  +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | OWNED - stated in the evidence base                                 |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | 1. DECCAN is one of the THREE major styles that dominated the       |
  |    medieval landscape, alongside Mughal and Rajput                  |
  | 2. In the TRANSITION PERIOD, "in the Vijayanagara South, a style    |
  |    AKIN TO THE DECCAN SCHOOL emerged"                               |
  | 3. RAGAMALA paintings were created as PAHARI, RAJASTHAN/RAJPUT,     |
  |    DECCAN and MUGHAL Ragamala - so a Deccan Ragamala exists         |
  | 4. BUNDI's wall paintings show "early Rajasthani style with         |
  |    MUGHAL AND DECCANI INFLUENCES"                                   |
  | 5. Among the explanations of Rajput painting's development:         |
  |    "the influx of artists and artworks from the DECCAN SULTANATES   |
  |    played a decisive role"                                          |
  | 6. Characterised in the recorded description as:                    |
  |    SATURATED COLOUR, ELONGATED FIGURES, FANTASTIC LANDSCAPE, and    |
  |    PERSIANATE-DECCAN COURT CULTURE; "not Mughal despite shared      |
  |    Persianate roots"                                                |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
  | NOT OWNED - and therefore NOT SUPPLIED ANYWHERE IN THIS SESSION     |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |  x  no named Deccani court is described as a painting centre        |
  |  x  no named Deccani painter                                        |
  |  x  no named Deccani manuscript or album                            |
  |  x  no dated Deccani painting or phase boundary                     |
  |  x  no Deccani technique term or material list                      |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*This is an evidence map, and it is the honest one. The left-hand column is enough to write a correct paragraph; the right-hand column is where candidates lose marks by filling silence from memory.*

### 1. Why this lesson exists at all

Every other school in this topic arrives with sites, patrons, painters and works. The Deccani stream arrives as a **name in a list of three**, plus four scattered corroborating mentions and one characterisation. That is a genuine asymmetry in the evidence, and it has to be handled rather than hidden.

⚠️ **The discipline this lesson teaches is transferable.** When a syllabus names something the evidence base treats thinly, there are exactly three legitimate moves: state what is recorded; state the boundary explicitly; and build the surrounding argument from the material that *is* evidenced. The illegitimate move - inventing plausible detail - is the single most expensive habit in this topic, because plausible detail is exactly what close options are built to punish.

### 2. What is recorded, assembled

✅ **Fact - the stream.** **Three major styles emerged that dominated the medieval landscape: Mughal, Rajput, Deccan.** The artists **borrowed elements from the Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles.**

✅ **Fact - the early anchor.** In the transition period, **in the Vijayanagara South, a style akin to the Deccan school emerged**.

✅ **Fact - a genre presence.** **Ragamala paintings were created as Pahari Ragamala, Rajasthan or Rajput Ragamala, Deccan Ragamala and Mughal Ragamala.**

✅ **Fact - an influence on Rajasthan, recorded twice.** **Bundi's paintings have an early Rajasthani style with Mughal and Deccani influences.** And among the recorded explanations of the development of Rajput painting: **the influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates played a decisive role.**

✅ **Fact - the recorded characterisation.** Deccani painting is characterised by **saturated colour, elongated figures, fantastic landscape** and **Persianate-Deccan court culture**, and the close-option distinction is that it is **not Mughal despite shared Persianate roots**.

### 3. The one genuinely strong argument the Deccani material supports

⚠️ **Deccani painting is the best available evidence that the three medieval streams were not sealed.** Consider what the record establishes without any additional detail:

1. A style akin to the Deccan school emerges in the **Vijayanagara South** during the transition period - that is, before and independently of the mature Mughal atelier.
2. **Deccani influence appears in Bundi's wall paintings**, in Rajasthan, alongside Mughal influence.
3. **An influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates** is recorded as one of the decisive explanations for the development of Rajput painting.
4. A **Deccan Ragamala** exists as one of the four named Ragamala families.

Put together, these make the Deccan a **source**, not merely a recipient. The conventional narrative in which everything radiates from the Mughal court is contradicted by its own evidence: Rajput painting's development is explained partly by Deccani artists arriving, and a Rajasthani wall-painting centre carries Deccani influence. ⚠️ That is an inference from four recorded facts, and it should be labelled as an inference - but it is a well-supported one and it is the most valuable thing this lesson produces.

### 4. The safe formulations, written out

Because this is a lesson about how to write, the formulations themselves are the content.

| If you need to say | Safe formulation | Unsafe formulation |
|---|---|---|
| What Deccani painting is | "One of the three major styles that dominated the medieval landscape, alongside Mughal and Rajput" | "The painting of the Bijapur, Golconda and Ahmadnagar courts" - not carried here |
| How it looks | "Characterised by saturated colour, elongated figures and fantastic landscape, within a Persianate-Deccan court culture" | Any specific palette, pigment or composition claim |
| Its relation to Mughal painting | "Not Mughal, despite shared Persianate roots" | "A provincial variant of Mughal painting" |
| Its early history | "A style akin to the Deccan school emerged in the Vijayanagara South in the transition period" | A foundation date or founding court |
| Its effect elsewhere | "An influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates is recorded as a decisive factor in the development of Rajput painting, and Bundi's wall paintings carry Deccani alongside Mughal influence" | Named Deccani artists at named Rajput courts |
| Its genre range | "Ragamala sets were produced in a Deccan family alongside Pahari, Rajput and Mughal families" | Named Deccani Ragamala sets |

### 5. The general rule, since it applies beyond this lesson

⚠️ **The evidence-boundary rule for the whole topic.** When you can name a tradition but cannot evidence its detail, write **three sentences and stop**: what it is classified as; what it is characterised by; and what it is recorded as influencing or being influenced by. Then return to evidenced ground. An examiner rewards the demonstrated boundary far more reliably than an unverifiable list, because the boundary shows judgement and the list shows only recall that may be wrong.

⚠️ **Where else in this session the same rule is applied.** Badami, named in the mural site list with no described programme (Lesson 3). Tirumalaipuram, where nothing significant remains (Lesson 6). The clipped entries in the illustrated-manuscript table and the notable-paintings table (Lessons 16 and 17). In each case the boundary is stated and nothing is supplied from memory.

### Examination use

Two things are examinable here. First, the identification content: Deccan as one of three medieval streams; saturated colour, elongated figures and fantastic landscape; Persianate-Deccan court culture; and the close-option distinction from Mughal painting despite shared Persianate roots. Second, and more valuable in a written answer, the argument that the Deccan was a source of influence rather than only a recipient of it, evidenced by the Vijayanagara-South emergence, the Deccani influence at Bundi, the recorded influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates into Rajput painting, and the existence of a Deccan Ragamala family. The lesson also supplies the transferable technique of writing a bounded paragraph where the evidence thins.

### Traps introduced here

- Supplying named Deccani courts, painters, manuscripts or dates that the evidence base does not carry.
- Describing Deccani painting as provincial Mughal work, when the recorded distinction is that it is not Mughal despite shared Persianate roots.
- Treating the Deccan as purely a recipient of Mughal influence, when it is recorded as a decisive source of influence on Rajput painting.
- Forgetting the Vijayanagara-South emergence, which is the earliest Deccan-related anchor in the record.
- Omitting the Deccan Ragamala from the four Ragamala families.
- Filling an evidence gap because the answer looks thin; a stated boundary is worth more than an unverifiable list.

### Revision notes

1. Deccan is one of the three major styles that dominated the medieval landscape, with Mughal and Rajput; all three borrowed from Sultanate art and developed individual styles.
2. Transition period: in the Vijayanagara South, a style akin to the Deccan school emerged.
3. Ragamala families: Pahari, Rajasthan or Rajput, Deccan, Mughal.
4. Bundi's wall paintings: early Rajasthani style with Mughal and Deccani influences.
5. Among the recorded explanations of Rajput painting's development: the influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates played a decisive role.
6. Characterisation: saturated colour, elongated figures, fantastic landscape, Persianate-Deccan court culture; not Mughal despite shared Persianate roots.
7. Not carried anywhere in this evidence base, and therefore never to be supplied: named Deccani courts as painting centres, named painters, named manuscripts or albums, dates or phase boundaries, technique terms or material lists.
8. The best-supported inference: the Deccan was a source of influence, not only a recipient.
9. The evidence-boundary rule: classification, characterisation, recorded relations - then stop.
10. The same rule governs Badami, Tirumalaipuram and the clipped source tables elsewhere in this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** An answer requires one paragraph on Deccani painting. Write the paragraph's content as a list of claims, and state for each whether it is recorded or inferred.

**Model answer:** Six claims, correctly typed. One, recorded: the Deccan is one of three major styles that dominated the medieval landscape alongside Mughal and Rajput, all of which borrowed from Sultanate art while developing their own individual styles. Two, recorded: a style akin to the Deccan school emerged in the Vijayanagara South during the transition period, which places a Deccan-related manner early rather than as a Mughal offshoot. Three, recorded characterisation: saturated colour, elongated figures and fantastic landscape within a Persianate-Deccan court culture, distinct from Mughal painting despite shared Persianate roots. Four, recorded: Bundi's wall paintings carry Deccani alongside Mughal influence. Five, recorded: an influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates is given as a decisive factor in the development of Rajput painting. Six, inferred from claims two, four and five: the Deccan functioned as a source of influence and not merely as a recipient of it, which qualifies any account that radiates all medieval Indian painting outwards from the Mughal court. The paragraph then stops, because no named Deccani court, painter, manuscript, date or technique is available, and a stated boundary is safer and more creditable than a supplied list.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a short paragraph signals weak preparation. In a close-option environment the opposite holds: the candidate who writes five recorded claims, one labelled inference and an explicit boundary has demonstrated exactly the judgement the examiner is testing, while the candidate who adds three plausible names has created three opportunities to be wrong.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Medieval Indian painting is best understood as a field of exchanges rather than a set of court styles." Examine, giving particular attention to the Deccan. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The exchange model fits the record better than the court-style model, and the Deccan is the sharpest test.

The record's own framing is exchange: three major styles - Mughal, Rajput and Deccan - emerged whose artists borrowed elements from Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles. Exchange is then documented in specific directions. Mughal artists arrived at Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; Mughal-trained artists migrated to the Kangra region after the empire's decline; and, crucially, an influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates is recorded as a decisive factor in the development of Rajput painting, while Bundi's wall paintings carry Deccani alongside Mughal influence. A Deccan Ragamala family exists beside Pahari, Rajput and Mughal ones.

The Deccan therefore transmits as well as receives, which a court-style model cannot accommodate.

The qualification is that exchange did not dissolve identity: the schools are recorded as developing individually, and Deccani work is explicitly not Mughal.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the record's borrowing-plus-development framing used as the governing formula 2 + at least three documented directions of movement including one out of the Deccan 4 + the Deccan Ragamala as genre evidence 2 + the qualification that exchange preserved distinct identities 2.**

*One family, four recorded origin stories, four principal branches and one sub-school famous enough to be examined by name. Note that Kishangarh sits inside Marwar - it is "one of the prominent that come under the Marwar School".*

### 1. The group, and its four competing origin stories

✅ **Fact - the group.** The **artworks from the regional schools are also commonly referred to as Central Indian Painting.** **Central Indian paintings used vibrant colours and drew inspiration from epics, Puranas and folklore, differing from the Mughal style.** The **Rajasthani School, more or less synonymous with the Rajput School, was patronised by Rajput rulers.**

✅ **Fact - the four explanations, all recorded as current.** **Sources in the development of Rajput paintings are many**, and the record lists four:

1. **Rajput courts, influenced by Mughal practices, developed local schools as Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh.**
2. **Others say that the influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates played a decisive role.**
3. **Yet others say that local and indigenous artistic traditions predated the arrival of Mughal influence in these centres.**
4. **And yet others point to the "*ganga-jamni*" confluence of cultures in Sultanate courts.**

⚠️ **This is an unusually valuable passage and should be used as such.** It is an explicit statement that the origin of Rajput painting is **contested among four positions**, and it is therefore the ideal opening for any question about the relationship between Mughal and Rajput painting. ❌ Do not present any one of the four as the settled account. ✅ The high-scoring move is to state that four explanations are current, name them, and then observe that they are not mutually exclusive: artists could arrive from Mughal and Deccani centres into courts that already had indigenous traditions, within a broader Sultanate-era cultural confluence.

⚠️ **Note that explanation 3 is a direct challenge to the other three.** If local traditions predated Mughal influence, then Rajput painting is not derivative at all. Keeping that option visible is what prevents the standard error of treating Rajasthani painting as downstream Mughal work.

### 2. Mewar

✅ **Fact - the politics.** **The kingdom of Mewar resisted Mughal suzerainty for the longest time, eventually agreeing to Mughal power in the reign of Shah Jahan.**

✅ **Fact - the early phase and its painter.** **Early Mewar painting is dominated by the 17th-century painter Sahibdin, who illustrated texts like *Ragamala*, *Ramayana*, and *Bhagavata Purana*.**

✅ **Fact - the later phase.** **Later Mewari paintings focused on court life, with detailed *tamasha* scenes of ceremonies and city life.**

✅ **Fact - the sub-styles.** **Nathdwara, Deogarh and Shahpura paintings are the sub-styles of the Mewar School of painting.**

⚠️ **Two things worth noticing.** First, Mewar supplies a **named seventeenth-century painter with a named body of work** - Sahibdin - which is the strongest individual attribution available in the Rajasthani group. Second, the school's trajectory runs **from religious text illustration to detailed scenes of ceremony and city life**, which is the same manuscript-to-court-subject movement observed in the Mughal atelier, arrived at independently. ⚠️ Note also that **Nathdwara** appears here as a Mewar sub-style and again in Lesson 18 as the home of Pichhwai painting; the place does two kinds of work in this topic.

### 3. Amber-Jaipur

✅ **Fact - the association and the school.** **The Amber rulers were another dynasty that was closely associated with the Mughals.** The **Amber (Dhundar) school**, seen in **Bairat and palace walls and the mausoleum at Amer Palace, Rajasthan**, **blends folk-style painting with occasional Mughal-influenced clothing and headgear**.

✅ **Fact - the peak and its patron.** The school **reached its pinnacle in the period of Sawai Pratap Singh (known for constructing Hawa Mahal of Jaipur) in the 18th century**. He was **a deeply religious man and a passionate patron of the arts**, and **these two aspects combined to ensure that his *suratkhana*, or department of painting, made miniatures to illustrate the *Bhagwata Purana*, the *Ramayana*, *Ragamala* paintings and several portraits.**

⚠️ **The "*suratkhana*" is the point to seize.** It is a **department of painting** at a Rajput court - the same institutional form as the Mughal *Tasvir Khana* of Lesson 11, under a different name. That is evidence that the Rajput courts adopted not merely a manner but an **organisational model**, and it is a better observation than any stylistic comparison. ⚠️ The alternative school-name **Dhundar** is worth carrying because a question may use either label.

### 4. Marwar

✅ **Fact - the extent and the subject.** **The Marwar school, encompassing Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer, depicted 15th-16th century court life with men and women in colourful attire, reflecting the desert kingdoms' prosperity and Mughal connections.**

✅ **Fact - the trajectory.** **Marwar paintings, initially Mughal-influenced, became predominantly Rajput after the 18th century.**

✅ **Fact - a dated patron and four named works.** **Under Man Singh (1803-1843), Jodhpur produced works like *Shiva Purana*, *Natacharitra*, *Durgacharitra*, and *Panchtantra*.**

✅ **Fact - the relationship to Kishangarh.** **The Kishangarh School is one of the prominent that come under the Marwar School.**

⚠️ **The trajectory statement is the one to use.** "Initially Mughal-influenced, became predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century" describes a school moving **away** from imperial influence over time. That is the opposite of the expected direction, and it is direct evidence against the view that regional schools were progressively absorbed into a Mughal manner.

### 5. Kishangarh, and the handling of *Bani Thani*

✅ **Fact - dates and the human material.** The **Kishangarh School (17th-18th century AD)**'s paintings are **associated with the most romantic legends - for example, the tale of Sawant Singh and his beloved Bani Thani, and the intertwining of lives and myths, romance and bhakti.**

✅ **Fact - the collaboration.** **The most celebrated works of this school were created by the painter Nihal Chand, in collaboration with prince Sawant Singh and poet Nagari Das.**

✅ **Fact - the painting.** **The Kishangarh School is famed for the painting *Bani Thani*, whose female figure resembles Radha with lotus-like elongated eyes, thin lips, a pointed chin, and a side-profile defined by her *odhni*.** **The artists also made many paintings on the devotional and amorous relations between Radha and Krishna.**

⚠️ **The attribution discipline, stated exactly, because this is where candidates over-reach.** The record establishes three things: that the school is **famed for** *Bani Thani*; that the school's **most celebrated works** were created by **Nihal Chand** in collaboration with Sawant Singh and Nagari Das; and that a reproduction of the painting is captioned as being **by Nihal Chand**. ✅ The safe formulation is therefore ***Bani Thani*, associated with Nihal Chand, of the Kishangarh School**. ❌ The unsafe formulation is a flat assertion in a context that requires documented single authorship. ❌ And under no circumstances attribute *Bani Thani* to any school other than Kishangarh.

⚠️ **Why this school is disproportionately examined.** It has everything a question needs: a named school, a named painting, a named painter, a named patron who was also a poet under a pen-name, a described face, and a devotional subject. The four described features - **lotus-like elongated eyes, thin lips, pointed chin, side profile defined by the *odhni*** - are precise enough to be tested individually.

### 6. Ragamala: the genre that joins three arts

✅ **Fact - what it is.** **Ragamala Paintings are a series of illustrative paintings from medieval India based on *Ragamala*, the "Garland of Ragas".** **They depict various Indian musical ragas** and **stand as a classical example of the amalgamation of art, poetry and classical music in medieval India.**

✅ **Fact - the four families.** **Ragamala paintings were created as Pahari Ragamala, Rajasthan or Rajput Ragamala, Deccan Ragamala and Mughal Ragamala.**

✅ **Fact - how a raga is pictured.** **In these paintings, each raga is personified by a colour**, and they **describe the story of a hero and heroine - *nayaka* and *nayika* - in a particular mood**. They **also elucidate the season and the time of day and night in which a particular raga is to be sung**. **Moreover, many paintings also demarcate the specific Hindu deities associated with the raga, like Bhairava or Bhairavi to Shiva, Sri to Devi.**

✅ **Fact - the six.** **The six principal ragas present in the Ragamala are Bhairava, Deepak, Sri, Malkaush, Megh and Hindola.**

> 🔑 Six principal ragas: **B-D-S-M-M-H** - *Bhairava, Deepak, Sri, Malkaush, Megh, Hindola*.

⚠️ **Why Ragamala is worth a paragraph in almost any painting answer.** It is a **single genre in which four schools all worked**, which makes it the natural vehicle for comparison; and it is the topic's clearest instance of painting carrying **non-visual content** - a musical mode, a time of day, a season, an emotional state and a deity, all encoded in a picture of two figures. A painting that tells you *when to sing* is doing something no purely visual account of Indian art will explain.

⚠️ **The cross-link to hold.** Ragamala and *Nayaka-Nayika Bheda* themes appear in **Marwar and Mewar wall painting** (Lesson 8) as well as in miniature; Sahibdin illustrated a *Ragamala* at Mewar; and Sawai Pratap Singh's *suratkhana* made Ragamala paintings at Jaipur. The genre crosses both formats and schools.

### Examination use

The Rajasthani group supplies the topic's densest identification material and one exceptionally useful argumentative passage. The four competing explanations of Rajput painting's development are the best available opening for any Mughal-Rajput comparison, because they establish that the question is contested. Identification recall should be secure on: Mewar's resistance until Shah Jahan, Sahibdin, the shift to *tamasha* scenes, and the Nathdwara-Deogarh-Shahpura sub-styles; Amber's alternative name Dhundar, Sawai Pratap Singh, Hawa Mahal and the *suratkhana* with its four output types; Marwar's three-city extent, its move from Mughal-influenced to predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century, and Man Singh's 1803-1843 works; Kishangarh's dates, Sawant Singh, Nagari Das, Nihal Chand and the four described features of *Bani Thani*; and Ragamala's four families, six principal ragas and its encoding of colour, season, time and deity.

### Traps introduced here

- Presenting one of the four origin explanations as the settled account.
- Placing Kishangarh outside the Marwar School.
- Attributing *Bani Thani* to Bundi, Jaipur or Kangra, or asserting single authorship without the "associated with" qualification.
- Describing Marwar as progressively absorbed into a Mughal manner, when the record has it becoming predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century.
- Treating the Amber *suratkhana* as a style rather than as a department of painting.
- Confusing Mewar's Nathdwara sub-style with the Pichhwai tradition of Nathdwara; both are recorded, and they are different things.
- Listing fewer than six principal ragas, or omitting the Deccan Ragamala family.
- Describing Ragamala paintings as illustrations of songs; they encode raga, season, time of day, mood and associated deity.

### Revision notes

1. Regional-school works are also called Central Indian Painting: vibrant colours; epics, Puranas and folklore; differing from the Mughal style. The Rajasthani School is more or less synonymous with the Rajput School and was patronised by Rajput rulers.
2. Four recorded explanations of Rajput painting's development: Mughal artists arriving at Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; a decisive influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates; local and indigenous traditions predating Mughal influence; and the *ganga-jamni* confluence in Sultanate courts.
3. Mewar: resisted Mughal suzerainty longest, agreeing under Shah Jahan; early painting dominated by the 17th-century Sahibdin, who illustrated *Ragamala*, *Ramayana* and *Bhagavata Purana*; later focus on court life with detailed *tamasha* scenes of ceremonies and city life; sub-styles Nathdwara, Deogarh, Shahpura.
4. Amber-Jaipur: Amber rulers closely associated with the Mughals; the Amber or Dhundar school seen at Bairat, on palace walls and at the Amer Palace mausoleum; blends folk-style painting with occasional Mughal-influenced clothing and headgear.
5. Sawai Pratap Singh, 18th century, builder of the Hawa Mahal: deeply religious and a passionate patron; his *suratkhana*, a department of painting, made miniatures of the *Bhagwata Purana*, the *Ramayana*, Ragamala paintings and several portraits.
6. Marwar: encompasses Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer; 15th-16th century court life, colourful attire, desert prosperity and Mughal connections; initially Mughal-influenced, predominantly Rajput after the 18th century; under Man Singh, 1803-1843, Jodhpur produced *Shiva Purana*, *Natacharitra*, *Durgacharitra* and *Panchtantra*.
7. Kishangarh, 17th-18th century, comes under the Marwar School; romantic legends including Sawant Singh and Bani Thani; most celebrated works by Nihal Chand in collaboration with prince Sawant Singh and poet Nagari Das.
8. *Bani Thani*: female figure resembling Radha; lotus-like elongated eyes, thin lips, pointed chin, side profile defined by the *odhni*; the school also produced many devotional and amorous Radha-Krishna paintings. Write it as associated with Nihal Chand, and never assign it to another school.
9. Ragamala: series of illustrative paintings based on the "Garland of Ragas"; depicts Indian musical ragas; a classical example of the amalgamation of art, poetry and classical music.
10. Four Ragamala families: Pahari, Rajasthan or Rajput, Deccan, Mughal.
11. Each raga personified by a colour; the story of a *nayaka* and *nayika* in a particular mood; season and time of day or night for singing; associated deities such as Bhairava or Bhairavi to Shiva and Sri to Devi.
12. Six principal ragas: Bhairava, Deepak, Sri, Malkaush, Megh, Hindola.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the passage listing four explanations for the development of Rajput painting more valuable in an answer than any one of those explanations would be?

**Model answer:** Because it converts a factual claim into a historiographical position, and historiography is what a "compare" or "examine" directive is testing. The four recorded explanations are: that Rajput courts, influenced by Mughal practices, developed local schools as Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; that the influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates played a decisive role; that local and indigenous artistic traditions predated the arrival of Mughal influence in these centres; and that the *ganga-jamni* confluence of cultures in Sultanate courts was responsible. Asserting only the first produces the commonest weak answer - Rajput painting as downstream Mughal work - and it is contradicted within the same evidence base by the third. Stating all four allows three moves that a single explanation forbids: identifying the question as open; noting that the explanations are not mutually exclusive, since artists could arrive from Mughal and Deccani centres into courts with existing traditions inside a wider Sultanate-era confluence; and supporting the plural account with independent evidence, such as Marwar painting becoming predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century rather than more Mughal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that naming a controversy is a way of avoiding commitment. It is the opposite: the passage lets you commit to a specific, defensible position - that the causes are multiple and compatible - and then evidence it, which is stronger than asserting a single cause the record does not endorse.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In the Rajput courts, the organisation of painting mattered as much as its style." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is well supported and should be argued through institutions, patrons and workforce rather than through manner.

The clearest evidence is institutional. At Amber-Jaipur, Sawai Pratap Singh - a deeply religious man and a passionate patron - maintained a *suratkhana*, a department of painting, which produced miniatures illustrating the *Bhagwata Purana* and the *Ramayana*, Ragamala paintings and several portraits. A named department with a defined output is the same organisational form as an imperial studio, adopted under a Rajput name, and it explains output range better than any stylistic observation can.

Workforce evidence points the same way. One recorded explanation of Rajput painting's development is that local schools formed as Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; another gives a decisive role to an influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates. Both are statements about the movement of trained people.

Collaboration matters too: Kishangarh's most celebrated works were created by the painter Nihal Chand in collaboration with prince Sawant Singh and the poet Nagari Das, which is a working arrangement across three roles.

Style is not thereby demoted. Mewar's *tamasha* scenes, Marwar's turn to a predominantly Rajput manner after the eighteenth century and Kishangarh's lotus-like elongated eyes are genuine formal facts.

But the organisation explains why those styles could be developed, staffed and sustained, which is the stronger explanatory claim.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the *suratkhana* identified as a department with its recorded output list 5 + two recorded workforce-movement explanations 4 + the Kishangarh three-role collaboration 3 + genuine stylistic facts conceded from three schools 2 + a verdict that ranks explanatory power rather than importance 1.**

*Two ends of one family: Basohli's vibrant primary colours and lotus-shaped eyes at one pole, Guler-Kangra's modelled figures and other-worldly feel at the other, with Mankot, Kullu, Mandi and Jammu distributed between them. Note the genuine complication - the Shangri Ramayana is recorded both at Kullu and as produced in Jammu.*

### 1. The family, its extent and its three great names

✅ **Fact.** **Pahari painting, from the sub-Himalayan states under Mughal influence, includes multiple schools across 22 princely States stretching from Jammu to Almora**, and the school runs from the **17th to the 19th century**. **Pahari paintings feature dynamic mythological and literary scenes, with unique figures.** **Three of the greatest painters of this school were Nainsukh, Manaku and Sansar Chand.**

⚠️ **Two cautions on that last sentence.** It names **Sansar Chand** among the three greatest painters, while elsewhere he is recorded as the **patron** under whom Kangra reached its zenith and as the **most important patron** of Himachal wall painting. The record supports both roles; report the name as it is recorded in each place and do not attempt to resolve the two roles into one. ⚠️ **Nainsukh** and **Manaku** are named here and nowhere elaborated; name them and stop.

⚠️ **The "22 princely States" figure is the strongest single argument against treating Pahari as one style.** Twenty-two states across a stretch from Jammu to Almora cannot share one workshop tradition, and the record duly supplies **seven** named centres - Basohli, Mankot, Guler, Kangra, Jammu or Dogra, Kullu and Mandi - with visibly different colour and temperament.

### 2. Basohli and Mankot: the intense pole

✅ **Fact - Basohli.** **Paintings of the Pahari school in the 17th century were from Basohli in Jammu and Kashmir. They were miniature paintings.** They **featured lotus-shaped eyes, receding hairlines, and vibrant primary colours, blending Mughal cloth techniques with unique local styles.** **The first patron of this school was Raja Kirpal Pal, who ordered the illustration of Bhanudatta's *Rasamanjari*, *Gita Govinda* and the *Ramayana* drawings.** **The most famous painter of this school was Devi Das, who was famous for his depiction of Radha and Krishna and the portrait of kings in their livery and in white garments.** **The use of contrasting colours is associated with this school, a practice borrowed from the Malwa paintings.**

⚠️ **The Malwa borrowing is the detail that upgrades an answer.** It shows Basohli drawing on a **Central Indian** source, not only on Mughal or local material, and it is the sort of specific cross-regional link that distinguishes prepared work.

✅ **Fact - Mankot.** **Mankot paintings are found in Jammu and Kashmir.** They **closely resemble the Basohli type of painting.** **Vivid colours and bold subjects are the specific features.** **In the mid-17th century, portraiture became a general theme.** **In the later phase, emphasis was given to naturalism and muted colours.**

⚠️ **Mankot is a miniature history of the whole Pahari family in one school.** It begins vivid and bold like Basohli and ends naturalistic and muted like Guler-Kangra. A single sub-school therefore traverses the same distance the family traverses, which is a neat way to show that the two poles are phases as well as places.

### 3. Guler and Kangra: the lyrical pole, and how it was made

✅ **Fact - Guler.** **The word "Guler" comes from the word "Galwa", which means cowherd.** It **has some similarities with Mughal Art.** The **main features are strong strokes with fine and sensitive expressions.** The paintings **mainly cover *Srimad Bhagawata*, *Bihari Satasai* and *Gita Govinda*.** They are **delicately done with well-modelled female figures with upturned nose and minutely done hair.** **This school is from Himachal Pradesh**, and dates **from the 18th century**.

✅ **Fact - the migration that created Kangra.** **After the decline of the Mughal empire, especially following Nadir Shah's invasion, many artists trained in the Mughal style migrated to the Kangra region of Himachal Pradesh, where they received patronage from the Rajput Kingdom.** **The migration of artists led to the birth of the Guler-Kangra School of painting.**

✅ **Fact - Kangra's zenith and character.** **This school reached its zenith under the patronage of Raja Sansar Chand.** **The paintings that he commissioned were marked with a sensuality and intelligence that the other schools lacked.** **The popular subjects were the *Gita Govinda*, *Bhagwata Purana*, *Satsai* of Biharilal and *Nal Damayanti*.** **Love scenes of Krishna were a very prominent theme.** **All the paintings had an other-worldly feel about them.**

✅ **Fact - the Twelve Months and the school's reach.** **Another very famous group of paintings is the "Twelve months paintings", where the artist tried to bring out the effects of the 12 months on the emotions of human beings.** **This emotive style was popular until the 19th century.** **The Kangra school became the parent school to the other ateliers which developed in the region of Kullu, Chamba and Mandi.** **In Kangra, the Sansar Chand Museum can be visited to see the prominent Kangra school paintings.**

⚠️ **The Kangra case is the topic's best-documented instance of school formation by migration**, and it should be written as a four-step chain: imperial decline, intensified by Nadir Shah's invasion; migration of Mughal-trained artists to Kangra; Rajput patronage received there; the Guler-Kangra School born. Note that the chain is **the same mechanism** as the Aurangzeb dispersal of Lesson 12, observed at its destination rather than at its source.

⚠️ **Handle "a sensuality and intelligence that the other schools lacked" as the record's own comparative judgement.** It is an evaluation, not a measurement. Quote it as a recorded assessment rather than asserting the ranking yourself.

⚠️ **The Twelve Months paintings are worth a sentence in any answer about theme.** A series organised by **month**, attempting the effect of each month on human emotion, is a **systematic** subject - the same impulse that organises Ragamala by raga, season and time of day. Indian court painting repeatedly builds sets around a **classificatory scheme**, and naming two such schemes shows the pattern rather than the instance.

### 4. Jammu-Dogra, Kullu and Mandi

✅ **Fact - Jammu or Dogra School, from the 17th century.** It **bears a remarkable resemblance to the Kangra style of painting.** **The *Shangri Ramayana* of the late 17th and early 18th centuries was produced in Jammu itself. This painting has mythology as the theme.**

✅ **Fact - Kullu, from the 17th century.** **Similar to Basohli paintings, Pahari artists in Kullu created the *Shangri Ramayana*, depicting events like Bal Kanda, Ayodhya Kanda, and Uttar Kanda, using dull, dark colours.**

✅ **Fact - Mandi, from the 17th century.** **From 1684 to 1727, Mandi had gone through the evolution of a new style of painting under Raja Sidh Sen.** **Mandi paintings, mostly miniatures, depict rulers with exaggerated features and religious themes like Shiva, Rama and Krishna, using dark, muted colours on dark backgrounds.**

⚠️ **A disclosed complication that must not be smoothed over.** The ***Shangri Ramayana*** is recorded **twice**: as **produced in Jammu itself** in the late seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries, and as **created by Pahari artists in Kullu** in dull, dark colours. The evidence does not reconcile the two. ✅ The disciplined formulation: *"the Shangri Ramayana is recorded both as produced in Jammu and as created by Pahari artists in Kullu; the two statements are not reconciled in the evidence."* ❌ Do not pick one and present it as settled, and do not invent a transfer or a second set to explain the difference.

⚠️ **Mandi's "exaggerated features" for rulers is a rare formal note about portraiture outside the Mughal orbit**, and its dark-on-dark palette is the strongest colour contrast to Basohli's vibrant primaries within the same family.

### 5. The comparison table, built on the axes that actually differ

| Sub-school | From | Where | Colour | Distinctive feature | Named person or work |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Basohli** | 17th c. | Jammu and Kashmir | **Vibrant primary**; contrasting colours borrowed from **Malwa** | **Lotus-shaped eyes, receding hairlines**; blends Mughal cloth techniques with local styles | Patron **Raja Kirpal Pal**; painter **Devi Das**; *Rasamanjari* of **Bhanudatta**, *Gita Govinda*, *Ramayana* |
| **Mankot** | 17th c. | Jammu and Kashmir | **Vivid**, later **muted** | Closely resembles Basohli; **portraiture** general from the mid-17th c.; later **naturalism** | - |
| **Guler** | 18th c. | Himachal Pradesh | Delicate | **Strong strokes, fine and sensitive expressions**; well-modelled female figures, **upturned nose**, minutely done hair | Name from **"Galwa"**, cowherd; *Srimad Bhagawata*, *Bihari Satasai*, *Gita Govinda* |
| **Kangra** | mid-18th c. | Himachal Pradesh | - | **Sensuality and intelligence**; **other-worldly feel**; parent school to Kullu, Chamba, Mandi | **Raja Sansar Chand**; *Gita Govinda*, *Bhagwata Purana*, *Satsai* of **Biharilal**, *Nal Damayanti*; **Twelve months paintings**; Sansar Chand Museum |
| **Jammu/Dogra** | 17th c. | Jammu | - | **Remarkable resemblance to Kangra** | ***Shangri Ramayana***, late 17th-early 18th c., mythology |
| **Kullu** | 17th c. | Himachal Pradesh | **Dull, dark** | Similar to Basohli | ***Shangri Ramayana*** - Bal Kanda, Ayodhya Kanda, Uttar Kanda |
| **Mandi** | 17th c.; the new style **1684-1727** | Himachal Pradesh | **Dark, muted on dark backgrounds** | Rulers with **exaggerated features** | **Raja Sidh Sen**; Shiva, Rama, Krishna |

### Examination use

Pahari painting is examined both by sub-school identification and by the migration argument. Identification should be secure on Basohli's lotus-shaped eyes, receding hairlines, vibrant primary colours, Raja Kirpal Pal, Devi Das and the Malwa-borrowed contrasts; Guler's etymology from *Galwa*, its strong strokes and upturned-nose female figures; Kangra's formation from Mughal-trained migrants after Nadir Shah's invasion, Raja Sansar Chand, the four named text subjects, the other-worldly feel, the Twelve Months paintings and its parentage of Kullu, Chamba and Mandi; Mandi's 1684-1727 style under Raja Sidh Sen with dark colours on dark backgrounds; Mankot's Basohli resemblance and its shift to naturalism and muted colour; and the Jammu-Kullu *Shangri Ramayana* complication. The argument to carry is that twenty-two princely states from Jammu to Almora cannot share one style, and the evidence duly shows two poles.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "Pahari" as a single style rather than as a family across twenty-two princely states from Jammu to Almora.
- Giving Kangra's lyrical naturalism to Basohli, or Basohli's vibrant primaries to Kangra.
- Forgetting that Basohli's contrasting colours are recorded as borrowed from Malwa painting.
- Resolving the *Shangri Ramayana* to one place when the record locates it both in Jammu and at Kullu.
- Making Kangra the earliest Pahari school; Basohli, Mankot, Kullu, Mandi and Jammu are all recorded from the seventeenth century, Kangra from the mid-eighteenth.
- Asserting the comparative judgement about Kangra's sensuality and intelligence as your own rather than as a recorded assessment.
- Treating Sansar Chand only as a patron or only as a painter when the record names him in both roles.

### Revision notes

1. Pahari painting: sub-Himalayan states under Mughal influence; multiple schools across 22 princely States from Jammu to Almora; 17th-19th century; dynamic mythological and literary scenes with unique figures.
2. Three of the greatest painters of the school: Nainsukh, Manaku and Sansar Chand.
3. Basohli, from the 17th century, Jammu and Kashmir: miniature paintings; lotus-shaped eyes, receding hairlines, vibrant primary colours; blends Mughal cloth techniques with unique local styles; first patron Raja Kirpal Pal, who ordered Bhanudatta's *Rasamanjari*, *Gita Govinda* and *Ramayana* drawings; most famous painter Devi Das, known for Radha and Krishna and portraits of kings in livery and white garments; contrasting colours borrowed from Malwa paintings.
4. Mankot, from the 17th century, Jammu and Kashmir: closely resembles Basohli; vivid colours and bold subjects; portraiture general from the mid-17th century; later naturalism and muted colours.
5. Guler, from the 18th century, Himachal Pradesh: name from "Galwa", cowherd; similarities with Mughal art; strong strokes with fine and sensitive expressions; *Srimad Bhagawata*, *Bihari Satasai*, *Gita Govinda*; well-modelled female figures with upturned nose and minutely done hair.
6. Kangra, from the mid-18th century: born of the migration of Mughal-trained artists to the Kangra region after the decline of the Mughal empire, especially following Nadir Shah's invasion, where they received Rajput patronage - the Guler-Kangra School.
7. Kangra's zenith under Raja Sansar Chand; sensuality and intelligence the other schools lacked; *Gita Govinda*, *Bhagwata Purana*, *Satsai* of Biharilal, *Nal Damayanti*; Krishna love scenes prominent; an other-worldly feel.
8. Twelve months paintings: the effects of the twelve months on human emotions; the emotive style was popular until the 19th century.
9. Kangra became the parent school to the ateliers of Kullu, Chamba and Mandi; the Sansar Chand Museum at Kangra holds prominent Kangra paintings.
10. Jammu or Dogra, from the 17th century: remarkable resemblance to Kangra; the *Shangri Ramayana* of the late 17th and early 18th centuries produced in Jammu itself, on a mythological theme.
11. Kullu, from the 17th century: similar to Basohli; Pahari artists created the *Shangri Ramayana* depicting Bal Kanda, Ayodhya Kanda and Uttar Kanda in dull, dark colours.
12. Mandi, from the 17th century: new style under Raja Sidh Sen, 1684-1727; mostly miniatures; rulers with exaggerated features; Shiva, Rama, Krishna; dark muted colours on dark backgrounds.
13. Disclosed complication: the *Shangri Ramayana* is placed both in Jammu and at Kullu; report both.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trace the formation of the Kangra school as a causal chain, and state what the chain proves about how styles are transmitted.

**Model answer:** The chain has four recorded links. First, imperial contraction: the Mughal empire declined, and the record singles out the period following Nadir Shah's invasion. Second, movement of trained people: many artists trained in the Mughal style migrated to the Kangra region of Himachal Pradesh. Third, a receiving patron: they received patronage from the Rajput Kingdom there. Fourth, a new formation: the migration of artists led to the birth of the Guler-Kangra School, which then reached its zenith under Raja Sansar Chand, with commissions marked by a sensuality and intelligence the other schools were said to lack, subjects drawn from the *Gita Govinda*, *Bhagwata Purana*, the *Satsai* of Biharilal and *Nal Damayanti*, and an other-worldly feel; it went on to become the parent school of the ateliers of Kullu, Chamba and Mandi. What the chain proves is that style is carried by trained individuals rather than by courts or dynasties. Nothing was transferred except people and their training; a political collapse in one place produced an artistic foundation in another; and the new school then generated its own descendants, which shows that transmitted skill becomes locally generative rather than remaining a copy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Kangra is therefore a provincial Mughal style. The record describes a new school with its own subjects, its own emotive Twelve Months series and its own offspring ateliers. Migration supplied the skill; the receiving court supplied the patronage, the subjects and the character.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Pahari painting demonstrates that a regional school is better described as a family of workshops than as a style." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well supported by this school in particular, and the examination should test it against both its internal variety and its shared inheritance.

The family case rests first on scale: Pahari painting spans multiple schools across twenty-two princely states from Jammu to Almora, over the seventeenth to nineteenth centuries. No single style can be distributed across that range, and the evidence duly records poles. Basohli, from the seventeenth century, uses vibrant primary colours and contrasting colours borrowed from Malwa painting, with lotus-shaped eyes and receding hairlines. Mandi, under Raja Sidh Sen between 1684 and 1727, uses dark, muted colours on dark backgrounds and gives rulers exaggerated features. Kullu works in dull, dark colours. Guler, from the eighteenth century, produces delicately modelled female figures with upturned noses and minutely done hair. Kangra, from the mid-eighteenth century, is characterised by an other-worldly feel and by the emotive Twelve Months paintings.

Formation supports the same reading: the Guler-Kangra School was born when Mughal-trained artists migrated to Kangra after the empire's decline and received Rajput patronage, and Kangra then became the parent school to ateliers at Kullu, Chamba and Mandi. Workshops beget workshops.

The qualification is real. Shared subjects recur across the family - *Gita Govinda*, Krishna themes, the *Ramayana* - and Mankot closely resembles Basohli while Jammu bears a remarkable resemblance to Kangra, so resemblance is not absent.

A family of workshops with shared repertoire and divergent handling is therefore the accurate description.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the twenty-two-state span used as the structural argument 3 + five sub-schools each distinguished on a named formal property 7 + the Kangra formation chain plus its parentage of three ateliers 5 + the qualification from shared subjects and recorded resemblances 3 + a final formula that keeps both variety and family likeness 2.**

## Lesson 16 - South Indian miniatures, the card game, and the illustrated manuscript as an object

### Visual: two southern schools from one ancestry, and the object behind them

```text

        |                                 |

     result of the DEFEAT of the      and patronage CONTINUED IN THE
     Thanjavur Nayakas by the         BRITISH PERIOD

     the Vijayanagara empire        TWO OR MORE FIGURES in each painting,
   MARATHA rulers patronised it       with ONE FIGURE PREDOMINATING all
     immensely in the 18TH CENTURY    the others IN SIZE AND COLOUR
   SUPPORT: GLASS and WOODEN        MATERIAL: 'GESSO PASTE' =
     PLANKS (PALAGAI PADAM)           WHITE LEAD POWDER + GAMBOSE + GLUE
     - not cloth and vellum,          acts as a base that helps a SHEEN
     preferred in North India         develop on the background
   brilliant COLOUR PATTERNS +      MUTED colours, not so bright,
     LIBERAL USE OF GOLD LEAF         providing CONTRAST with the background
   GEMSTONES and CUT GLASS to
     embellish LARGER-THAN-LIFE
     images, mostly KRISHNA
   zenith under MAHARAJA SERFOJI II
     of the Maratha dynasty

     Government of India
```
