   HAND PAINTED by artisans; very popular in MUGHAL COURTS; coloured
   background with EACH SUIT A DIFFERENT COLOUR; referenced in the
   BABURNAMA; MYSORE GANJIFA cards received GI STATUS IN 2008
```

*Two schools, one ancestry, and two completely different material solutions to the same problem of making a divine image shine: Tanjore adds gold leaf, gems and cut glass to a hard support; Mysore builds a gesso ground that produces a sheen under muted colour.*

### 1. What makes the southern miniature different

✅ **Fact.** **South Indian miniature paintings, emerging in the early medieval period, differed from northern schools by using gold heavily and focusing on divine figures rather than rulers.**

⚠️ **Two contrasts in one sentence, and both are examinable.** A **material** contrast - heavy gold - and a **subject** contrast - divinity rather than rulership. Set against the Mughal album page of Lesson 12, whose signature achievement is individual portraiture of a ruler, the southern schools are doing something structurally opposite: not the particular person observed, but the deity made radiant.

✅ **Fact - the shared ancestry.** **Both Tanjore and Mysore paintings have originated from the same source - Vijayanagara paintings to begin with and Nayaka paintings subsequently.**

⚠️ **This is a genuine link back to Lesson 7 and should be used.** The mural programmes of Vijayanagara and the Nayakas are recorded as the source of two later **miniature** schools. Format changes from wall to plank and glass; the lineage of imagery does not.

### 2. Tanjore

✅ **Fact - origin and patronage.** **The Thanjavur or Tanjore School is famous for its special style of decorative paintings.** It **probably developed as the result of the defeat of the Thanjavur Nayakas by the Marathas and the fall of the Vijayanagara empire.** **The Maratha rulers patronised this art form immensely during the 18th century.**

✅ **Fact - support and material.** The paintings are **unique as they are mostly created on glass and wooden planks (*Palagai padam*) instead of cloth and vellum, which was preferred in North India.** They are **unique because of the use of brilliant colour patterns and the liberal use of gold leaf.**

✅ **Fact - embellishment, subject and peak.** **Thanjavur artists used gemstones and cut glass to embellish larger-than-life images, mostly depicting Krishna in various poses and key life events.** These paintings **reached their zenith under the patronage of Maharaja Serfoji II of the Maratha dynasty, who was a great patron of art**.

✅ **Fact - recognition.** **This art form has been awarded geographical indication (GI) status by the Government of India.**

⚠️ **The one word that repays attention: "probably".** The origin explanation - defeat of the Thanjavur Nayakas by the Marathas and the fall of Vijayanagara - is hedged in the record. Preserve the hedge.

⚠️ **The support is the discriminator.** **Glass and wooden planks**, with the Tamil term ***Palagai padam***, explicitly contrasted with the **cloth and vellum** of the north. If an item tests what distinguishes Tanjore painting, support and gold leaf are the two answers. ⚠️ Note also the phrase **larger-than-life images**: a "miniature" school whose images are larger than life is a useful reminder that the southern group is classified by tradition and lineage rather than by the twenty-five-square-inch rule.

### 3. Mysore

✅ **Fact - patronage and theme.** **These paintings were patronised by the rulers of the Mysore province and patronage continued in the British period as well.** **The major theme of the Mysore paintings is the depiction of Hindu gods and goddesses.**

✅ **Fact - the composition rule.** **The unique part of these paintings is that they had two or more figures in each painting and one figure predominated all the others in size and colour.**

✅ **Fact - the material.** **They use the "gesso paste", which is a mixture of white lead powder, gambose and glue.** **This paste acts as a unique base to the painting that helps a sheen to be developed on the background.** **The use of muted colours that are not so bright provides a contrast with the background.**

⚠️ **The Mysore composition rule is an unusually precise, testable statement.** Two or more figures, one dominating in **size and colour** - that is a compositional hierarchy, and it encodes theology: the principal deity is made principal by scale and chroma rather than by placement or gesture. It is the southern counterpart of the attribute system used elsewhere in this topic.

⚠️ **Keep gesso and gold apart.** **Tanjore** is gold leaf, gemstones and cut glass on glass or wooden planks. **Mysore** is a gesso ground of white lead powder, gambose and glue producing a sheen, worked in muted colours. Both are "shine" strategies; the materials are entirely different, and a close-option item will exploit any confusion.

⚠️ **The British-period continuation is worth one line.** Mysore patronage **continued into the British period**, which makes it one of the few court traditions in this topic with documented survival across the colonial transition - a useful counterweight to the assumption that colonial rule simply displaced court painting.

### 4. Ganjifa

✅ **Fact.** **Ganjifa is a medieval period card game.** The **cards were traditionally hand painted by artisans and were very popular in Mughal courts.** The **cards have a coloured background, with each suit having a different colour.** **The reference to Ganjifa cards can even be found in the book *Baburnama*.** **Mysore Ganjifa Cards or Paintings have received the GI status in 2008.**

⚠️ **Ganjifa is the topic's only painted object whose purpose is play**, and that is exactly why it is worth naming. It demonstrates the range of the painted object in this tradition: wall, manuscript folio, album page, glass panel, temple hanging - and a deck of cards. ⚠️ The *Baburnama* reference is a dated textual anchor; the **2008** GI is a dated institutional one. ⚠️ Any current registry position should be verified against the geographical-indication authority rather than assumed from this session.

### 5. Illustrated manuscripts as an object type

✅ **Fact - the definition.** **Illustrated manuscripts are pictorial translations of epic or literary verses, with text in a demarcated space.**

✅ **Fact - their social use.** **Considered precious and valuable artefacts, the illustrated manuscripts were often gifted to princesses as part of their dowries when they got married.**

✅ **Fact - the origin.** **The tradition began with the Pala rulers of Bengal and Bihar, notably under King Ramapala (11th century), on palm leaves.**

✅ **Fact - the Jain practice.** **Under Jainism, *shaastradaan* - donation of books - involved gifting illustrated manuscripts to monastery *bhandars*.** **Early works were on palm leaves, later on paper (14th century), richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli, depicting *Tirthipatas*, mandalas and secular stories.**

✅ **Fact - the Mughal continuation.** **During medieval times, every Mughal successor somehow contributed towards enhancing the status of illustrated manuscripts.**

✅ **Fact - named manuscripts, as far as the record is legible.**

| Manuscript | Tradition and period |
|---|---|
| ***Kalpasutra*** | **Jainism, Svetambara sect** |
| ***Kalakacharyakatha*** | **Jainism, Svetambara sect** |
| ***Sangrahini Sutra*** | **In Jainism - written in the 12th century** |
| ***Uttaradhyana Sutra*** | **Jainism** |

❌ **A disclosed evidence boundary.** In the available record the **details column of this manuscript table is clipped**, so only partial descriptions survive - for the *Kalpasutra*, that it deals with events relating to the Tirthankaras; for the *Kalakacharyakatha*, that it depicts a story involving an abduction and rescue; for the *Sangrahini Sutra*, that it depicts a conception of the universe and a mapping of space. **Nothing beyond what is legible is supplied here, and no clipped description is completed from memory.** Name the manuscript, its tradition and, where given, its century; stop there.

⚠️ **Two observations that are worth more than the table.** First, the **dowry** use: an illustrated manuscript circulating as marriage property is evidence that painting had **transferable economic value** independent of its patron. Second, ***shaastradaan***: donating books to monastery *bhandars* is a **religious merit** mechanism that systematically produced and preserved illustrated manuscripts - which is a large part of why Jain manuscript painting survives as well as it does. Survival, once again, has an institutional explanation.

⚠️ **The material chain to hold.** Palm leaf under the Pala rulers from the eleventh century, to **paper in the fourteenth century** in the Jain tradition, **richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli**. That is the same palm-leaf-to-paper transition met in Lesson 9, now dated and with its new luxury materials named. Lapis lazuli reappears here, having first appeared as Ajanta's blue in Lesson 3.

### Examination use

This lesson carries four separable examinable blocks. Tanjore: Maratha patronage in the eighteenth century, glass and wooden planks called *Palagai padam*, gold leaf, gemstones and cut glass, larger-than-life Krishna images, Serfoji II, and GI status. Mysore: rulers of Mysore with patronage continuing into the British period, Hindu gods and goddesses, the two-or-more-figures rule with one predominating in size and colour, and the gesso paste of white lead powder, gambose and glue producing a sheen under muted colours. Ganjifa: a medieval card game, hand painted, popular in Mughal courts, each suit a different colour, referenced in the *Baburnama*, with Mysore Ganjifa receiving GI status in 2008. Illustrated manuscripts: the definition, the dowry use, the Pala origin under Ramapala in the eleventh century, Jain *shaastradaan*, the fourteenth-century move to paper with gold and lapis lazuli, and the four named manuscripts with their disclosed clipped details.

### Traps introduced here

- Swapping the two materials: gesso paste belongs to Mysore, gold leaf and gemstones to Tanjore.
- Giving Tanjore a cloth or vellum support; it is glass and wooden planks, *Palagai padam*.
- Dropping the record's "probably" from the Tanjore origin explanation.
- Forgetting that Mysore patronage continued into the British period.
- Stating the Mysore composition rule as one figure only; it is two or more figures with one predominating in size and colour.
- Dating the Mysore Ganjifa GI to any year other than 2008, or asserting a current registry position from this session.
- Completing a clipped manuscript description from memory.
- Attributing the illustrated-manuscript tradition's origin to the Mughals; it began with the Pala rulers of Bengal and Bihar under Ramapala in the eleventh century.

### Revision notes

1. South Indian miniatures emerged in the early medieval period and differ from northern schools by using gold heavily and focusing on divine figures rather than rulers.
2. Both Tanjore and Mysore originate from the same source: Vijayanagara paintings first, Nayaka paintings subsequently.
3. Tanjore: famous for decorative paintings; probably developed from the defeat of the Thanjavur Nayakas by the Marathas and the fall of Vijayanagara; Maratha rulers patronised it immensely in the 18th century.
4. Tanjore support and materials: glass and wooden planks, *Palagai padam*, instead of the cloth and vellum preferred in North India; brilliant colour patterns; liberal use of gold leaf; gemstones and cut glass embellishing larger-than-life images, mostly Krishna; zenith under Maharaja Serfoji II; GI status awarded by the Government of India.
5. Mysore: patronised by the rulers of Mysore, continuing in the British period; theme is Hindu gods and goddesses; two or more figures per painting with one predominating in size and colour.
6. Mysore material: gesso paste of white lead powder, gambose and glue, forming a base that helps a sheen develop on the background; muted colours provide contrast with the background.
7. Ganjifa: medieval card game; hand painted by artisans; very popular in Mughal courts; coloured background with each suit a different colour; referenced in the *Baburnama*; Mysore Ganjifa cards received GI status in 2008.
8. Illustrated manuscripts: pictorial translations of epic or literary verses with text in a demarcated space; precious artefacts often gifted to princesses as dowry.
9. The tradition began with the Pala rulers of Bengal and Bihar, notably under King Ramapala in the 11th century, on palm leaves.
10. Jain *shaastradaan*, the donation of books, involved gifting illustrated manuscripts to monastery *bhandars*; early works on palm leaves, later on paper in the 14th century, richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli, depicting *Tirthipatas*, mandalas and secular stories.
11. Every Mughal successor contributed towards enhancing the status of illustrated manuscripts.
12. Named manuscripts: *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharyakatha*, Jainism, Svetambara sect; *Sangrahini Sutra*, Jainism, written in the 12th century; *Uttaradhyana Sutra*, Jainism. Details are partly clipped in the available record and are not completed here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Tanjore and Mysore share an ancestry and a purpose but solve the problem of radiance in opposite ways. Set out both solutions and explain what the difference shows.

**Model answer:** Both schools descend from the same source - Vijayanagara painting first and Nayaka painting subsequently - and both serve the southern preference for divine figures rendered with heavy use of gold rather than for rulers. Tanjore's solution is additive and hard. The image is made on glass or wooden planks, called *Palagai padam*, rather than on the cloth and vellum preferred in the north; brilliant colour patterns are combined with a liberal use of gold leaf; and gemstones and cut glass are applied to embellish larger-than-life images, mostly of Krishna. Radiance is therefore produced by attaching reflective material to the surface. Mysore's solution is subtractive and optical. A gesso paste of white lead powder, gambose and glue is laid as a base, which helps a sheen to develop on the background, and the figures are then worked in muted colours that are deliberately not bright, so that the restrained colour contrasts with the luminous ground. Radiance is produced by preparing the ground and then holding the colour back. The difference shows that a shared iconographic purpose does not dictate a shared technique: two workshops with the same ancestry and the same devotional aim arrived at opposite material strategies, which is why material description, not subject description, identifies a school.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating gold as a single feature of southern painting. Gold leaf physically applied at Tanjore and a white-lead gesso sheen at Mysore are different materials doing similar visual work, and an identification item will offer one as a distractor for the other.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The illustrated manuscript should be studied as a social object, not only as a painted one." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is sound, and the evidence for it is largely non-artistic.

An illustrated manuscript is defined as a pictorial translation of epic or literary verses with text in a demarcated space - an aesthetic description. But the record then describes its circulation. Such manuscripts were considered precious and valuable artefacts and were often gifted to princesses as part of their dowries, which makes them transferable property carrying value independent of their patron. Under Jainism, *shaastradaan*, the donation of books, involved gifting illustrated manuscripts to monastery *bhandars*, which is a merit-earning act that simultaneously created demand and built repositories - a substantial part of the explanation for why Jain manuscript painting survives comparatively well.

Patronage completes the picture: the tradition begins with the Pala rulers of Bengal and Bihar under Ramapala in the eleventh century, and every Mughal successor contributed to enhancing the status of illustrated manuscripts.

Painted analysis still identifies the work; social analysis explains its production, circulation and survival.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the dowry circulation read as transferable value 3 + *shaastradaan* explained as a demand-and-preservation mechanism 4 + the patronage span from Ramapala to the Mughal successors 2 + a verdict that assigns identification to one method and explanation to the other 1.**

                    |  <---- REJECTED by the next position
                    v
 3. BENGAL SCHOOL OF ART
    "supposed to have arisen as a REACTION AGAINST THE EXISTING STYLES"
    ABANINDRANATH TAGORE, early 20th century
      ARABIAN NIGHTS series - a mark on a global scale
      tried to incorporate SWADESHI values in Indian art and to REDUCE
        the influence of WESTERN MATERIALISTIC style among artists
      BHARAT MATA (1905); various Mughal-influenced paintings
    "The Bengal school painters REJECTED the art of Raja Ravi Verma as
      they considered it IMITATIVE AND WESTERNISED"
                    |
                    v
 4. AFTER 1947: no single style
    CUBIST current -> M.F. Hussain, the HORSE motif for fluidity of motion
    PROGRESSIVE ARTISTS GROUP, formed after 1947, SIX founding members,
      bold progressive themes with softer abstract ones, drawing on
      European Modernism WITHOUT A UNIFORM STYLE
```

*Modern Indian painting is a sequence of rejections. Each position is best defined by what it refused: Company painting refused nothing and served its patron; Ravi Varma refused the miniature idiom; the Bengal School refused both Company hybridity and Ravi Varma's academic realism; and the post-1947 group refused the obligation to have a single style at all.*

### 1. Company and Bazaar painting

✅ **Fact - the frame.** **Modern Indian Painting blended Indian art with European styles and themes. It gave rise to Company Painting and Bazaar Painting.**

✅ **Fact - the comparison, as far as the record is legible.**

| Feature | **Company Painting (*Kampani Kalam*)** | **Bazaar Painting** |
|---|---|---|
| **Period** | **Colonial period** | **Colonial period** |
| **Style** | **Hybrid of Rajput, Mughal and other Indian styles with European elements** | **Influenced by European and Roman elements; minimal Indian content** |
| **Origin** | **Kolkata, Chennai, Delhi, Patna, Varanasi, Thanjavur** | **Bengal and Bihar regions** |
| **Patrons** | **British East India Company officers, Mary Impey, Marquess Wellesley** | **British officials and patrons** |

❌ **A disclosed evidence boundary.** In the available record the right-hand column of this comparison is **clipped**, so the Bazaar entries for style and patrons survive only partially. What is legible is given above; **nothing further is supplied, and no clipped cell is completed from memory.**

⚠️ **The named patrons are the detail worth carrying.** **Mary Impey** and **Marquess Wellesley** are named individual patrons of Company painting, and naming them converts a general statement about colonial patronage into evidence. ⚠️ Note the geography too: Company painting is recorded at **six named centres** across the subcontinent, which makes it a genuinely distributed phenomenon rather than a Calcutta speciality.

⚠️ **The distinction that matters.** Company painting is a **hybrid with a substantial Indian component** - Rajput, Mughal and other Indian styles with European elements added. Bazaar painting, by contrast, is recorded as having **minimal Indian content** under European and Roman influence. That is the opposite of the intuitive assumption, in which a "bazaar" art would be more indigenous than a "Company" one, and it is exactly the kind of counter-intuitive fact an objective item is built on.

### 2. Raja Ravi Varma

✅ **Fact.** **Raja Ravi Varma (1848-1906) was one of India's greatest painters.** He was **unique as he brought together elements of South Indian painting with the Western techniques of colour and style.** He **belonged to the state of Kerala and is known as the "Father of Modern Indian Art".** Some of his extremely famous works include ***Ladies in the Moonlight*, *Shakuntala*, *Damayanti and Swan***. He **gained nationwide recognition for his paintings from the epic Ramayana, especially the one titled "*Ravana kidnapping Sita and killing Jatayu*".** **A film, "*Rang Rasiya*", has been made on him.**

⚠️ **The composite description is the point.** South Indian painting **plus** Western technique of colour and style, applied to **epic subjects**, for a **nationwide** audience. Each element matters: without the epic subject there is no national recognition; without the Western technique there is no break from the miniature idiom; without the southern element the synthesis claim collapses. ❌ Do not attach a medium, print process or circulation figure to him from memory; the record supplies the works and the recognition, not the mechanism.

### 3. The Bengal School: origin, features and the double rejection

✅ **Fact - origin.** **The Bengal School is supposed to have arisen as a reaction against the existing styles of painting**, in **the works of Abanindranath Tagore in the early 20th century**. **His *Arabian Nights* series made a mark on a global scale.** **He tried to incorporate Swadeshi values in Indian art and tried to reduce the influence of Western materialistic style among artists.** **He is known for his painting *Bharat Mata* (1905) and various Mughal-influenced paintings.**

✅ **Fact - the eight recorded features.**

| Feature | What is recorded |
|---|---|
| **Based on Indian traditions** | The Indian mentality is demonstrated by paintings with **Indian themes, such as Mahakali, Shiva-Parvati, Krishna and Gopis** |
| **Influence of Ajanta paintings** | Draws inspiration from **Ajanta art**; exhibits Ajanta art's **rhythm, grace, harmony** and other characteristics |
| **Linear delicacy** | The Ajanta paintings are **mirrored in the Bengal School's lines**; the lines have **a gentle rhythm** |
| **Softness and rhythm in figures** | **No hardness** in the figures, giving a delicate impression, with a pleasing rhythm |
| **Gorgeous colour scheme** | A **wash technique**, with **subdued colours**, not overly vibrant |
| **Impact of Mughal and Rajasthani schools** | In certain locations, the effect of **Mughal and Rajasthani** schools is also evident |
| **Light and shade** | The paintings are gentle because of the **exquisite use of light and shade** |
| **Impressive and Indian subject matter** | Remarkably impressive and distinctly Indian; **literary, religious, historical and other themes** are employed |

✅ **Fact - the rejection.** **The Bengal school painters rejected the art of Raja Ravi Verma as they considered it imitative and westernised.**

⚠️ **Why the eight features are worth learning as eight.** A question asking what the Bengal School was can be answered with "a nationalist style" - which earns little - or with **eight named criteria including a specific technique**. The **wash technique** with subdued colour is the single most concrete of them, and the **Ajanta inspiration** is the most argumentatively useful, because it makes the movement's claim to Indianness rest on a **specific historical source** rather than on assertion.

⚠️ **The two-front argument, which is the heart of any Bengal School answer.** The school reacted against **the existing styles of painting**, and it explicitly rejected **Ravi Varma** as imitative and westernised. So it stood against colonial-period hybridity **and** against academic realism at the same time. That is why technique became a political statement: choosing wash over oil, and Ajanta over the academy, was a choice about what Indian art should be.

⚠️ **A necessary qualification.** The Bengal School's critique should not be read as a total rejection of all European or pan-Asian exchange; **Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks also shaped it**. ⚠️ Carry this as an analytical qualification, and do not turn it into a claim about specific artists or works.

### 4. The other Bengal School painters

✅ **Fact - Nandalal Bose.** **The other notable painter of this school was Nandalal Bose, whose works led to further development of Modern Indian Art.** He was **associated with Santiniketan**. He is **known for his white-on-black Gandhi sketch of the Dandi March, which achieved iconic status during the 1930s**. He was **entrusted with the task of illustrating the original document of the Constitution of India**, and **with sketching the emblems of the civilian awards Bharat Ratna and Padma awards**. **The painting of *Shiva and Sati* by Nandalal Bose is also very famous.**

✅ **Fact - Rabindranath Tagore.** **Another very famous painter of this school was Rabindranath Tagore.** **His paintings were unique as they used dominant black lines that made the subject look prominent. He made small sized paintings.** **Some art historians argue that his paintings can be linked to his writings.**

⚠️ Report the writing link as **a scholarly reading**, which is how the record frames it - "some art historians argue" - and not as an established fact.

✅ **Fact - the others.** **Other famous painters of the Bengal school were Asit Kumar Haldar, Manishi Dey, Mukul Dey and Sunayani Devi.**

✅ **Fact - named work-artist pairs, as far as the record is legible.**

| Painting | Artist |
|---|---|
| ***Journey's End*** | **Abanindranath Tagore** |
| ***Tiller of the Soil (Indian Farmer)*** | **Nandalal Bose** |

❌ **A disclosed evidence boundary.** The remaining rows of this table are **clipped in the available record**: *Rasa-Lila* is attributed to an artist whose name begins "Kshitinda", *Meghdoot* to one beginning "Ram Gop", and *Radhika* to one beginning "M.A.R.Ch". **No partial artist name is completed here from memory, and no work is paired with an artist beyond the two legible rows above.** This is the single sharpest instance in this session of the prohibition on unsupported artist-work attribution.

⚠️ **Why Nandalal Bose is the strongest single figure for an answer about art and the nation.** The Dandi March sketch is a political image; the illustration of the original Constitution document and the sketching of the Bharat Ratna and Padma emblems place an artist inside the **iconography of the state itself**. That is a claim about art's institutional role, not merely about its subject matter.

### 5. Sister Nivedita

✅ **Fact.** **The Irish-born educationist, author, social activist and thinker Margaret Elizabeth Noble (1867-1911), also known as Sister Nivedita**, was a **significant contributor to the field of women's education and empowerment**; she **promoted science and art**, but is **most remembered for awakening national consciousness among the people of India**. She **not only endorsed, encouraged and appreciated Indian art but also plunged herself into discovering and reconstructing Indianness in Indian art, thus making art an essential tool in awakening and invigorating the national consciousness of India.** She **believed that the rebirth of "Indianness" in art was essential for the reawakening of the motherland**, and **held prolonged discussions and active interactions with young students to desist from the existing practice of aping Western art and to strive to establish the idea of Indian art with its indigenous roots as the binding force.** **The young artists of the Bengal School of Art were also mentored by Sister Nivedita.**

✅ **Fact - her recorded assessment of *Bharat Mata*.** She said of Abanindranath Tagore's painting: **"From beginning to end, the Bharat Mata picture is an appeal, in the Indian language, to the Indian heart. It is the first great masterpiece in a new style. I would reprint it, if I could, by tens of thousands, and scatter it broadcast over the land, till there was not a peasant's cottage, or a craftman's hut, between Kedar Nath and Cape Comorin, that had not this presentment of Bharat-Mata somewhere on its walls. Over and over again, as one looks into its qualities, one is struck by the purity and delicacy of the personality portrayed".**

⚠️ **Use the quotation as her recorded statement**, attributed to her, and not as a neutral art-historical verdict. ⚠️ **Why she matters structurally.** A "school" is usually described through its painters. Sister Nivedita evidences the **intellectual and institutional support network** around the Bengal School - mentorship, advocacy and a programme for what the art was for. ⚠️ Note also what her wish reveals: she wants the image **reprinted by tens of thousands** and scattered across the land. A nationalist painting aspires to **mass circulation**, which quietly concedes the very print public that Ravi Varma's reputation rested on.

### 6. After 1947

✅ **Fact - the Cubist current.** **Indian Cubist painting, inspired by European Cubism, broke down and reassembled objects on canvas using abstract forms, striving for a balance between line and colour.** **One of the most popular Cubist artists in India was M.F. Hussain**, who **used the motif of a horse frequently as an effective way to depict the fluidity of motion**.

✅ **Fact - the Progressive Artists Group.** **After 1947, a group of painters gained attention for blending bold, progressive themes with softer, abstract ones, drawing inspiration from European Modernism without a uniform style.** **This group was formed by six founding members - F.N. Souza, S.H. Raza, M.F. Hussain, K.H. Ara, H.A. Gade and S.K. Bakre.**

> 🔑 Six founders: **S-R-H-A-G-B** - *Souza, Raza, Hussain, Ara, Gade, Bakre*.

⚠️ **The phrase "without a uniform style" is the content, not a qualification.** Every earlier position in this lesson is defined by a shared manner - Company hybridity, Ravi Varma's academic realism, the Bengal School's wash and line. The post-1947 group is defined by **the absence of one**, which is a genuine break: the obligation to paint in a recognisably Indian manner is set aside, and modernism is absorbed rather than imitated. ❌ Do not attach awards, dates beyond "after 1947", individual works or a manifesto to the group from memory.

### Examination use

Modern painting is best prepared as a sequence of positions, each defined by its rejection. Secure recall: the Company-Bazaar comparison with its six Company centres and the named patrons Mary Impey and Marquess Wellesley, with the clipped Bazaar cells disclosed; Ravi Varma's dates, Kerala origin, epithet, four named works and the film; the Bengal School's origin in Abanindranath Tagore, the *Arabian Nights* series, *Bharat Mata* of 1905, the eight recorded features including the wash technique and the Ajanta inspiration, and the explicit rejection of Ravi Varma as imitative and westernised; Nandalal Bose's Dandi March sketch, the Constitution illustration and the Bharat Ratna and Padma emblems; Rabindranath Tagore's dominant black lines and small format; the four further named painters; Sister Nivedita's dates, role and recorded assessment; the Cubist current with Hussain's horse motif; and the Progressive Artists Group's six founders and its deliberate absence of a uniform style.

### Traps introduced here

- Assuming Bazaar painting is the more indigenous of the two colonial hybrids; the record gives it minimal Indian content.
- Completing a clipped cell in the Company-Bazaar table or a clipped artist name in the paintings table.
- Calling Ravi Varma a Bengal School precursor; the school explicitly rejected his art as imitative and westernised.
- Reducing the Bengal School to "traditional Indian painting" instead of naming the wash technique, the Ajanta inspiration and the two-front rejection.
- Presenting the Bengal School as a total rejection of all external exchange, when Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks also shaped it.
- Treating the link between Rabindranath Tagore's paintings and his writings as established; it is what some art historians argue.
- Quoting Sister Nivedita's assessment as a neutral verdict rather than as her recorded statement.
- Assigning a uniform style, a manifesto or awards to the Progressive Artists Group.

### Revision notes

1. Modern Indian Painting blended Indian art with European styles and themes, giving rise to Company Painting and Bazaar Painting.
2. Company Painting, *Kampani Kalam*: colonial period; hybrid of Rajput, Mughal and other Indian styles with European elements; centres at Kolkata, Chennai, Delhi, Patna, Varanasi, Thanjavur; patrons British East India Company officers, Mary Impey, Marquess Wellesley.
3. Bazaar Painting: colonial period; influenced by European and Roman elements with minimal Indian content; from the Bengal and Bihar regions; British officials and patrons. Remaining cells are clipped in the record and are not completed.
4. Raja Ravi Varma, 1848-1906: Kerala; "Father of Modern Indian Art"; combined South Indian painting with Western techniques of colour and style; *Ladies in the Moonlight*, *Shakuntala*, *Damayanti and Swan*; nationwide recognition for *Ravana kidnapping Sita and killing Jatayu*; the film *Rang Rasiya*.
5. Bengal School: supposed to have arisen as a reaction against the existing styles of painting, in the works of Abanindranath Tagore in the early 20th century; *Arabian Nights* series; Swadeshi values; reduction of Western materialistic influence; *Bharat Mata*, 1905; Mughal-influenced paintings.
6. The eight features: Indian traditions and themes such as Mahakali, Shiva-Parvati, Krishna and Gopis; influence of Ajanta with its rhythm, grace and harmony; linear delicacy mirroring Ajanta lines; softness and rhythm in figures; a wash technique with subdued colours; impact of Mughal and Rajasthani schools; exquisite use of light and shade; impressive and distinctly Indian literary, religious and historical subject matter.
7. The Bengal School painters rejected Raja Ravi Varma's art as imitative and westernised - the two-front position, against colonial hybridity and against academic realism.
8. Qualification: the school was not a total rejection of external exchange; Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks also shaped it.
9. Nandalal Bose: Santiniketan; white-on-black Gandhi sketch of the Dandi March, iconic in the 1930s; illustrated the original document of the Constitution of India; sketched the emblems of the Bharat Ratna and Padma awards; *Shiva and Sati*.
10. Rabindranath Tagore: dominant black lines making the subject prominent; small-sized paintings; some art historians argue a link with his writings.
11. Other Bengal School painters: Asit Kumar Haldar, Manishi Dey, Mukul Dey, Sunayani Devi.
12. Legible work-artist pairs: *Journey's End* - Abanindranath Tagore; *Tiller of the Soil (Indian Farmer)* - Nandalal Bose. Other rows are clipped and are not completed.
13. Sister Nivedita, Margaret Elizabeth Noble, 1867-1911: Irish-born educationist, author, social activist and thinker; women's education and empowerment; promoted science and art; awakened national consciousness; mentored young Bengal School artists; urged them away from aping Western art towards indigenous roots; her recorded assessment of *Bharat Mata* as "the first great masterpiece in a new style".
14. Indian Cubist painting: inspired by European Cubism; broke down and reassembled objects using abstract forms; balance between line and colour; M.F. Hussain's frequent horse motif for fluidity of motion.
15. Progressive Artists Group: formed after 1947; six founders - F.N. Souza, S.H. Raza, M.F. Hussain, K.H. Ara, H.A. Gade, S.K. Bakre; bold progressive themes with softer abstract ones; European Modernism without a uniform style.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Bengal School's choice of technique was itself a political act, and identify the qualification that prevents this from being overstated.

**Model answer:** Because the available techniques each carried an allegiance, and choosing one meant refusing the others. The school arose as a reaction against the existing styles of painting, and its painters explicitly rejected Raja Ravi Varma's art as imitative and westernised. Against his academic handling it adopted a wash technique with subdued, not overly vibrant, colours; a linear delicacy that mirrored the lines of Ajanta and carried a gentle rhythm; softness in the figures with no hardness; and an exquisite use of light and shade. Its acknowledged source was Ajanta, whose rhythm, grace and harmony it sought to exhibit - that is, a historical Indian tradition rather than a European academy. Abanindranath Tagore's stated purpose completes the case: he tried to incorporate Swadeshi values in Indian art and to reduce the influence of the Western materialistic style among artists, and *Bharat Mata* of 1905 is the emblematic work. Method, source and purpose therefore align, which is what makes technique political. The qualification is that this was not a total rejection of external exchange: Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks also shaped the movement, and the school's own repertoire shows the effect of Mughal and Rajasthani painting, with Abanindranath himself producing Mughal-influenced work.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Bengal School as a return to an authentic uncontaminated Indian manner. Its own eight recorded features include Mughal and Rajasthani influence, its inspiration is a specific historical site rather than an unbroken living tradition, and its wash method has a transnational dimension. The claim it makes is programmatic, not archaeological.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Modern Indian painting is best written as a sequence of refusals." Examine, from Company painting to the Progressive Artists Group. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The formulation organises the material better than any style-based account, and it can be tested position by position.

Company painting refuses nothing: it is a colonial-period hybrid of Rajput, Mughal and other Indian styles with European elements, made at Kolkata, Chennai, Delhi, Patna, Varanasi and Thanjavur for East India Company officers and named patrons such as Mary Impey and Marquess Wellesley. Bazaar painting, influenced by European and Roman elements with minimal Indian content, goes further in the same direction.

Ravi Varma, 1848-1906, refuses the inherited idiom, bringing together South Indian painting with Western techniques of colour and style and winning nationwide recognition for epic subjects such as *Ravana kidnapping Sita and killing Jatayu*.

The Bengal School refuses both. It arose as a reaction against the existing styles of painting, and its painters rejected Ravi Varma's art as imitative and westernised, adopting instead a wash technique with subdued colours, a linear delicacy mirroring Ajanta, and Swadeshi purpose, with *Bharat Mata* of 1905 as its emblem.

After 1947 the refusal turns inward: the Progressive Artists Group, founded by Souza, Raza, Hussain, Ara, Gade and Bakre, blends bold progressive themes with softer abstract ones, drawing on European Modernism explicitly without a uniform style - a refusal of the obligation to look Indian at all.

The limit is that each refusal retained what it rejected: the Bengal School shows Mughal and Rajasthani influence, and Indian Cubism absorbs a European movement.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the Company-Bazaar position with centres and named patrons 4 + Ravi Varma's specific refusal with one named work 3 + the Bengal School's double refusal with at least three of its eight features 6 + the post-1947 refusal of style-obligation with the six founders 4 + the limit that each refusal retains what it rejects, evidenced twice 3.**

## Lesson 18 - Folk and tribal painting as living production systems

Progress: 18/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: seventeen named traditions by region, support, material, community and function - and the discipline of not merging them

                                                             life after death
