*Sorting by support rather than by state is the only arrangement that makes these seventeen traditions memorable, because support determines technique, and technique is what an identification item tests.*

### 1. The discipline this lesson enforces

⚠️ **The one rule.** Folk and tribal painting is **not a homogeneous category**. Each named tradition has a distinct **region**, **community**, **support**, **material** and **function**, and treating them as interchangeable loses exactly the specificity an examiner is testing.

⚠️ **The second rule, which is about description rather than fact.** These are **living traditions**, not survivals. Several of the records below describe a tradition **changing** - Madhubani moving onto paper and cloth, Patua moving to poster colours on stitched paper and to social and political themes, Warli done today on cloth with white poster colours, Saura designs now popular in modern clothing. ❌ Do not describe any of them as frozen or as relics; ✅ describe them as production systems with a material, a community, an occasion and, in several cases, a market.

### 2. The traditions, region by region

✅ **Fact - Pichhwai.** **Pichhwai, a traditional art from Rajasthan (mainly Nathdwara), depicts tales of Lord Krishna (Sreenath ji) under Vaishnavism.** **Originally made for temple hangings in the Pushtimarg Sampradaya, it is now a major export of Nathdwara.** **Vallabhacharya, founder of the Pushtimarg Sampradaya, a subtradition of Vaishnavism, is credited with the introduction of the Pichhwai art form in the 16th century.**

⚠️ **Pichhwai is the model case of the craft-ecology chain** - ritual origin, temple use, export market - and it is the best single example to cite when a question invites discussion of living heritage and its economy.

✅ **Fact - Madhubani.** **Traditionally done by the women of villages around Madhubani town (Bihar)**, these are **also called Mithila Paintings**. **Mentioned in the Ramayana**, Madhubani paintings **commonly depict Hindu deities like Krishna, Rama, Durga, Lakshmi and Shiva, with symbolic motifs such as fish for luck and fertility.** **Madhubani paintings, now done on paper or cloth, depict auspicious occasions with bold colours, floral patterns, double borders, and two-dimensional figures.** The tradition **is believed to have begun in the Ramayana era during Sita and Rama's marriage.** **Passed down mainly by women, it gained recognition in 1970 when Jagdamba Devi was honoured.** **Other famous painters associated with this art form are Baua Devi, Bharti Dayal, Ganga Devi, Mahasundari Devi and Sita Devi.** **Since the art has remained confined to a specific geographical area, it has been given GI status.**

⚠️ Preserve the record's hedges: the Ramayana-era origin is **believed**, and the Ramayana mention is a textual claim. ⚠️ The **1970** recognition of **Jagdamba Devi** is the dated institutional fact, and the five further named women painters are the specificity that distinguishes a prepared answer. ⚠️ Note the stated **reason** for the GI: confinement to a specific geographical area.

✅ **Fact - Tikuli.** **Tikuli art of Bihar, derived from the word *tikuli* (bindi), is painted on hardboard cut into shapes and polished with enamel coats.** **It often features Madhubani motifs.**

⚠️ Tikuli is the easiest of the Bihar pair to lose. Its discriminators are the **hardboard support** and the **enamel polish**, not its motifs, which it shares with Madhubani.

✅ **Fact - Pattachitra.** **Pattachitra, a traditional Odisha painting, combines classical and folk elements.** **The name comes from Sanskrit: *patta* (canvas) and *chitra* (picture).** **The base of the painting is treated cloth**, while **natural colours like burnt coconut shell and lamp black are used**. **Outlines are drawn with a brush in red or yellow, filled with colour, decorated with foliage and flowers, framed intricately, and finished with lacquer for gloss.** Themes are **inspired from the Jagannath and Vaishnava sects, and sometimes from the Shakti and Shaiva sects as well.** **Raghurajpur in Odisha is known for this art form.** Pattachitra paintings **depict images similar to those of the old murals of the State, especially the ones in Puri and Konark.** **Pattachitra on palm leaf is known as *talapattachitra*.**

⚠️ **The sequence is the answer.** Treated cloth, brush outline in red or yellow, colour fill, foliage-and-flower decoration, intricate framing, lacquer gloss. A five-step process description beats any adjective, and *talapattachitra* is the palm-leaf variant term worth carrying.

✅ **Fact - Patua.** **Patua art of Bengal, over a thousand years old, features scroll paintings depicting *Mangal Kavyas* - auspicious stories of Hindu deities.** **Traditionally, Muslim Patuas travel between villages singing these stories.** **Traditionally on cloth showing religious stories, Patua paintings are now done with poster colours on stitched paper to depict social and political themes.** **They mainly come from Medinipur, Murshidabad, South 24 Parganas and Birbhum.**

⚠️ **Patua is the topic's clearest case of a tradition changing on every axis at once** - support from cloth to stitched paper, medium to poster colours, subject from religious to social and political - while its performance practice, travelling and singing, persists. ⚠️ Note the recorded detail that the Patuas are **Muslim** painters of **Hindu deities**; it is a fact about the community and should be stated plainly, without elaboration.

✅ **Fact - Kalamkari.** **Kalamkari painting, named after the *kalam* (pen) used, is done on treated cotton fabric that is bleached and softened.** **Artists use a bamboo pen, sharpened and wrapped with cloth to control the flow of colours.** **Black outlines, made from a special mixture, are drawn first and then filled with vegetable dyes.** **Kalamkari, from Srikalahasti and Machilipatnam (Andhra Pradesh), includes freehand Hindu mythological paintings and Persian-influenced block-printed designs.** **It dates to the Vijayanagara era and has GI status.**

⚠️ **The two-centre, two-technique structure is the examinable point.** **Srikalahasti** and **Machilipatnam**; **freehand mythological** work and **Persian-influenced block-printed** work. A candidate who writes "Kalamkari is a painted textile of Andhra Pradesh" has said a third of what is available. ⚠️ Note the link back to Lesson 7: the Lepakshi murals are seen as the progenitor of Kalamkari, and Kalamkari is recorded as dating to the Vijayanagara era - the two statements are consistent. ⚠️ The dyeing and printing trade itself belongs to crafts and textiles.

✅ **Fact - Kalighat.** **Kalighat painting, from 19th-century Kolkata near the Kalighat Temple, is hand-coloured on cloth and depicts Hindu deities, epic scenes, and later everyday life.** **These paintings greatly influenced painters such as Jamini Roy.**

⚠️ **Kalighat is the bridge between this lesson and Lesson 17.** A nineteenth-century urban pilgrimage-market painting that influenced a modern painter shows that the folk and the modern are not separate compartments. ❌ Do not extend the Jamini Roy link into a description of his work; the record gives the influence and nothing more.

✅ **Fact - Warli.** **Warli painting, practised by the Warlis of the Gujarat-Maharashtra border since 2500-3000 BC, resembles the Bhimbetka mural paintings of Madhya Pradesh.** **These ritualistic paintings have a central *chaukat* (square) surrounded by scenes of daily life, nature and festivals, often depicting Palaghata (fertility goddess) and male gods as human-like spirits.** **Traditionally the paintings are done on mud walls with triangles, circles and squares, and use white pigment from gum and rice powder on a red ochre background.** **Women paint them for auspicious occasions, and today they are also done on cloth with white poster colours.**

⚠️ **Handle the date with care.** "Since 2500-3000 BC" is the record's claim about the antiquity of the practice, and it is paired with a resemblance claim to Bhimbetka. ✅ Report it as recorded. ❌ Do not treat it as an archaeological dating of surviving Warli paintings, and do not convert the Bhimbetka **resemblance** into a claim of descent.

⚠️ **The *chaukat* is the compositional key.** A central square with daily life, nature and festivals around it is a structure, not a style, and structure is what distinguishes Warli from the superficially similar Saura.

✅ **Fact - Thangka.** **Presently practised in Sikkim, Himachal Pradesh, the Ladakh region and Arunachal Pradesh, Thangka paintings were originally used as a medium of reverence that evoked the highest ideals of Buddhism.** **They were traditionally made by monks and specific ethnic groups, with skills passed down through generations.** **Thangkas are painted on cotton with natural dyes, use colours symbolically and are often framed in silk brocade.**

⚠️ **Thangka is not a secular folk form**, and a classification question may test exactly that. It is a Buddhist devotional object, made by monks and specific ethnic groups, and it connects directly to the Ladakh monastic murals of Lesson 8: the same iconographic world in a portable support.

✅ **Fact - Manjusha.** **This art form belongs to the Bhagalpur region of Bihar.** **It is also known as Angika art, where "ang" refers to one of the Mahajanapadas.** **Since snake motifs are always present, it is also called snake painting.** **These paintings are generally executed on boxes of jute and paper.**

⚠️ Three names for one tradition - **Manjusha, Angika art, snake painting** - and a distinctive support, **boxes of jute and paper**. The *Anga* Mahajanapada reference supplies the etymology.

✅ **Fact - Phad.** **Phad painting, from Rajasthan, is a religious scroll art depicting local deities like Pabuji and Devnarayan.** **Painted with vegetable colours on long cloths (15-30 ft), it features large-eyed figures and lively procession scenes.**

✅ **Fact - Cheriyal.** **Indigenous to Telangana, this Nakashi scroll art narrates Hindu epics and Puranic tales like comics, often with music by balladeers.** **The scrolls can be massive, reaching up to 45 feet.** **This art form has been accorded the Geographical Indication status in 2007.**

⚠️ **Keep the three scroll traditions apart by their measurements and subjects.** **Phad**: Rajasthan, 15-30 feet, Pabuji and Devnarayan, large-eyed figures, procession scenes. **Cheriyal**: Telangana, Nakashi, up to 45 feet, Hindu epics and Puranic tales narrated like comics with balladeers' music, GI in **2007**. **Patua**: Bengal, *Mangal Kavyas*, sung by travelling painters, now on stitched paper. All three are narrative scrolls performed with words or music, which is itself a point worth making: these are **painted performances**, not static pictures.

✅ **Fact - Pithora.** **The paintings are done by some tribal communities of Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh and are said to serve a religious and spiritual purpose.** **They are painted for peace and prosperity during family occasions, and often depict animals, especially horses.**

✅ **Fact - Saura.** **They are made by the Saura Tribe of Odisha and are similar to Warli paintings.** **They are essentially a wall mural painting which are ritualistic in nature.** **Made in white on red or yellow backgrounds with plant and mineral colours, they feature stick-like geometric figures and are now popular in modern clothing designs.**

⚠️ **Saura against Warli is a likely close-option pair.** Both are ritual wall murals with white geometric figures. The discriminators: **Warli** is the Gujarat-Maharashtra border, a **red ochre** ground, white from **gum and rice powder**, with a central ***chaukat*** and **Palaghata**; **Saura** is the Saura Tribe of **Odisha**, **red or yellow** backgrounds, **plant and mineral colours**, stick-like geometric figures, now on modern clothing.

✅ **Fact - Paitkar.** **Paitkar, an ancient scroll painting tradition of Jharkhand, is culturally linked to Maa Manasa, a revered goddess in tribal households.** **These paintings are linked with rituals and themes of life after death.** **They are an ancient art now nearing extinction.**

⚠️ The record's own phrase is **"now nearing extinction"**; report it as recorded. It is the one tradition in the list described that way, and it is the natural case to cite where a question concerns transmission risk. ❌ Do not attach practitioner numbers or programme names.

✅ **Fact - Gond.** **This painting style, named after the Gond tribe, has been practised on mud walls in Madhya Pradesh for nearly 1400 years.** **It is also quite commonly found in Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra, Chhattisgarh and Odisha.** **According to the Gond belief system, all natural elements are sacred and paintings are made to honour and revere them.**

✅ **Fact - Santhal.** **These tribal paintings are drawn by a special community called Jadu Patua, or magic painters, in the Santhal tribal belts of the Bengal/Bihar borders.** **Santhal painters never came under the influences of the Mughals, Rajput or the British.** **They use hand-made paper, sometimes backed with canvas, and natural vegetable colours, a hallmark of tribal art.**

⚠️ **The Santhal entry carries an unusually strong claim** - that these painters **never came under Mughal, Rajput or British influence**. It is the record's own statement and is worth quoting precisely because it is the exception that defines the rule: almost every other tradition in this session is described through contact, borrowing and exchange.

### 3. The comparison grid, for revision

| Tradition | Region | Support and material | Function or distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Pichhwai** | Rajasthan, mainly Nathdwara | Temple hangings | Krishna as Sreenathji; Pushtimarg; introduced by Vallabhacharya in the 16th century; now a major export |
| **Madhubani** | Bihar, around Madhubani town; also Mithila | Now paper or cloth | Painted by women; deities; fish for luck and fertility; bold colours, floral patterns, double borders, two-dimensional figures; GI |
| **Tikuli** | Bihar | **Hardboard** cut into shapes, **enamel** coats | Name from *tikuli*, bindi; often features Madhubani motifs |
| **Pattachitra** | Odisha; Raghurajpur | Treated cloth; burnt coconut shell, lamp black; **lacquer** finish | *Patta* + *chitra*; Jagannath and Vaishnava themes; palm-leaf version = *talapattachitra* |
| **Patua** | Bengal - Medinipur, Murshidabad, South 24 Parganas, Birbhum | Cloth traditionally, now **poster colours on stitched paper** | *Mangal Kavyas*; Muslim Patuas travel and sing; now social and political themes |
| **Kalamkari** | Andhra Pradesh - Srikalahasti and Machilipatnam | Treated, bleached, softened cotton; **bamboo pen**; vegetable dyes | Freehand mythological and Persian-influenced block-printed; dates to the Vijayanagara era; GI |
| **Kalighat** | 19th-century Kolkata, near the Kalighat Temple | Hand-coloured on cloth | Deities, epic scenes, later everyday life; influenced Jamini Roy |
| **Warli** | Gujarat-Maharashtra border | Mud walls; white from **gum and rice powder** on **red ochre** | Central ***chaukat***; Palaghata; painted by women for auspicious occasions |
| **Thangka** | Sikkim, Himachal, Ladakh, Arunachal | Cotton, natural dyes, **silk brocade** frames | Buddhist reverence; made by monks and specific ethnic groups; symbolic colour |
| **Manjusha** | Bhagalpur, Bihar | **Boxes of jute and paper** | Also Angika art and snake painting; snake motifs always present |
| **Phad** | Rajasthan | Long cloths, **15-30 ft**; vegetable colours | Pabuji and Devnarayan; large-eyed figures; procession scenes |
| **Cheriyal** | Telangana | Nakashi scroll, up to **45 feet** | Epics and Puranic tales like comics; balladeers' music; **GI in 2007** |
| **Pithora** | Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh | - | Religious and spiritual purpose; peace and prosperity at family occasions; animals, especially horses |
| **Saura** | Odisha, Saura Tribe | White on **red or yellow**; plant and mineral colours | Ritual wall mural; stick-like geometric figures; now in modern clothing designs |
| **Paitkar** | Jharkhand | Scroll | Maa Manasa; rituals and life after death; **nearing extinction** |
| **Gond** | Madhya Pradesh, nearly **1400 years**; also AP, Maharashtra, Chhattisgarh, Odisha | Mud walls | All natural elements sacred |
| **Santhal** | Bengal/Bihar borders, Santhal belts | **Hand-made paper**, sometimes backed with canvas; natural vegetable colours | Painted by **Jadu Patua**, magic painters; never under Mughal, Rajput or British influence |

### Examination use

This is the topic's single densest identification lesson, and it is examined by pairing: Warli against Saura, Madhubani against Tikuli, Phad against Cheriyal against Patua, Pattachitra against Kalamkari. The reliable discriminators are support and material, not theme. Dated institutional facts worth securing: Jagdamba Devi's recognition in 1970, Cheriyal's GI in 2007, and the GI status recorded for Madhubani, Kalamkari and Tanjore. For a written answer on living heritage, the Pichhwai chain from ritual origin through temple use to export, the Patua transformation across support, medium and subject, and Paitkar's recorded decline together supply the evidence for treating these as production systems rather than survivals.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "folk art is colourful and traditional" instead of naming region, community, material and function.
- Merging Warli and Saura, or Madhubani and Tikuli.
- Calling Thangka a secular folk form; it is Buddhist, made by monks and specific ethnic groups.
- Treating Warli's "since 2500-3000 BC" as an archaeological date for surviving paintings, or the Bhimbetka resemblance as descent.
- Assigning Kalamkari to one centre or one technique; it has two of each.
- Forgetting *talapattachitra* as the palm-leaf form of Pattachitra.
- Confusing scroll lengths: Phad 15-30 feet, Cheriyal up to 45 feet.
- Describing these traditions as frozen survivals when the record repeatedly describes change of support, medium, subject and market.
- Asserting current geographical-indication positions from this session rather than routing them for verification.

### Revision notes

1. **The rule.** Each tradition has a distinct region, community, support, material and function; none is interchangeable with another, and several are recorded as changing support, medium, subject and market.
2. **Pichhwai** - Rajasthan, mainly Nathdwara; Krishna as Sreenathji under Vaishnavism; originally temple hangings for the Pushtimarg Sampradaya; now a major export of Nathdwara; Vallabhacharya credited with introducing it in the 16th century.
3. **Madhubani or Mithila** - villages around Madhubani town, Bihar; traditionally by women; mentioned in the Ramayana and believed to have begun in the Ramayana era at Sita and Rama's marriage; Krishna, Rama, Durga, Lakshmi, Shiva; fish for luck and fertility; now on paper or cloth; bold colours, floral patterns, double borders, two-dimensional figures; recognition in 1970 when Jagdamba Devi was honoured; Baua Devi, Bharti Dayal, Ganga Devi, Mahasundari Devi, Sita Devi; GI because the art has remained confined to a specific geographical area. **Tikuli** - Bihar; from *tikuli*, bindi; hardboard cut into shapes, polished with enamel coats; often Madhubani motifs. **Manjusha** - Bhagalpur, Bihar; also Angika art, "ang" from a Mahajanapada, and snake painting because snake motifs are always present; on boxes of jute and paper.
4. **Pattachitra** - Odisha; *patta* canvas plus *chitra* picture; treated cloth; burnt coconut shell and lamp black; brush outline in red or yellow, colour fill, foliage and flowers, intricate framing, lacquer gloss; Jagannath and Vaishnava, sometimes Shakti and Shaiva; Raghurajpur; similar to old murals at Puri and Konark; on palm leaf, *talapattachitra*.
5. **The three narrative scrolls, distinguished by length and place.** **Patua** - Bengal, over a thousand years old; *Mangal Kavyas*; Muslim Patuas travel between villages singing the stories; now poster colours on stitched paper with social and political themes; Medinipur, Murshidabad, South 24 Parganas, Birbhum. **Phad** - Rajasthan; Pabuji and Devnarayan; vegetable colours on long cloths of 15-30 ft; large-eyed figures and lively procession scenes. **Cheriyal** - Telangana; Nakashi scroll; Hindu epics and Puranic tales narrated like comics, often with balladeers' music; up to 45 feet; GI status in 2007.
6. **Kalamkari** - named after the *kalam*; treated cotton, bleached and softened; bamboo pen sharpened and wrapped with cloth to control colour flow; black outlines first, then vegetable dyes; Srikalahasti and Machilipatnam, Andhra Pradesh; freehand Hindu mythological and Persian-influenced block-printed; dates to the Vijayanagara era; GI.
7. **Kalighat** - 19th-century Kolkata near the Kalighat Temple; hand-coloured on cloth; deities, epic scenes, later everyday life; greatly influenced painters such as Jamini Roy - the bridge from this lesson to modern painting.
8. **Warli** - Warlis of the Gujarat-Maharashtra border, recorded as practised since 2500-3000 BC; resembles Bhimbetka murals; central *chaukat* surrounded by daily life, nature and festivals; Palaghata the fertility goddess; male gods as human-like spirits; mud walls, triangles, circles, squares; white pigment from gum and rice powder on red ochre; painted by women for auspicious occasions; today also on cloth with white poster colours.
9. **Saura** - Saura Tribe of Odisha; similar to Warli; ritual wall mural; white on red or yellow backgrounds with plant and mineral colours; stick-like geometric figures; now popular in modern clothing designs. The Warli-Saura discriminators are the ground colour, the pigment source and the central *chaukat*.
10. **The two other wall traditions.** **Pithora** - tribal communities of Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh; said to serve a religious and spiritual purpose; painted for peace and prosperity during family occasions; animals, especially horses. **Gond** - named after the Gond tribe; on mud walls in Madhya Pradesh for nearly 1400 years; also common in Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra, Chhattisgarh and Odisha; all natural elements sacred and honoured in painting.
11. **Thangka** - Sikkim, Himachal Pradesh, Ladakh, Arunachal Pradesh; originally a medium of reverence evoking the highest ideals of Buddhism; traditionally made by monks and specific ethnic groups, skills passed down generations; cotton, natural dyes, symbolic colour, often framed in silk brocade. Not a secular folk form.
12. **Paitkar** - Jharkhand; ancient scroll tradition; culturally linked to Maa Manasa, revered in tribal households; rituals and themes of life after death; now nearing extinction - the topic's one recorded case of transmission failure.
13. **Santhal** - Jadu Patua or magic painters in the Santhal tribal belts of the Bengal/Bihar borders; never came under Mughal, Rajput or British influence; hand-made paper sometimes backed with canvas; natural vegetable colours. The one tradition recorded as outside the contact history that explains almost every other entry in this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** Choose Patua and Pichhwai and use them to show why "living tradition" is a more accurate description than "surviving folk art".

**Model answer:** Patua painting of Bengal is over a thousand years old and consists of scrolls depicting *Mangal Kavyas*, the auspicious stories of Hindu deities, which Muslim Patuas traditionally carry between villages while singing them. Every material element of that practice has changed: paintings traditionally on cloth showing religious stories are now done with poster colours on stitched paper and depict social and political themes. Support, medium and subject have all moved, while the performance practice of travelling and singing persists. That is adaptation, not survival. Pichhwai shows the same point through economics. It originates in Rajasthan, mainly Nathdwara, depicting Krishna as Sreenathji, made originally as temple hangings for the Pushtimarg Sampradaya, and credited to Vallabhacharya in the sixteenth century; it is now a major export of Nathdwara. A ritual object has acquired a market without ceasing to be a ritual form. "Surviving folk art" implies a static remnant persisting by inertia, which neither case fits. "Living tradition" names what the record actually describes - a community, a material, an occasion and, increasingly, a market, each of which can change while the tradition continues.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that commercialisation marks the end of authenticity. The record treats the Nathdwara export trade as part of Pichhwai's present existence, and the same pattern appears in Saura designs on modern clothing and Warli on cloth with poster colours. The change to note is the change in the production system, not a fall from an imagined pure state.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Folk and tribal painting should be assessed as a production system - material, community, occasion and market - rather than as a style." Examine with named examples. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is the right frame, and the named traditions supply each of its four elements.

Material identifies more reliably than style. Warli uses white pigment from gum and rice powder on a red ochre mud wall; Saura, superficially similar, uses white on red or yellow grounds with plant and mineral colours; Tikuli is painted on hardboard polished with enamel; Manjusha is executed on boxes of jute and paper; Santhal painters use hand-made paper sometimes backed with canvas. Kalamkari is defined by a bamboo pen, black outlines and vegetable dyes on treated cotton.

Community is equally constitutive. Madhubani is traditionally painted by the women of villages around Madhubani town; Patua scrolls are carried and sung by Muslim Patuas; Thangkas are made by monks and specific ethnic groups; Santhal work is by the Jadu Patua, the magic painters.

Occasion supplies the purpose. Warli is painted by women for auspicious occasions; Pithora for peace and prosperity at family occasions; Pichhwai originally as temple hangings for the Pushtimarg Sampradaya; Paitkar for rituals and themes of life after death.

Market is now part of the system. Pichhwai is a major export of Nathdwara; Saura designs appear in modern clothing; Cheriyal received geographical-indication status in 2007 and Madhubani holds it because the art remained confined to a specific area.

Style still describes; the four-part system explains why the tradition exists, who sustains it, and why Paitkar is recorded as nearing extinction.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = material discrimination across at least four named traditions 5 + community identification across at least three 3 + occasion across at least three 3 + market and recognition with two dated facts 3 + Paitkar used to show what the framework diagnoses that style cannot 1.**

## Lesson 19 - Attribution, conservation, comparison, and building the answer

Progress: 19/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the attribution ladder, the contested list, the survival argument, the single current linkage, the demand map, the theses, the mark-scaled structures and the complete risk list

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the technique, conservation and survival-argument material; the attribution and provenance cautions; the directive-and-demand map, thesis options, mark-scaled structures and factual-risk controls
CA search: "Ajanta paintings restoration State Museum Hyderabad eye copies 2026"
CA found: *Deccan Chronicle*, "Ajanta Paintings at State Museum to Get New Life", published 28 July 2026 - the Ajanta Paintings Gallery's hand-painted replicas, known as "eye copies", are to be restored in collaboration with the Noor International Microfilm Centre and the heritage department
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four kinds of claim about a painting, ranked by how much they can bear

```text
  LEVEL 1  RECORDED IDENTIFICATION AT THE OBJECT
           the cave number, the name, the named figure within it
           e.g. Bagh Cave 4 is named RANG MAHAL and carries JATAKA murals
                Brihadeswara carries a portrait of RAJARAJA CHOLA I
                with his guru KARUVUR DEVAR
           -> safest. Reproduce as recorded.
                              |
  LEVEL 2  RECORDED PATRONAGE AND DATE
           a named patron with a reign span, or a recorded period
           e.g. NARASIMHAVARMAN II RAJASIMHA, 700-720 CE, at Kanchi
                RAJA SIDH SEN at Mandi, 1684-1727
                MADHO SINGH at Jaipur, 1777-1802
           -> safe, but check whether the record itself hedges
                              |
  LEVEL 3  RECORDED TECHNIQUE, MATERIAL AND FORMAL PROPERTY
           e.g. FRESCO SECCO at Ajanta and Ellora
                GESSO PASTE at Mysore; GOLD LEAF and PALAGAI PADAM at Tanjore
                BASOHLI's lotus-shaped eyes and vibrant primary colours
           -> strong, and often the best discriminator in a close-option item
                              |
  LEVEL 4  ATTRIBUTION OF A WORK TO AN INDIVIDUAL ARTIST
           e.g. BANI THANI and NIHAL CHAND
           -> weakest. Use "ASSOCIATED WITH" unless single authorship
              is documented. NEVER complete a partial name.
```

*The ladder is a rule about what kind of sentence you are writing. A level-four claim cannot be made in the register of a level-one claim, and most avoidable losses in this topic come from doing exactly that.*

### 1. The complete list of points this topic must not settle

The following are the places where the evidence is genuinely open. Each has a safe formulation, and the safe formulation is the answer.

| Contested or bounded point | The disciplined formulation |
|---|---|
| **Bagh's excavation dates** | Gupta period, with the record giving both a 5th-6th century and a 5th-7th century range |
| **Bagh as "more materialistic than spiritualistic"** | The record's relative descriptive contrast of emphasis, stated with its Buddhist subject matter attached |
| **Hallisalasya's cave** | Attributed to the Bagh paintings generally; no cave is named, no modern dance form is equated, no reading beyond "devotional or courtly" |
| **Sittanavasal's technique word** | Recorded as "fresco technique with many mineral colours", grouped with Ajanta and Bagh, whose method is specifically fresco secco |
| **The Ajanta-Sittanavasal-Thanjavur chain** | *Said to* provide a link; report the hedge |
| **Armamalai's Jain and Shaiva panels** | The site's re-use is recorded; individual panel attribution between phases is not settled |
| **Babur's patronage of Bihzad** | *Said to have* patronised; keep the hedge |
| **Shah Jahan's European inspiration** | *It is said that* he was inspired by the European style; keep the hedge |
| **The Tanjore origin story** | *Probably* developed from the defeat of the Thanjavur Nayakas and the fall of Vijayanagara |
| **The origin of Rajput painting** | Four explanations are recorded and none is settled; they are not mutually exclusive |
| **The *Shangri Ramayana*** | Recorded both as produced in Jammu and as created by Pahari artists in Kullu; the two are not reconciled |
| **Sansar Chand's role** | Recorded both among the three greatest Pahari painters and as the patron under whom Kangra reached its zenith |
| **Bani Thani's authorship** | Kishangarh School; associated with Nihal Chand, whose collaboration with Sawant Singh and Nagari Das produced the school's most celebrated works |
| **Rabindranath Tagore's paintings and his writings** | *Some art historians argue* the link |
| **The Lepakshi-Kalamkari relation** | Lepakshi murals are *seen as* the progenitor or precursor of Kalamkari |
| **Warli's antiquity and the Bhimbetka resemblance** | Recorded as practised since 2500-3000 BC and as resembling Bhimbetka; resemblance is not descent |
| **Madhubani's Ramayana-era origin** | *Believed* to have begun in the Ramayana era |
| **The 2026 Preliminary item's number** | Two records held here number the item differently - one as Question 5, one as item 6. The numbering is unresolved and neither number is stated as settled; the question's wording, paper, year, set and ownership are unaffected and remain fully usable |
| **Badami as a painting site** | Named in the mural site list with no described programme in the evidence |
| **The clipped source tables** | The illustrated-manuscript details, the Company-Bazaar cells and three artist names in the notable-paintings table are clipped; nothing is completed |

### 2. The survival argument, stated once and reusable everywhere

⚠️ **The rule.** Only **two Pallava mural locations** and **two Chola mural temples** survive; **nothing significant remains at Tirumalaipuram**; **Vijaya Cholishwaram** retains only faintly visible lines; **Sittanavasal's paintings are not in their best-preserved state**; and **most of the rock-cut caves originally had mural paintings but a few survive today**.

✅ **The sentence to write.** *"Only two Pallava mural locations and two Chola mural temples survive. This constrains what can be claimed about the scale of the tradition, not what can be claimed about its quality."*

⚠️ **Three separate causes of loss, which a strong answer distinguishes.**

| Cause | Mechanism | Named instance |
|---|---|---|
| **Technique** | Paint on an already dried surface is a film on the wall rather than a bond within it | The rock-cut cave tradition generally |
| **Support** | A mural cannot be moved when its building fails | Tirumalaipuram |
| **Overpainting** | A later programme is laid over an earlier one | **Nayaka-period superimpositions** over Rajaraja-period work at **Brihadeswara** |

⚠️ **Why superimposition is the most interesting of the three.** It is not loss by decay but loss by **succession**, and it creates a genuine dilemma: a wall carrying two periods can present only one at a time. The **fact of the layering** is owned here; conservation policy, institutional arrangements and any status claim belong to heritage.

### 3. The single current linkage for this topic

📰 **The item, dated and attributed.** *Deccan Chronicle*, "Ajanta Paintings at State Museum to Get New Life", **published 28 July 2026**.

✅ **What the report records.** The **Ajanta paintings at the State Museum, Hyderabad**, are set to be restored **in collaboration with the Noor International Microfilm Centre and the heritage department**. The **Ajanta Paintings Gallery houses hand-painted replicas, known as "eye copies", of the murals from the Ajanta Caves in Maharashtra**, making the State Museum, in the report's words, **the only museum in India to possess such a collection**. The paintings were **made on canvas by artists who were specially sent to Ajanta for the project**; **around 40 are on display and the rest are preserved in the museum's store**. They depict **the life of the Buddha, Bodhisattvas, Jataka tales, royal courts, merchants, musicians, dancers and everyday life**, and include replicas of **Padmapani Bodhisattva, Vajrapani Bodhisattva and several courtly scenes**. **Many have suffered damage**, and the proposed conservation aims to restore and preserve the collection; **experts from the Noor International Microfilm Centre will begin after completing an ongoing manuscript conservation project**. The report quotes **Prof. Arjun Rao, director of the heritage department**: *"Conservation initiatives have been proposed to stabilise and restore these paintings using traditional methods so that this unique documentation can be preserved for future generations."* It further records that **the Hyderabad archaeological department, under the leadership of Dr Ghulam Yazdani, initiated a project in the early twentieth century to create replicas of the Ajanta murals before the originals deteriorated further**, and that **the replicas later became part of the Hyderabad museum collection**.

⚠️ **Why this item earns its place in this topic, and it earns it three times over.**

1. **It is a documented instance of the survival problem being anticipated.** A replication project was begun **before the originals deteriorated further** - which is the conservation response to precisely the fragility that fresco secco predicts.
2. **It creates a second-order conservation problem.** The replicas are themselves now damaged and require conservation. A copy made to outlast an original has become an object needing preservation, which is the clearest available illustration that no support is permanent.
3. **Its contents map onto this session's Ajanta lesson exactly.** The replicated subjects - the life of the Buddha, Bodhisattvas, Jataka tales, royal courts, merchants, musicians, dancers, everyday life, with **Padmapani** and **Vajrapani** named - are the same programme taught in Lesson 4, which allows the item to be used as evidence rather than as decoration.

❌ **The boundaries on this item, observed strictly.** It is attributed to a **dated newspaper report** of a museum and department position. No primary conservation document was consulted. It asserts nothing about the present condition of the **original** Ajanta murals, nothing about conservation **policy**, and nothing about institutional structure or funding. No further current item is used anywhere in this session.

### 4. The demand map: what a question is really asking

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Trace the evolution of Indian painting* | Format change - rock, wall, manuscript, album, canvas and print, community - tied to patronage change | A chronological list of schools |
| *Mughal painting and its regional legacy* | The atelier as a collective workshop, and dispersal as the mechanism of regional diffusion | "Mughal painting influenced Rajput painting" |
| *Distinguish the Rajasthani and Pahari schools* | Sub-school-level distinctions - Mewar, Amber-Jaipur, Marwar, Kishangarh; Basohli, Guler, Kangra, Mankot, Kullu, Mandi, Jammu - with theme and palette | Treating each family as one style |
| *Murals as historical evidence* | Survival bias, patron themes, technique, superimposition | Assuming what survives is what existed |
| *Folk and tribal painting today* | Material, community, region, occasion, market, recognition, transmission | "Folk art is colourful and traditional" |
| *Painting and the national movement* | The Bengal School as a double reaction - against colonial hybridity and against academic realism - with its eight recorded features | "Artists supported the freedom struggle" |
| *Contribution of a dynasty to painting* | Named surviving works, patron, theme, and an honest survival caveat | Generic "painting flourished" |
| *Technique in Indian painting* | Fresco secco versus true fresco; gesso versus gold leaf; bamboo pen and vegetable dye; wash technique | Using "fresco" as a general word for wall painting |
| *Art and the state* | Nandalal Bose illustrating the Constitution and sketching the Bharat Ratna and Padma emblems; Abu'l Hasan's allegories of divine right | Vague claims about art and politics |

### 5. Five qualified theses, each reusable

- ⚠️ **Format-and-patronage thesis, the most reusable.** "Indian painting's history is best told as a sequence of formats, each matched to a patronage and a viewing public: monumental murals for collective devotional viewing, manuscript and album miniatures for private connoisseurship, and print, canvas and market production for a mass public."
- ⚠️ **Workshop thesis.** "The Mughal miniature was produced by a collaborative studio recruited from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa, not by isolated geniuses - which is why the dispersal of trained artists when imperial patronage contracted was enough to seed Rajasthani and Pahari schools."
- ⚠️ **Survival-bias thesis.** "Judgements about painting traditions must distinguish what was produced from what survived: only two Pallava mural locations and two Chola mural temples survive, and reading that as evidence of a thin tradition confuses preservation with production."
- ⚠️ **Nationalist-art thesis.** "The Bengal School's significance is that it made technique itself a political statement - rejecting both colonial hybridity and Ravi Varma's academic realism in favour of a wash technique and an Ajanta-derived line claimed as Indian."
- ⚠️ **Living-tradition thesis.** "Folk and tribal painting is not a survival from the past but a functioning production system - material, community, ritual occasion and market together - and institutional recognition touches only one link of it."

### 6. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis, then the format concerned, then two named works or schools with technique and theme, then one limitation of survival or attribution, then verdict |
| **15** | Thesis, then the mural-to-miniature format shift, then the atelier mechanism, then two regional sub-schools with their distinctions, then a modern or folk closing dimension, then verdict |
| **20** | All of the above, plus the full chronological arc from rock art, a technique discussion covering fresco secco, pigments and supports, patronage-and-audience analysis, the layering and survival problem, the folk and tribal living-tradition dimension, and a verdict answering the exact wording |

### 7. The complete risk list

❌ Do not, anywhere in an answer on this topic:

- call any Indian mural site **true fresco**, or use "fresco" unqualified where the technique matters;
- treat **rock art** and **rock painting** as synonyms, or place cupules outside the Lower Palaeolithic;
- assign all Ajanta murals to the Gupta period, or move The Dying Princess out of Cave 16;
- name a specific Bagh cave for the **Hallisalasya** dance, equate it with a named modern classical dance form, or read it beyond "devotional or courtly";
- silently choose one of the two recorded **Bagh** date ranges;
- push the Bagh "materialistic" formula into a doctrinal classification;
- present the paucity of **Pallava** murals as proof of a minor tradition;
- drop the recorded hedges - "said to", "probably", "believed", "some art historians argue", "seen as";
- claim significant surviving painting at **Tirumalaipuram**, or extensive painting at **Ellora** beyond five caves;
- supply a subject, date, cave number or technique for **Badami** paintings;
- attribute **Bani Thani** to any school other than **Kishangarh**, or assert single authorship without the "associated with" qualification;
- complete a **clipped** source entry - a manuscript description, a Company-Bazaar cell or a partial artist name;
- swap **gesso paste** (Mysore) with **gold leaf** (Tanjore), or give Tanjore a cloth or vellum support;
- swap the titles ***Nadir ul Asr*** (Ustad Mansur) and ***Nadir-al-Zaman*** (Abu'l Hasan);
- assign the shift from manuscripts to **albums and individual portraiture** to any emperor other than Jahangir;
- present Mughal painting as a smooth rise to naturalism, ignoring Shah Jahan's artificial elements and unnatural stillness;
- supply named Deccani courts, painters, manuscripts, dates or techniques;
- treat **Pahari** as one style, or make Kangra the earliest Pahari school;
- resolve the ***Shangri Ramayana*** to one location;
- merge **Warli** with **Saura**, or **Madhubani** with **Tikuli**;
- call **Thangka** a secular folk form;
- describe any living tradition as a frozen survival;
- assert a current **geographical-indication**, conservation, display or institutional status beyond the dated facts recorded in this session - the recognition of 1970, the Cheriyal GI of 2007, the Mysore Ganjifa GI of 2008, and the single dated report in this lesson;
- invent pigment recipes, panel dimensions, painting-level dates, cost figures or practitioner numbers.

### Examination use

This lesson is the control layer for the whole topic. The attribution ladder decides what register a sentence may be written in; the contested list supplies a safe formulation for every open point; the survival argument with its three distinct causes of loss converts a weakness in the evidence into an argument; the dated current item supplies the one live anchor and demonstrates the second-order conservation problem; the demand map decides which of the preceding eighteen lessons a question actually wants; the five theses supply openings and verdicts; the mark-scaled structures decide what fits; and the risk list is a list of forfeitures, avoiding which is worth more than any additional fact.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing a level-four artist attribution in the register of a level-one recorded identification.
- Treating a hedged statement as settled because the hedge is inconvenient to the argument.
- Using the current item to make claims about the condition of the original Ajanta murals, or about conservation policy.
- Choosing a thesis the question's directive does not support - offering the survival-bias thesis to a question about technique, for example.
- Treating superimposition as decay rather than as loss by succession.
- Adding an unverifiable fact at the end of an otherwise safe answer.

### Revision notes

1. The attribution ladder, in descending order of security: recorded identification at the object; recorded patronage and date; recorded technique, material and formal property; attribution of a work to an individual artist.
2. Level-four claims use "associated with" unless single authorship is documented; partial names are never completed.
3. The **twenty** contested or bounded points, each with its safe formulation, from the two Bagh date ranges to the clipped source tables.
4. The survival rule: only two Pallava mural locations and two Chola mural temples survive; Tirumalaipuram retains nothing significant; Vijaya Cholishwaram retains faint lines; Sittanavasal is not in its best-preserved state; most rock-cut caves originally had murals and few survive.
5. Three distinct causes of loss: technique - paint on a dried surface; support - a mural cannot be moved; overpainting - Nayaka superimpositions over Rajaraja-period work at Brihadeswara.
6. The single current linkage, from a report dated 28 July 2026: the State Museum, Hyderabad, is to restore its Ajanta Paintings Gallery - hand-painted canvas replicas known as "eye copies", around 40 on display and the rest in store, depicting the life of the Buddha, Bodhisattvas, Jataka tales, royal courts, merchants, musicians, dancers and everyday life, including Padmapani and Vajrapani - in collaboration with the Noor International Microfilm Centre and the heritage department, using traditional methods; the replicas were created under a project initiated by the Hyderabad archaeological department under Dr Ghulam Yazdani in the early twentieth century, before the originals deteriorated further.
7. What that item illustrates: anticipation of loss; the second-order problem that a replica made to outlast an original itself needs conservation; and a subject list that matches the Ajanta programme exactly.
8. The demand map: nine question shapes, each with what it is really testing and what must not be written.
9. Five reusable theses: format-and-patronage, workshop, survival-bias, nationalist-art, living-tradition.
10. Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20 marks, with the rock-art arc, the technique discussion, the layering problem and the folk dimension added only at 20.
11. The risk list is a list of forfeitures; avoiding all of it is worth more than any single additional fact.

### Concept check

**Question:** Apply the attribution ladder to two cases from this topic - one where a claim can be made flatly, and one where it cannot - and explain what changes between them.

**Model answer:** The flat case is Bagh Cave 4. The record identifies the most significant cave at the site as Cave 4, states that it is known as Rang Mahal, and states that its walls carry murals depicting the Buddhist Jataka tales. That is a level-one claim: an identification recorded at the object itself, with a number, a name and a subject. It can be written without qualification, and adding a hedge would weaken it. The case that cannot be made flatly is *Bani Thani*. Three separate statements exist: that the Kishangarh School is famed for the painting; that the school's most celebrated works were created by the painter Nihal Chand in collaboration with prince Sawant Singh and the poet Nagari Das; and that a reproduction of the painting carries the painter's name in its caption. What is securely established is the school, which is a level-one-to-two claim; the pairing of the individual artist with that individual work is a level-four claim resting on a caption and a general statement about the school's celebrated works. The correct formulation is therefore "*Bani Thani*, associated with Nihal Chand, of the Kishangarh School". What changes between the two cases is the kind of evidence, and therefore the kind of sentence: a recorded identification supports an assertion, while an inferred pairing supports only an association.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that hedging is a general safety habit to be applied everywhere. Over-hedging a securely recorded fact is itself an error, because it suggests the candidate cannot tell the levels apart. The ladder asks you to match the strength of the sentence to the strength of the evidence in both directions.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In the study of Indian painting, what has been lost is as analytically important as what survives." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is sound, and the examination should establish the scale of loss, the distinct mechanisms behind it, and the limits of arguing from absence.

The scale is recorded. Most of the rock-cut caves originally had mural paintings and only a few survive. Only two Pallava mural locations survive - the Kailashnath ambulatory at Kanchi and Panamalai - and Chola wall painting survives in only two temples, of which Vijaya Cholishwaram retains only faintly visible lines. Nothing significant remains at Tirumalaipuram, and Sittanavasal's floral creepers, celestial nymphs and birds are not in their best-preserved state.

The mechanisms are three and must be kept apart. Technique: the murals are paintings done on already dried surfaces, and at Ajanta and Ellora specifically fresco secco, so the paint film rests on the plaster rather than binding into it. Support: a wall-bound painting cannot be removed when its building fails. Succession: at Brihadeswara, Rajaraja-period work carries Nayaka-period superimpositions, so a later programme displaced an earlier one without any decay at all.

Loss is analytically productive because it disciplines inference. It forbids reading the paucity of Pallava murals as evidence of a minor tradition, and it explains why literary mention in *Silappadikaram* and *Manimekalai* matters as evidence of practice.

Its limit is symmetrical: absence cannot evidence presence either. What survives constrains claims about scale in both directions, and the honest conclusion states the constraint rather than filling it.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = five named survival statements establishing scale 5 + the three loss mechanisms distinguished, with superimposition identified as non-decay 6 + the disciplinary consequence for inference about Pallava painting 4 + literary evidence used to show what loss makes valuable 3 + the symmetrical limit that absence proves nothing either way 2.**

**No model answer is supplied anywhere in this file for any official question.** Every Mains question set for practice in this file - the nineteen lesson-local questions and the six final questions - is **original**: none reproduces an official question, none paraphrases an official question's operation, and none re-words an official demand under a different verb. The six owned official Mains questions appear only in this section, with their wording as it survives, a directive analysis and a concise answer approach, and with no answer text, opening sentence, closing sentence or skeleton attached anywhere.

### 2026 Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination, General Studies Paper I

> The Hallisalasya painting in the Bagh Caves represents :
>
> (a) A joyous folk dance
>
> (b) Buddha in a meditative pose
>
> (c) The depiction of Shiva and Parvati on Kailasha
>
> (d) Samudramanthan (Churning of the Ocean)

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper I |
| Booklet | TDMN-A-ASO, printed page 3-A |
| Question number | ⚠️ **Disclosed discrepancy, not resolved.** The repository's 2026 Preliminary routing ledger and this topic's own evidence base both record the item as **Question 5**. The official Set-A booklet's text layer prints it as item **6** on printed page 3-A, and a coordinate-level reading of that page places the item number at the head of the third right-column item, after the Nagara-shikhara item and the Jain forms-of-existence item. The scan is an imperfect text layer in which at least one other item number is rendered ambiguously, so **neither number is asserted as settled here**. Wording, paper, year, set and ownership are unaffected |
| Answer key | ⚠️ A **provisional** 2026 Set-A key is held locally. It is provisional, and **no answer letter is recorded or inferred** |
| Ownership | Routed to this topic by the 2026 Preliminary ledger, and integrated into this topic's own evidence base |

| Official local source | `books\prelima_question_paper_answers\2026-GS1-Set A.pdf`, PDF page 3, read from the extracted English text layer and checked against the page's word coordinates |

**Directive and demand analysis.** The item is a single-statement identification with four close options drawn from four different iconographic families - a dance, a Buddhist devotional subject, a Shaiva narrative and a Puranic episode. It is therefore testing whether the candidate has read the site's own recorded description closely, rather than whether they can reason about Gupta-period iconography in general. Site-level familiarity alone is not the competence being tested; sentence-level familiarity is.

**Answer approach, without an answer.** The preparation required is exact and narrow, and it is entirely a preparation task rather than an option task: work from Lesson 5, which reproduces the full recorded sentence on the Bagh programme together with the three prohibitions attached to it - that no specific Bagh cave is named in that connection, that no named modern classical dance form may be substituted, and that no reading beyond the record's own words is supported. **No option is preferred, excluded or mapped here.**

**What the item confirms about this topic's examination profile.** It is a single-sentence fact from a site description, tested against options from unrelated families. That is the characteristic shape of a painting item, and it is why Lessons 2 to 8 and 14 to 18 are written as precise fact sets rather than as narratives.

> Who among the following Mughal Emperors shifted emphasis from illustrated manuscripts to album and individual portrait?
>

>

>
> (d) Shahjahan

|---|---|

**Directive and demand analysis.** A four-emperor discrimination in which all four options are genuine Mughal patrons of painting. The item is therefore testing a **format** fact rather than a patronage fact: it asks the candidate to attach a specific change in the kind of object being made to a specific reign. A candidate who has learned an evaluative summary of Mughal painting without learning what changed, reign by reign, will have no basis for deciding.

**Answer approach, without an answer.** Prepare the whole sequence rather than any single reign, using the six-reign table in Lesson 12, which sets out for each of Babur, Humayun, Akbar, Jahangir, Shah Jahan and Aurangzeb the object being made, the dominant subject and one named technical or material fact. Lesson 11 supplies the workshop and manuscript phase in the same form. **No option is preferred, excluded or mapped here**, and no reign is singled out in this block.

| **CDS I 2024** | Which early Chola painting was discovered at the Brihadisvara Temple, Thanjavur, in 1931 | Lesson 7 ⚠️ note that the **1931** discovery date is carried only by this reproduced item, not by the topic's own evidence, and is not asserted as this session's fact |
| **NDA I 2023** | A Renaissance artist and a named European work | ❌ Not relevant to this topic; recorded only because it sits in the same reproduced list |
| **CAPF 2022** | Which listed feature was **not** a feature of nineteenth-century painting in India | Lesson 17 |
| **CAPF 2022** | Which artwork Sister Nivedita hailed as the first masterpiece of Indian art | Lesson 17 |
| **CAPF 2022** | Two statements on early Malwa and early Mughal painting and their antecedents | Lessons 11 and 15 |
| **CDS I 2020** | Which Mughal painter made a named painting of Jahangir and Shah Abbas | Lesson 12 |
| **CDS I 2016** | Which listed subjects are **not** depicted in Rajput paintings | Lessons 11, 12 and 14 |
| **CDS I 2016** | Which listed book was **not** illustrated at Akbar's court | Lesson 11 |
| **CDS II 2015** | Which listed person was **not** a painter at Akbar's court | Lesson 11 |

⚠️ **The preparation point these ten items make collectively.** Most of them turn on **named lists** taught in Lessons 11, 12, 14 and 17 - the Akbari manuscripts, the Akbari painters, the named works of Jahangir's chief portrait painter, the Rajasthani repertoire, and the recorded assessments in the modern section. Named lists are the highest-yield form of preparation in this topic precisely because objective items are built as membership tests. ⚠️ Option lists are deliberately not reproduced for these corroborating items, so that no routing in this table can decide any of them.

## Exact official Civil Services Main Examination questions owned by this topic

✅ **Six questions**, preserved in the reproduced previous-years' Main Examination list inside the core art source. **No official paper for any of these years is held locally**, so the following applies to all six: ❌ **no marks, word limit or printed question position is claimed anywhere**, and the wording is reproduced as it survives in that list. **No model answer is supplied for any of them.**

### 2012 - the Flute Playing Krishna theme

> The 'Flute Playing Krishna' theme is very popular in Indian art. Discuss. *(CSE, 2012)*

**Directive.** *Discuss* - set out the theme, its range and its significance, with reasons rather than assertions.

**Conceptual demand.** The question hands you a **motif** and asks why it recurs across Indian art. The answer is won by showing the motif's **distribution across formats, schools and regions**, because recurrence across unconnected production systems is what "very popular" means evidentially.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Establish the motif's spread from the material taught here: flute-playing Krishna appears in **Bundi's** wall paintings and in **Jaipur's** city-palace commissions under Madho Singh; Krishna themes dominate **Jaisalmer's** Rang Mahal, **Bikaner's** Chandra and Anup Mahals, **Marwar's** Krishna-Radha and Nayaka-Nayika subjects and **Mewar's** Radha-Krishna havelis; Krishna love scenes are a prominent theme at **Kangra**, and the *Gita Govinda* is a subject at **Basohli**, **Guler** and **Kangra** alike; **Kishangarh** made many paintings on the devotional and amorous relations of Radha and Krishna; **Tanjore** images are mostly of Krishna in various poses and key life events; **Pichhwai** depicts Krishna as Sreenathji; **Madhubani** depicts Krishna among its deities; **Guruvayur's** Kerala murals treat Krishna's childhood; and **Kota's** Rajmahal front wall shows Krishna's childhood and the forests of Vrindavan. Then explain the recurrence - a devotional current that made Krishna the deity of love, a format-independent subject that works equally on a wall, a page, a hanging and a plank, and a narrative fund deep enough to supply both the child and the lover. Close on the qualification that ubiquity is not uniformity: the same motif is doing devotional work at Nathdwara, courtly work at Jaipur and market work in an export trade.

### 2011 - distinguishing two pairs

> While banging out their salient features, distinguish between either 'Madhubani' Art and 'Manjusha' Art or 'Rajasthani' Schools of Painting and the 'Pahari' School of Painting. *(CSE, 2011)*

⚠️ **A disclosed wording defect.** The phrase **"banging out"** is how the question survives in the reproduced list, and it is evidently a typographical corruption of a standard directive of the "bringing out" form. ❌ The corrupted word is **reproduced as it survives and is not silently corrected**, and no claim is made about the original printed wording, because no official 2011 paper is held locally.

**Directive.** *Distinguish*, with salient features - a comparison demand with an either/or choice between two pairs.

**Conceptual demand.** Both options test whether the candidate can separate two things that are routinely merged. The first pair merges two Bihar folk traditions; the second merges two school **families**, each of which contains multiple sub-schools.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** For the first pair, distinguish on the four axes taught in Lesson 18 - region within Bihar, support and material, motif, and function - noting that Manjusha carries two further names and an invariant motif, while Madhubani carries a named transmission line and an institutional recognition. For the second pair, refuse at the outset the framing that treats each as one style: name the Rajasthani sub-schools and the Pahari sub-schools taught in Lessons 14 and 15, distinguish the families on origin, patronage structure and characteristic subject, and then distinguish **within** each family so that the answer demonstrates sub-school-level control. Close with the shared inheritance that prevents the contrast from becoming a caricature.

### 1993 - the Bundi School

> Write about the chief features of the Bundi School of Painting. *(CSE, 1993)*

**Directive.** *Write about the chief features* - a descriptive demand requiring specificity rather than argument.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** The material is in Lesson 8. Establish Bundi's place within Harauti and the Hada Rajput country, its strategic position on the Delhi-to-Deccan route and the effect of that position on its painting; then the fort and the earliest work at Badal Mahal; then the stylistic character recorded for it, including the influences it carries; then its named subjects; then the Jain wall painting at the Adinatha temple; and finally the relation to Kota, which was carved out of Bundi and went on to outshine it. ⚠️ The strongest feature of this answer is the one candidates omit: Bundi is **not** a pure Rajput idiom, and saying so with its recorded influences is what distinguishes the answer.

### 1990 - three short notes

> Write about the chief features of: (a) Kishangarh style of painting (b) Neo art movement of Rabindra Nath Tagore (c) Kangra School of Painting *(CSE, 1990)*

**Directive.** *Write about the chief features* of three separate subjects - a three-part short-note demand.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** For (a), Lesson 14 supplies dates, the school's place within Marwar, the romantic legends, the three-person collaboration, the famous painting and its four described features. For (b), ⚠️ note carefully that the question names a **movement** associated with Rabindranath Tagore; the evidence carried here records his **own painting practice** - dominant black lines, small-sized works, and a scholarly reading linking his paintings to his writings - together with the Bengal School context in which he is placed as a painter. ✅ Answer within that recorded material and state the boundary rather than supplying a movement programme from memory. For (c), Lesson 15 supplies the formation by migration, the patron, the recorded character, the four text subjects, the Twelve Months series and the school's parentage of three further ateliers.

### 1986 - the Oriental School of Art

> Write a note on the origin and development of Oriental School of Art. *(CSE, 1986)*

**Directive.** *Write a note on the origin and development* - a chronological-analytical demand.

⚠️ **A disclosed terminological boundary.** The label **"Oriental School of Art"** is not used in this topic's evidence base, which uses **"Bengal School of Art"** for the movement originating in the works of Abanindranath Tagore in the early twentieth century. ✅ The disciplined approach is to answer on the movement the evidence describes, note the label used by the evidence, and not assert an equivalence the evidence does not state.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Origin: a reaction against the existing styles of painting, in Abanindranath Tagore's work in the early twentieth century, with Swadeshi purpose and the stated aim of reducing Western materialistic influence. Development: the eight recorded features, the *Arabian Nights* series and *Bharat Mata* of 1905, the explicit rejection of Ravi Varma, the named further painters and the Santiniketan connection, Sister Nivedita's mentorship and advocacy, and the qualification that Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks also shaped it. Lesson 17 carries all of this.

### 1985 - Mughal against Rajput

> What are the basic differences between Mughal Style of Painting and Rajput Style of Painting? *(CSE, 1985)*

**Directive.** *What are the basic differences* - a comparison demand asking for the grounds of difference, not a list of features.

**Conceptual demand.** The word "basic" is the instruction. The answer must reach the **underlying** differences - patronage structure, subject and source - rather than surface contrasts of colour.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Ground the comparison in patronage and institution first: an imperial salaried department against Rajput courts in which Maharajas, nobles and the business community all commission, with a department of painting recorded at one of them. Then subject: court life and individual portraiture against epics, Puranas, folklore and devotional themes. Then the recorded conventions, including the skin-tone convention distinguishing the two families. Then the recorded relation between them - the four explanations of Rajput painting's development, the arrival of Mughal artists at named Rajput centres, and the contrary evidence that Marwar became predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century. ⚠️ Close on the qualification that prevents a caricature: the schools are recorded as borrowing from a shared inheritance and then developing individually, and Rajput centres carry Deccani as well as Mughal influence.

## Neutral answer approach for the whole topic

⚠️ **Because no recent Mains demand is owned, the practical preparation question is what a future demand would look like.** The six owned Mains questions answer it with unusual clarity. Every one of them is either a **named-school or named-tradition description** (1993, 1990a, 1990c), a **distinction between two named things** (2011, 1985), a **motif study** (2012) or a **movement history** (1986). None asks for a general survey of Indian painting.

⚠️ **The two likeliest forms, and where each is prepared.** A **school-or-tradition description** is prepared by Lessons 14, 15, 16, 17 and 18, each of which is written as a fact set with named patrons, supports, materials and subjects. A **distinction demand** is prepared by the comparison tables in Lessons 9, 13, 14, 15, 16 and 18 and by the master comparisons below, and its defining requirement is sub-school-level control rather than family-level generalisation.

⚠️ **The Preliminary profile is different and is prepared differently.** **Six** Civil Services Preliminary items across **2009, 2013, 2017, 2018, 2019 and 2026** test, respectively, membership of a stated pair of Gupta-period mural examples, mural presence at three named places, the site of a named painting, the school of a named work, the reign associated with a change of format, and the subject of a named painting at a named cave site. All six are **membership or identification** items. They are prepared by the named lists and the identification rule in Lesson 19, not by argument.

❌ **No further PYQ claim is made.** No question is reconstructed from memory, no wording is invented, no marks or word limit is attached to any item, and no answer letter appears anywhere in this file.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

## Block A - Foundations, rock art and the mural idea, Lessons 1-3

**A1. Question:** State the classification that organises this topic, explain why that axis was chosen over chronology, and name one respect in which the evidence is asymmetric.

**Model answer:** The classification is five-fold by format: prehistoric rock art; mural painting; miniature painting; modern painting; and folk and tribal painting. The axis is format rather than chronology because these formats coexist rather than succeed one another - Kerala murals were being painted in the sixteenth century while Mughal albums were assembled and Jain manuscript painting continued, and folk traditions are being painted now - so a chronological account would interleave them and lose the explanation. Format also carries the causal content: each format implies a support, a patronage structure and a viewing public, from the twenty-five-square-inch portable page to the monumental prepared wall. The asymmetry is that the evidence is uneven across the five. Mural and miniature painting are documented site by site and school by school; the Deccani stream is named as one of the three medieval styles but carries no named court, painter, manuscript, date or technique; and Badami appears in the mural site list with no described programme at all. The asymmetry should be reported, never filled.

**A2. Question:** Distinguish the three kinds of rock art and attach each to its Indian period, then explain why the distinction is not merely terminological.

**Model answer:** A rock painting adds pigment to the surface and in India belongs mainly to the Upper Palaeolithic and Mesolithic. A rock engraving, or petroglyph, removes part of the rock by carving, incising or pecking, and belongs mainly to the Mesolithic and Neolithic; Konkan Geoglyphs, Kupgal Hill, Vikramkhol and Daraki-Chattan are named examples. A cupule is a small round artificial depression hammered or pecked into a rock surface, the simplest and oldest known form of rock art, and belongs mainly to the Lower Palaeolithic; Daraki-Chattan, Bhimbetka and Kupgal Hill are named examples. The distinction is not merely terminological for two reasons. It separates three different technical acts with different tools and different chronologies, so a site's technique constrains its likely date. And it prevents a category error in identification items, where engraving sites are routinely offered as distractors among painting sites.

**A3. Question:** Explain what the term "fresco secco" commits you to, and list the four consequences it has for the rest of this topic.

**Model answer:** It commits you to a dry-surface method: the murals are paintings done on already dried surfaces, with pigment resting on the plaster rather than binding into it as it would in wet-plaster true fresco. Four consequences follow. First, fragility: the paint film is susceptible to flaking, damp and abrasion, which is part of the explanation for the record that most rock-cut caves originally had mural paintings and only a few survive. Second, terminology discipline across every site: Ajanta and Ellora are named as fresco secco, Sittanavasal is recorded as "fresco technique with many mineral colours" and grouped with Ajanta and Bagh, and no distinct technique term is supplied for Bagh, so Ajanta's label is not transferable. Third, the survival argument in Lesson 19 rests on it, alongside immovable support and later overpainting. Fourth, conservation relevance: a surface-bound paint layer is what makes both the original murals and their twentieth-century replicas subjects of conservation rather than durable records.

## Block B - Cave and temple murals, Lessons 4-7

**B1. Question:** Set out the Ajanta fact set in the order that would survive an identification item, and identify the two facts most often merged.

**Model answer:** Carving span: second century BC to seventh century AD, out of volcanic rock. Form: approximately twenty-nine caves in a horse-shoe layout. Makers and patrons: inscribed by Buddhist monks, patronised by the Vakataka kings, especially King Harisena. Painting phases: Caves 9 and 10 Shunga, the rest Gupta. Themes: the life of the Buddha, historical and Jataka; Bodhisattvas; kings, queens, dancers; animals; floral and geometric designs. Style: fluid lines, graceful figures, naturalistic expressions, symbolic use of gesture. Technique and pigments: fresco secco on dry plaster, with red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, white from lime and black from lamp soot. Named works: the Cave 1 Bodhisattvas Vajrapani, Manjusri and Padmapani in *tribhanga*, denoting power, wisdom and compassion; The Dying Princess in Cave 16; chaitya-hall paintings in Caves 19 and 26; the reception of a Persian embassy by Pulakeshin II. The two facts most often merged are the excavation span and the patronage, which belong to different claims, and the painting phases, since assigning all the murals to the Gupta period erases Caves 9 and 10.

**B2. Question:** Explain how the Sittanavasal material converts a list of southern sites into an argument, and state the hedge that must travel with it.

**Model answer:** Sittanavasal supplies three things a list cannot. It supplies patronage - the Pandyas patronised the rock-cut Jain temple there and the Jain cave temple at Tirumalaipuram. It supplies meaning - the famous lotus-pond mural, with its green-hued pond, fish among leaves and flowers and human figures, is associated with the Jain *Samavasarana*, the first sermon after *keval gyan*, whose audience of celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish is called the *Bhavyas*, so the natural elements in the painting are the congregation. And it supplies direction - the paintings are said to provide a link between the Ajanta paintings of the fourth to sixth centuries and the eleventh-century Chola paintings at Thanjavur, which turns unconnected sites into a proposed chain with Armamalai's eighth- to ninth-century Pallava work under later Chola patronage as a second bridge. The hedge that must travel with it is the record's own "said to": the link is a proposed relationship, not a demonstrated transmission, and writing it as transmission overstates the evidence.

**B3. Question:** Four southern kingships produced four painting programmes for four different reasons. State each cause and its evidence in one sentence apiece.

**Model answer:** Chola painting follows imperial temple-building: over a thousand temples were built until 1279, Dravida temple art reached its height, and wall painting survives in two temples, with Brihadeswara carrying Shiva in his forms and a portrait of Rajaraja Chola I with his guru Karuvur Devar. Vijayanagara painting follows a deliberate redefinition of kingship: rulers refused identification with one linguistic group, emerged as protectors of the Hindu religion and shaped policy that transformed art production, with prosperity and migration producing a cosmopolitan culture, and with Abdur Razzak and Domingo Paes recording the magnificence of the wall paintings. Nayaka painting follows a stated ideology: they were not divine kings but believed in a policy of *bhoga*, admired Rama for physical charm rather than moral norms, and their temples, sculptures and paintings were accordingly opulent. Kerala painting follows trade: Greeks, Romans, Persians and Arabs traded its spices, Marco Polo and Ibn Batuta record the wealth, and that solid economic base led to temple architecture and wall painting from the sixteenth century with the Pallimana Temple at Thrissur.

## Block C - Northern walls, and the miniature from palm leaf to dispersal, Lessons 8-13

**C1. Question:** Compare the engines behind Rajasthani wall painting and Ladakhi monastic wall painting, and say what the comparison establishes.

**Model answer:** Rajasthani wall painting runs on three recorded drivers. Politically, Akbar bound the Rajputs through matrimonial alliance and mansabdari, and in the course of that association they learned to appreciate Mughal art and were exposed to Persian and Arabic traditions, which acted as a catalyst. Economically, the rise of the Marwari class and increased prosperity produced patronage in which Maharajas, nobles and the business community all commissioned. Religiously, the most powerful inspiration came from religion, with Krishna popularised as the deity of love and Jain communities also patronising wall art. Ladakhi wall painting runs on texts and practice instead. The *Sadhanmala* explains most of the wall paintings and elaborates 312 *sadhanas*; the *Hevajratantra* makes the five Dhyani Buddhas celestial beings to be visualised in meditation; the mandala is an instrument of *sadhana*; siddhas are painted to motivate followers; and monastic halls are described as space for wall painting and as objects of concentration and meditation. The comparison establishes that wall painting has no single explanation: one tradition is produced by alliance, commerce and devotion, the other by a manual of practice, and the right causal account has to be found for each tradition rather than assumed.

**C2. Question:** Trace the material and institutional chain from a Pala palm leaf to a Jahangir-period album page, naming the decisive step at each stage.

**Model answer:** The chain has five steps. Support: Pala painting between 750 and 1150 is executed on palm leaf or vellum within illustrated manuscripts, and its sinuous lines, subdued backgrounds, simple compositions and single figures are what a narrow folio permits. Material change: the Apabhramsa tradition of western India moves from palm leaf in its early Jain phase to paper later, and the illustrated-manuscript record dates the Jain move to paper to the fourteenth century, richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli - paper being the precondition for a larger, more flexible page. Synthesis: Sultanate painting fuses Persian elements carried by the artists' origins with indigenous traditions, prefers manuscript illustration, and produces the *Nimatnama* under Nasir Shah of Mandu, moving the illustrated book into court life. Institution: Akbar establishes a department and the salaried *Tasvir Khana*, recruits Indian artists alongside the Persian masters, adds three-dimensional figures, foreshortening and calligraphy, and shifts the subject from popular life to court life across seven great manuscripts. Object: under Jahangir the emphasis shifts from illustrated manuscripts to albums and to naturalistic individual portraiture, with decorated margins sometimes as elaborate as the paintings. The decisive steps are paper, the salaried department and the album.

**C3. Question:** State the dispersal mechanism precisely and show it operating at two different destinations.
