**Model answer:** The mechanism is that style resides in trained people rather than in courts, so a contraction of patronage relocates rather than destroys it. Under Aurangzeb, disinterest led to a decline in Mughal painting as artists moved to provincial courts, and the atelier's dispersal helped Rajasthan and Pahari centres; the atelier itself had been assembled from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa, so the workforce was already regionally distributed. At the first destination, Rajasthan, one of the four recorded explanations of Rajput painting's development is that Rajput courts developed local schools as Mughal artists arrived in Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh. At the second, the hills, many artists trained in the Mughal style migrated to the Kangra region after the decline of the Mughal empire and especially following Nadir Shah's invasion, received Rajput patronage there, and their migration led to the birth of the Guler-Kangra School, which reached its zenith under Raja Sansar Chand and went on to become the parent school of the ateliers at Kullu, Chamba and Mandi. Both destinations show transmitted skill becoming locally generative rather than producing copies.

## Block D - Regional schools, modern painting, living traditions and control, Lessons 14-19

**D1. Question:** Explain why sub-school-level control is the actual demand behind any Rajasthani-versus-Pahari comparison, and demonstrate it briefly.

**Model answer:** Because both labels name families, not styles, and a family-level contrast is a caricature that the evidence contradicts. On the Rajasthani side, Mewar resisted Mughal suzerainty longest and moves from Sahibdin's *Ragamala*, *Ramayana* and *Bhagavata Purana* illustration to detailed *tamasha* scenes of ceremonies and city life, with Nathdwara, Deogarh and Shahpura sub-styles; Amber-Jaipur, also called Dhundar, blends folk-style painting with occasional Mughal-influenced clothing and headgear and peaks under Sawai Pratap Singh's *suratkhana*; Marwar covers Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer and becomes predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century; Kishangarh, within Marwar, produces the Radha-like figure of *Bani Thani*. On the Pahari side, Basohli uses vibrant primary colours, lotus-shaped eyes and receding hairlines with contrasts borrowed from Malwa; Mandi uses dark muted colours on dark backgrounds under Raja Sidh Sen between 1684 and 1727; Kullu uses dull dark colours; Guler produces well-modelled female figures with upturned noses; and Kangra is characterised by an other-worldly feel and the Twelve Months paintings. Demonstrating the internal range on both sides is the answer; a single-sentence contrast between the two families is not.

**D2. Question:** Take the single current linkage recorded in this session and state exactly what it evidences and what it does not.

**Model answer:** The item is a report dated 28 July 2026 that the Ajanta paintings at the State Museum, Hyderabad, are to be restored in collaboration with the Noor International Microfilm Centre and the heritage department. What it evidences is threefold. It evidences anticipation of loss: the Hyderabad archaeological department, under Dr Ghulam Yazdani, initiated a project in the early twentieth century to create replicas of the Ajanta murals before the originals deteriorated further, and artists were specially sent to Ajanta to paint the "eye copies" on canvas. It evidences a second-order conservation problem: many of those century-old replicas have themselves suffered damage and now require stabilisation and restoration using traditional methods, so a copy made to outlast an original has become an object needing preservation. And it evidences the content of the Ajanta programme independently, since the replicated subjects are the life of the Buddha, Bodhisattvas, Jataka tales, royal courts, merchants, musicians, dancers and everyday life, including Padmapani and Vajrapani. What it does not evidence is anything about the present condition of the original Ajanta murals, anything about conservation policy or institutional structure, and anything beyond what a dated newspaper report of a museum and department position can carry.

**D3. Question:** Build the twenty-mark skeleton for a demand to trace the evolution of Indian painting, and name the two places where the answer is most likely to be lost.

**Model answer:** The skeleton has eight moves. One, a format-and-patronage thesis: painting's history is a sequence of formats, each matched to a patronage and a viewing public. Two, rock art with its three techniques and its period-wise palette and subject shift, ending at Bhimbetka's four periods. Three, the mural, defined by its prepared wall and its fresco secco technique, taken through Ajanta, Bagh, the southern chain and the four later kingships, with the survival rule stated. Four, the material break to the portable page: the twenty-five-square-inch rule, Pala and Apabhramsa, and the palm-leaf-to-paper shift. Five, the institutional break: Sultanate synthesis, Akbar's salaried department, and Jahangir's shift to albums and individual portraiture. Six, the dispersal and the regional schools, Rajasthani, Pahari, Deccani and South Indian, with sub-school naming. Seven, the market and the nation: Company and Bazaar painting, Ravi Varma, the Bengal School's double rejection, and the post-1947 refusal of a uniform style. Eight, the living traditions as production systems, with a qualified verdict. The two places the answer is most likely to be lost are the technique vocabulary, where "fresco" used loosely forfeits the discipline the whole answer rests on, and the regional section, where treating Rajasthani and Pahari as single styles collapses the evidence the answer has just built.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions below are **original**. None reproduces or paraphrases any official question recorded in this file.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The support, not the subject, is what distinguishes one Indian painting tradition from another." Comment.**

### Model

The claim is the more reliable identification rule, though it is not a complete account.

Subjects recur across unconnected traditions. Krishna appears on Kota's Rajmahal wall, in Kishangarh miniatures, in Kangra love scenes, in Guruvayur's Kerala murals, in Nathdwara's Pichhwai hangings, in Tanjore panels and in Madhubani paintings. Subject therefore cannot separate them.

Supports do. Warli is on mud walls in white pigment from gum and rice powder over red ochre; Tanjore is on glass and wooden planks, *Palagai padam*, with gold leaf, gemstones and cut glass; Mysore is on a gesso ground of white lead powder, gambose and glue; Pattachitra is on treated cloth finished with lacquer; Manjusha is on boxes of jute and paper; Thangka is on cotton framed in silk brocade; Santhal work is on hand-made paper backed with canvas.

The limit is that supports are shared too - palm leaf serves both Pala and early Jain painting - so support identifies best when read with material and community.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the recurrence of a single subject across at least four traditions 3 + at least five supports named with their materials 4 + the limit that supports can also be shared, with a named instance 2 + a rule that combines support, material and community 1.**

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"In Indian painting, an expensive material is a piece of patronage evidence." Examine.**

### Model

The proposition holds where the material has to be obtained rather than made, and it should be examined case by case.

At Ajanta the palette is mostly local - red ochre, yellow ochre, white from lime, black from lamp soot - but the blue is lapis lazuli, a mineral that must be acquired, so the programme implies resources beyond the site, which is consistent with monastic execution under Vakataka royal patronage. Jain illustrated manuscripts move to paper in the fourteenth century and are richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli, which fits their circulation as precious artefacts given as dowry and donated to monastery *bhandars*. Tanjore uses gold leaf, gemstones and cut glass under Maratha patronage, reaching its zenith under Serfoji II. Shah Jahan simply ordered artists to increase the use of gold and silver.

The limit is that cheapness proves nothing in reverse: Warli's rice-powder white and Santhal's vegetable colours reflect method and community, not poverty of patronage.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = lapis lazuli at Ajanta read against a locally obtainable palette 3 + one manuscript and one southern case with named materials and patrons 4 + the Shah Jahan instruction as direct evidence 1 + the asymmetry that inexpensive materials do not evidence weak patronage 2.**

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"Indian painting repeatedly organises itself around classificatory schemes rather than around individual invention." Discuss.**

### Model

The observation is well supported across formats and periods, and the discussion should show the schemes at work before qualifying them.

The earliest is prescriptive: the six limbs of Indian art, the *Shadanga* outlined in Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra* in the third century AD - appearance, proportion, expression, aesthetic composition, resemblance and use of brush and colours - is a standard against which work is judged. Ancient texts then classify painting forms themselves: Lepya Chitra, Lekhya Chitra, Dhuli Chitra and Pata Chitra, with the *Mudrarakshasa* adding Cauka, Dighala and Yama Pitaka.

Later schemes organise whole sets. Ragamala painting builds series on the Garland of Ragas, personifying each raga by a colour, placing a *nayaka* and *nayika* in a particular mood, specifying season and time of day and often naming the associated deity, with six principal ragas - Bhairava, Deepak, Sri, Malkaush, Megh and Hindola - and four family versions, Pahari, Rajput, Deccan and Mughal. Kangra's Twelve Months paintings attempt the effect of each month on human emotion. Ladakh's mural programme is specified by texts: the *Sadhanmala* elaborates 312 *sadhanas*, the *Hevajratantra* fixes the five Dhyani Buddhas, and the mandala arranges them with sometimes sixteen Bodhisattvas and eight goddesses.

The qualification is that schemes did not suppress individuality. Akbar's salaried artists were able to develop individual styles, and five are named; Basohli's Devi Das, Mewar's Sahibdin and Kishangarh's Nihal Chand are identifiable within schemes.

Classification supplied the commission; invention occurred inside it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the *Shadanga* and the textual form-vocabularies as prescriptive schemes 4 + Ragamala unpacked with colour, mood, season, time, deity, six ragas and four families 5 + the Twelve Months series and the Ladakh textual programme 4 + the qualification from named individual painters working inside schemes 2.**

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"Every major change in Indian painting was preceded by the movement of people." Critically examine.**

### Model

The proposition is strongly supported for the medieval and early modern period and weaker at both ends, which is where the criticism lies.

The supported core is extensive. Sultanate painting is a synthesis of Persian elements borrowed from the artists' origins with indigenous traditions - the carrier is the painter. Humayun, exiled to Persia after losing the throne to Sher Shah Suri, acquired Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali at Shah Tahmasp's court and brought them back; they created the *Tutinama* under Akbar. Akbar then recruited talented Indian artists who had served previous rulers, which is the recorded origin of "Indian influence", and assembled an atelier from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa. Aurangzeb's disinterest sent artists to provincial courts; Mughal artists arrived at Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; and Mughal-trained artists migrating to Kangra after Nadir Shah's invasion founded the Guler-Kangra School. The Deccan sultanates' artists and artworks are recorded as a decisive factor in Rajput painting's development, and Vijayanagara's prosperity promoted migration that profoundly influenced art.

The criticism has two parts. Some changes are internal rather than migratory: Jahangir's shift from manuscripts to albums and portraits reflects a patron's taste, and Shah Jahan's turn to artificial elements and unnatural stillness is an instruction to an existing workshop. And some traditions record the opposite: Santhal painters are said never to have come under Mughal, Rajput or British influence.

Movement explains transmission; patronage and local practice explain selection and refusal.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the artists'-origins formulation plus the Humayun recruitment chain 4 + Akbar's recruitment and the multi-regional atelier 3 + two dispersal destinations plus the Deccan contribution 4 + the internal-change counter-cases from two reigns 3 + the Santhal counter-instance and a transmission-versus-selection verdict 1.**

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Indian painting is better evidence for the history of patronage than for the history of taste." Critically examine.**

### Model

The proposition is largely correct because patronage is recorded directly while taste is usually inferred, but the evidence contains enough explicit statements of preference to require qualification.

Patronage is documented with unusual precision. Ajanta was patronised by the Vakataka kings, especially Harisena; Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, 700-720 CE, patronised the best Pallava wall painting; Brihadeswara carries Rajaraja Chola I with his guru Karuvur Devar; Tirumala Nayaka, 1623-1659, patronised the Meenakshi paintings and Raghunath Nayaka, 1600-1633, the Brihadeswara *Parikrama* additions; Raja Kirpal Pal commissioned Basohli's *Rasamanjari*; Sawai Pratap Singh maintained a *suratkhana*; Akbar ran a salaried *Tasvir Khana*; Maratha rulers patronised Tanjore, which peaked under Serfoji II; and Mary Impey and Marquess Wellesley patronised Company painting. Structural patronage facts are equally recorded: Marwari commercial wealth, Jain community patronage, Kerala's spice-trade base, and the business community among Rajasthani patrons.

But taste is sometimes stated outright rather than inferred. The Nayakas are recorded as rejecting divine kingship for a policy of *bhoga* and admiring Rama for physical charm rather than moral norms, with opulent art as the stated consequence. Jahangir is recorded as a naturalist by nature who preferred flora and fauna. Shah Jahan is recorded as liking artificial elements and brighter palettes and as ordering more gold and silver. Akbar is recorded as looking upon paintings as a means of study and amusement.

Patronage is therefore the better-evidenced history, but in this record the two are frequently the same sentence, and a strong answer says so.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = at least six named patronage cases across three formats 6 + structural patronage facts beyond individual patrons 4 + at least three explicit statements of recorded taste 6 + the observation that patronage and taste are often recorded in the same statement 3 + a qualified verdict 1.**

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"A painting tradition survives not because it is admired but because its production system survives." Critically examine, using both historical schools and living traditions.**

### Model

The proposition is a useful corrective, and it must be tested against both halves of the topic.

For historical schools the evidence supports it strongly. Mughal painting did not end because it ceased to be admired: Aurangzeb's disinterest withdrew the patronage and artists moved to provincial courts, and the style continued wherever the production system was rebuilt - at Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh, and at Kangra, where Mughal-trained migrants receiving Rajput patronage founded a new school. Conversely, admiration without a system preserves nothing: only two Pallava mural locations and two Chola mural temples survive, nothing significant remains at Tirumalaipuram, and most rock-cut caves that originally had murals have lost them, because a fresco-secco film on an immovable wall has no production system to renew it.

For living traditions the proposition is almost definitional. Pichhwai persists through a chain of ritual origin, temple use in the Pushtimarg Sampradaya and a Nathdwara export trade. Patua persists by changing support to stitched paper, medium to poster colours and subject to social and political themes while retaining its travelling, singing performance. Madhubani persists through transmission by women, named practitioners from Jagdamba Devi's recognition in 1970 onwards, and geographical-indication protection. Paitkar, by contrast, is recorded as nearing extinction.

Two qualifications matter. Institutional preservation can substitute for a living system, as replica-making and museum conservation show. And *shaastradaan*, the Jain donation of manuscripts to *bhandars*, shows that a religious practice can function as a preservation system in itself.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the Mughal dispersal as survival-by-relocation 4 + the mural survival record as admiration-without-a-system 4 + three living traditions each analysed as a production system 6 + Paitkar as the negative case 2 + both qualifications, institutional substitution and *shaastradaan* 3 + a qualified verdict 1.**

# REMEDIATION

## Common-error map

| # | Error | Correction |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Explaining "miniature" as "a small painting" | The word derives from *minium*, the old Latin word for red lead paint, with the verb *miniare*; the size ceiling is a separate technical precondition |
| 2 | Quoting the miniature rule as twenty-five inches | Not larger than twenty-five **square** inches, with the subject at not more than one-sixth of actual size |
| 3 | Calling any Indian mural "true fresco" | The recorded method is painting on already dried surfaces; Ajanta and Ellora are named as fresco secco |
| 4 | Transferring the fresco secco label to Bagh | No distinct technique term is supplied for Bagh; Sittanavasal is recorded as "fresco technique with many mineral colours" |
| 5 | Treating "rock art" as equal to "rock painting" | Painting adds pigment, engraving or petroglyph removes rock, cupule is a pecked hollow; each has its own dominant Indian period |
| 6 | Placing cupules in the Mesolithic | Cupules belong mainly to the Lower Palaeolithic |
| 7 | Reversing the Upper Palaeolithic colour code | Red for hunters, green mostly for dancers |
| 8 | Saying most Bhimbetka paintings are Upper Palaeolithic | Four periods are present; most belong to the Mesolithic |
| 9 | Using 1957 or 1867-68 as painting dates | 1957 is Wakankar's discovery of Bhimbetka; 1867-68 is Carlyle's first discovery of rock paintings in India |
| 10 | Assigning all Ajanta murals to the Gupta period | Caves 9 and 10 are Shunga; the rest are Gupta |
| 11 | Calling Padmapani a Buddha | He is a Bodhisattva, Avalokiteshvara, symbol of compassion, shown holding a lotus |
| 12 | Moving The Dying Princess out of Cave 16 | It is in Cave 16; the Bodhisattva trio is in Cave 1 |
| 13 | Treating the Pulakeshin II panel as Chalukya patronage of Ajanta | Ajanta's recorded patronage is Vakataka; the panel is a subject, not a patron record |
| 14 | Choosing one Bagh date range silently | The record gives both 5th-6th and 5th-7th century ranges, both mainly Gupta |
| 15 | Naming a Bagh cave for the Hallisalasya dance | It is attributed to the Bagh paintings generally, not to a named cave |
| 16 | Equating Hallisalasya with a named modern classical dance form | Prohibited; the recorded description stops at "devotional or courtly" |
| 17 | Confusing the three Rang Mahals | Bagh Cave 4 is one; the Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort is another; a third bears the same name elsewhere |
| 18 | Naming Ellora or Sittanavasal as a Gupta-period mural example | The recorded pair is Ajanta and Bagh |
| 19 | Claiming extensive Ellora painting | Five caves, mostly Cave 16, largely ceilings; newer than Ajanta; smaller in scale and more decorative |
| 20 | Supplying Badami painting content | Badami is named in the mural site list with no described programme |
| 21 | Claiming surviving painting at Tirumalaipuram | Nothing significant remains now |
| 22 | Reading Mahendravarman's epithets as biography | *Vichitra Chitta*, *Chettakri* and *Chitrakarppulli* are honorific appellations |
| 23 | Treating the Pallava survival record as evidence of a thin tradition | Two surviving locations limit claims about scale, not about quality |
| 24 | Asserting the Ajanta-Sittanavasal-Thanjavur transmission | The record says the link is *said to* exist |
| 25 | Describing the Sittanavasal lotus pond as nature study | It is associated with the *Samavasarana*, whose audience includes birds, animals and fish |
| 26 | Placing Ravan Chhaya in the south | Keonjhar district, Odisha |
| 27 | Omitting the Nayaka superimpositions at Brihadeswara | Rajaraja-period work carries Nayaka-period overpainting |
| 28 | Calling Lepakshi a Nayaka programme | Vijayanagara, sixteenth century, at the Veerbhadra temple |
| 29 | Upgrading the Lepakshi-Kalamkari relation | The murals are *seen as* the progenitor or precursor |
| 30 | Describing Kerala murals as temple-only | Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram and Matancheri palaces hold some of the richest |
| 31 | Mixing the three dated southern patrons | Raja Wodeyar 1578-1617; Raghunath Nayaka 1600-1633; Tirumala Nayaka 1623-1659 |
| 32 | Calling Bundi an unmixed Rajput idiom | Its wall paintings carry Mughal and Deccani influences |
| 33 | Calling Kota flat and stylised | Its paintings are noted for realism, with a predominance of red |
| 34 | Omitting the business community from Rajasthani patronage | Patrons were Maharajas, nobles and the business community; Jain communities also patronised |
| 35 | Treating the Ladakh mandala as decoration | It is an instrument of *sadhana*, with a Dhyani Buddha at the centre |
| 36 | Calling the Dhyani Buddhas historical | The *Hevajratantra* conceptualises them as celestial beings for visualisation |
| 37 | Giving Apabhramsa features to Pala painting | Fish-shaped bulging eyes and double chins are Apabhramsa; Pala is sinuous, subdued, simple, single-figure |
| 38 | Treating Apabhramsa as exclusively Jain | Jain themes were commonest; Vaishnava use, *Gita Govinda* and secular love are recorded |
| 39 | Calling the *Nimatnama* a religious manuscript | It is a book on cookery, made under Nasir Shah of Mandu, Malwa Sultanate |
| 40 | Confining Lodi Khuladar to Delhi | It was followed between Delhi and Jaunpur |
| 41 | Crediting Akbar with recruiting the two Persian masters | Humayun acquired them at Shah Tahmasp's court in exile |
| 42 | Treating the *Tasvir Khana* as a style | It is a salaried studio within a department for illustration and scribing |
| 43 | Attributing a whole Akbari manuscript to one painter | Production was collaborative in a multi-regional atelier |
| 44 | Assigning the album-and-portrait shift to the wrong reign | It is recorded under Jahangir |
| 45 | Swapping the two titles | Ustad Mansur is *Nadir ul Asr*; Abu'l Hasan is *Nadir-al-Zaman* |
| 46 | Saying manuscript illustration ceased under Jahangir | The *Ayar-i-Danish* was illustrated in his reign |
| 47 | Presenting Mughal painting as a smooth rise to naturalism | Shah Jahan turned to artificial elements and unnatural stillness |
| 48 | Supplying Deccani courts, painters or dates | None is carried in the evidence base |
| 49 | Treating the Deccan only as a recipient | Deccan sultanate artists are recorded as a decisive factor in Rajput painting's development |
| 50 | Presenting one origin story for Rajput painting | Four explanations are recorded and none is settled |
| 51 | Placing Kishangarh outside Marwar | It comes under the Marwar School |
| 52 | Asserting flat single authorship of *Bani Thani* | Kishangarh School, associated with Nihal Chand |
| 53 | Saying Marwar became more Mughal over time | It became predominantly Rajput after the eighteenth century |
| 54 | Treating Pahari as one style | Multiple schools across twenty-two princely states from Jammu to Almora |
| 55 | Making Kangra the earliest Pahari school | Basohli, Mankot, Kullu, Mandi and Jammu are recorded from the seventeenth century; Kangra from the mid-eighteenth |
| 56 | Resolving the *Shangri Ramayana* | It is recorded both as produced in Jammu and as created at Kullu |
| 57 | Swapping Tanjore and Mysore materials | Tanjore: gold leaf, gemstones, cut glass, glass and wooden planks. Mysore: gesso paste of white lead powder, gambose and glue |
| 58 | Misdating the Mysore Ganjifa GI | 2008 |
| 59 | Crediting the Mughals with originating illustrated manuscripts | The tradition began with the Pala rulers under Ramapala in the eleventh century |
| 60 | Assuming Bazaar painting is more indigenous than Company painting | Bazaar painting is recorded as having minimal Indian content |
| 61 | Calling Ravi Varma a Bengal School precursor | The school rejected his art as imitative and westernised |
| 62 | Reducing the Bengal School to "traditional painting" | Eight features are recorded, including the wash technique and Ajanta-derived line |
| 63 | Assigning a uniform style to the Progressive Artists Group | It drew on European Modernism explicitly without a uniform style |
| 64 | Completing a clipped source entry | The manuscript details, the Company-Bazaar cells and three artist names are clipped and are never completed |
| 65 | Merging Warli and Saura | Warli: Gujarat-Maharashtra border, red ochre ground, gum-and-rice-powder white, central *chaukat*, Palaghata. Saura: Saura Tribe of Odisha, red or yellow grounds, plant and mineral colours |
| 66 | Merging Madhubani and Tikuli | Tikuli is on hardboard polished with enamel coats, though it often uses Madhubani motifs |
| 67 | Confusing the scroll lengths | Phad 15-30 feet; Cheriyal up to 45 feet |
| 68 | Calling Thangka a secular folk form | It is Buddhist, made by monks and specific ethnic groups |
| 69 | Describing living traditions as frozen survivals | Support, medium, subject and market are recorded as changing |
| 70 | Asserting current institutional or status positions | Only the dated facts recorded here are asserted; current positions are routed for verification |

## Targeted drills

**Drill 1 - technique vocabulary.** For each of Ajanta, Ellora, Bagh and Sittanavasal, write the exact technique statement the evidence supports, and mark which of the four has no distinct technique term.

**Drill 2 - the three rock-art acts.** Write the three technical definitions, attach the dominant Indian period to each, and assign every named site in Lesson 2 to one or more of the three.

**Drill 3 - the closed pair.** Write the sentence naming the two examples of Gupta-period mural painting, then name three other cave sites taught in this topic and state exactly what each one is recorded as.

**Drill 4 - the southern chain.** Reconstruct the chain from fifth-century literary mention to eleventh-century Thanjavur, naming every site, patron and hedge along the way.

**Drill 5 - four kingships.** For Chola, Vijayanagara, Nayaka and Kerala, write the cause of the painting programme in one sentence each, with one named site and one named patron or witness apiece.

**Drill 6 - Rajasthani wall-painting regions.** For each of Harauti, Marwar, Bikaner, Jaisalmer, Mewar and Amber, name the fort or palace, the earliest recorded location within it, and two themes.

**Drill 7 - the Ladakh programme.** Name the two iconographic texts, the number of *sadhanas*, the five-figure group they specify, the structure of a mandala, and the three principal named monasteries with what each holds.

**Drill 8 - early miniature comparison.** Complete the Pala-versus-Apabhramsa grid from memory on region, dates, religion, support, line, eye, colour and named names.

**Drill 9 - the Mughal sequence.** Write the six-reign table with, for each reign, the object being made, the dominant subject and one named technical or material fact.

**Drill 10 - Jahangir's people.** Name the two titled masters with their exact titles, the four named works of the chief portrait painter, the one manuscript illustrated in the reign, and the three famous naturalistic animal subjects.

**Drill 11 - Deccan boundary.** Write the five recorded Deccan statements and then write, as a separate list, the five categories of Deccani detail that are not carried and must never be supplied.

**Drill 12 - Rajasthani sub-schools.** For Mewar, Amber-Jaipur, Marwar and Kishangarh, write the patron or painter, the trajectory, and one named work or output type.

**Drill 13 - Ragamala.** Write the four families, the six principal ragas, and the five things a Ragamala painting encodes beyond its figures.

**Drill 14 - Pahari colour map.** Place Basohli, Mankot, Kullu, Mandi, Guler and Kangra on a scale from vibrant primary to soft modelled, and give the recorded colour statement for each.

**Drill 15 - southern miniature materials.** Write the Tanjore material chain and the Mysore material chain without overlap, then name the shared ancestry of both.

**Drill 16 - the modern sequence of refusals.** For Company painting, Bazaar painting, Ravi Varma, the Bengal School and the Progressive Artists Group, write what each accepted and what each refused.

**Drill 17 - Bengal School features.** Write all eight recorded features, then mark the two that are technical rather than thematic.

**Drill 18 - folk support sort.** Sort all seventeen named folk and tribal traditions into wall or floor, cloth, and paper or board, and give the distinguishing material for each.

**Drill 19 - close-pair discrimination.** For Warli against Saura, Madhubani against Tikuli, Phad against Cheriyal against Patua, and Tanjore against Mysore, write the single most reliable discriminator in each case.

**Drill 20 - the hedge list.** Write out every hedged statement in this topic with its exact hedging word, and then write the safe formulation for each.

**Drill 21 - the attribution ladder.** Take five claims from across the topic, assign each to a level, and rewrite any that is currently written at the wrong level.

**Drill 22 - the survival argument.** Write the survival rule, the three distinct causes of loss with a named instance each, and the symmetrical limit on arguing from absence.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 - the format-and-patronage master table

| Format | Support | Patronage | Viewing context | Named cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Rock art** | Rock shelter, cave wall, open rock face; quartzite walls recorded | Pre-state communities; no commission | Open, communal | Bhimbetka, Lakhudiyar, Adamgarh Hills, Gudahandi |
| **Cave mural** | Prepared plaster on rock-cut walls and ceilings | Monastic establishment with royal endowment | Collective, devotional, in situ | Ajanta, Bagh, Ellora, Sittanavasal, Armamalai |
| **Temple and palace mural** | Prepared wall of a built structure | Royal, temple trust, or noble and mercantile | Public devotional, or courtly | Kailashnath Kanchi, Panamalai, Brihadeswara, Lepakshi, Meenakshi, Guruvayur, Bundi Fort, Jodhpur Fort |
| **Monastic mural** | Hall walls of a monastery | Monastic community | Practice - an object of concentration and meditation | Alchi Sumstek, Hemis Dukhang, Tabo |
| **Manuscript miniature** | Palm leaf, vellum, then paper | Monastic, then Jain community, then royal | Private, page by page | Pala manuscripts; *Kalpasutra*, *Kalakacharyakatha*; *Nimatnama*; the seven Akbari manuscripts |
| **Album page** | Paper, with decorated margins | Individual imperial | Connoisseurial | Jahangir-era portraiture and natural history |
| **Regional court miniature** | Paper | Rajput and hill courts, through departments such as the *suratkhana* | Court and devotional | Mewar, Amber-Jaipur, Marwar, Kishangarh; Basohli, Guler, Kangra, Mankot, Kullu, Mandi, Jammu |
| **Southern panel** | Glass and wooden planks; gesso ground | Maratha and Mysore rulers, continuing into the British period | Devotional, domestic and shrine | Tanjore, Mysore |
| **Card** | Hand-painted card | Courtly and artisanal | Play | Ganjifa, popular in Mughal courts; Mysore Ganjifa |
| **Colonial hybrid** | Paper and cloth | East India Company officers and named individual patrons; commercial buyers | Private collection and sale | Company painting at six named centres; Bazaar painting |
| **Canvas and print** | Canvas | Market and nationalist public | Mass circulation and exhibition | Raja Ravi Varma; Bengal School; Progressive Artists Group |
| **Community and ritual** | Mud wall, cloth, scroll, paper, hardboard, jute-and-paper boxes | Household, shrine, monastery, and now market | Ritual occasion, performance, and export | The seventeen folk and tribal traditions |

⚠️ **The mechanism sentence that turns this table into an argument:** as the payer changes, the **size, portability, subject and audience** of painting change with them.

## Map 2 - the causal chain of the whole topic

```text
  PIGMENT ON ROCK (no patron, no building)
     |  invention of the prepared plaster surface
     v
  MURAL: fixed, monumental, collective
     |  requires a building and an endowment
     |  technique = paint on a dried surface -> FRAGILE
     |  support cannot move -> LOSS WHEN THE BUILDING FAILS
     |  later programmes overpaint earlier ones -> LOSS BY SUCCESSION
     v
  SURVIVAL BECOMES THE CENTRAL EVIDENTIAL PROBLEM
     |  2 Pallava locations | 2 Chola temples | nothing at Tirumalaipuram
     v
  PORTABLE PAGE: palm leaf -> PAPER (14th c. in the Jain record)
     |  <= 25 square inches; subject at <= 1/6 actual size
     |  needs only a patron, an artist and material
     v
  MANY SMALL CENTRES BECOME POSSIBLE
     |  Sultanate synthesis: Persian elements arrive WITH THE ARTISTS
     v
  THE SALARIED DEPARTMENT (Tasvir Khana; later the suratkhana)
     |  style now resides in TRAINED PEOPLE, not in a person
     v
  THE ALBUM PAGE: independent image -> INDIVIDUAL PORTRAITURE
     |  patronage contracts (Aurangzeb; Nadir Shah's invasion)
     v
  DISPERSAL -> Bikaner, Jodhpur, Kishangarh; Kangra
     |  receiving courts select what they adopt
     v
  REGIONAL SCHOOLS DEVELOP INDIVIDUALLY
     |  colonial commission and market arrive
     v
  COMPANY AND BAZAAR PAINTING -> RAVI VARMA (academic realism, epic subject)
     |  technique becomes a political question
     v
  BENGAL SCHOOL: wash technique + Ajanta line + Swadeshi purpose
     |  a double refusal
     v
  AFTER 1947: no uniform style required
     |
     +-- running underneath all of it, from before the first mural to now:
         COMMUNITY AND RITUAL PAINTING, sustained by material, community,
         occasion and, latterly, market
```

## Map 3 - argument map, the four disputes this topic actually contains

```text
  DISPUTE 1: Did Rajput painting derive from Mughal painting?
    FOR: Mughal artists arrived at Bikaner, Jodhpur, Kishangarh;
         Bundi shows Mughal influence; matrimonial alliance and
         mansabdari brought Rajputs into contact with Mughal art
    AGAINST: local and indigenous traditions are recorded as PREDATING
         Mughal influence; Deccan sultanate artists are recorded as
         DECISIVE; the ganga-jamni Sultanate confluence is a fourth
         explanation; Marwar became PREDOMINANTLY RAJPUT after the 18th c.
    VERDICT: four explanations are recorded, none settled, and they are
         not mutually exclusive

  DISPUTE 2: Does scarcity of surviving murals evidence a thin tradition?
    FOR: only two Pallava locations and two Chola temples survive
    AGAINST: fresco secco is fragile; the support cannot be moved;
         overpainting destroys without decay; literary mention in
         Silappadikaram and Manimekalai attests practice before survival
    VERDICT: survival constrains claims about SCALE, not about QUALITY;
         and absence evidences nothing in either direction

  DISPUTE 3: Was the Bengal School a return to an authentic Indian manner?
    FOR: Indian themes; Ajanta inspiration; Swadeshi purpose; explicit
         rejection of Ravi Varma as imitative and westernised
    AGAINST: its own recorded features include Mughal and Rajasthani
         influence; Abanindranath produced Mughal-influenced paintings;
         Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks shaped it;
         Sister Nivedita's wish was for MASS REPRINTING - a print public
    VERDICT: a programmatic claim about what Indian art should be,
         not an archaeological recovery of what it was

  DISPUTE 4: Is folk and tribal painting a survival or a living system?
    FOR SURVIVAL: great antiquity is claimed - Warli since 2500-3000 BC,
         Gond for nearly 1400 years, Patua over a thousand years;
         Paitkar is recorded as nearing extinction
    FOR LIVING SYSTEM: Madhubani now on paper or cloth; Patua now in
         poster colours on stitched paper with social and political
         themes; Warli now on cloth with white poster colours; Saura
         designs now in modern clothing; Pichhwai now a major export
    VERDICT: antiquity and adaptation are both recorded; the tradition
         persists BECAUSE the production system adapts
```

## Map 4 - the seven answer spines

| Demand shape | Spine |
|---|---|
| **Evolution of Indian painting** | Format-and-patronage thesis → rock art with its three acts → mural with technique and survival → the portable page and its size rule → the salaried department → the album and portraiture → dispersal and regional schools → market, nation and post-1947 → living traditions → verdict |
| **Mughal painting and its legacy** | Workshop thesis → Humayun's exile recruitment → Akbar's department, recruitment and subject shift → Jahangir's album, portrait and natural history → Shah Jahan's reversal → Aurangzeb and dispersal → two named destinations → the borrowing-plus-individual-development formula → verdict |
| **Rajasthani against Pahari** | Refuse the family-level contrast → four recorded origin explanations → four Rajasthani sub-schools with patron, trajectory and output → seven Pahari centres on colour and subject → the shared repertoire that prevents caricature → verdict |
| **Murals as historical evidence** | Survival-bias thesis → the survival record → three distinct loss mechanisms → what murals do evidence: patronage, diplomacy, technology, doctrine → the limits of selection and dating → verdict |
| **Painting and the nation** | Nationalist-art thesis → Company and Bazaar patronage → Ravi Varma's synthesis and its public → the Bengal School's eight features and double refusal → art inside state iconography → the qualification of transnational shaping → post-1947 refusal of a uniform style → verdict |
| **Folk and tribal painting today** | Living-tradition thesis → material, community, occasion, market as the four axes → four or five named traditions across the axes → recognition facts with their dates → Paitkar as the negative case → the boundary to society and to crafts → verdict |
| **A dynasty's contribution to painting** | Name the cause of the programme → named surviving works with patron and theme → technique or material where recorded → the survival caveat stated explicitly → one comparison with a neighbouring programme → verdict |

## Map 5 - the sixteen dated anchors

| Date | Anchor |
|---|---|
| **3rd century AD** | Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra* outlines the *Shadanga* |
| **750-1150 AD** | The Pala School |
| **700-720 CE** | Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, patron of the best Pallava wall painting |
| **11th century** | Illustrated manuscripts begin with the Pala rulers under Ramapala; Chola paintings at Thanjavur |
| **11th-15th centuries** | The Apabhramsa School predominant in Western India |
| **14th century** | Arrival of Muslims brings a cultural renaissance; Jain manuscripts move to paper |
| **15th century** | *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharya Katha* |
| **16th century** | Lepakshi murals; the Kerala tradition evolves with the Pallimana Temple; Vallabhacharya credited with introducing Pichhwai |
| **1562** | Raja Bharmal's daughter marries Akbar |
| **1614** | Jahangir carves Kota out of Bundi for Madho Singh; Mewar becomes mansabdar |
| **1684-1727** | Raja Sidh Sen's new style at Mandi |
| **1867-68** | Archibald Carlyle makes the first discovery of rock paintings in India |
| **1905** | *Bharat Mata* by Abanindranath Tagore |
| **1947** | The Progressive Artists Group forms after this year |
| **1957 and 2003** | Bhimbetka discovered by V. S. Wakankar; declared a World Heritage Site |
| **1970, 2007, 2008** | Jagdamba Devi honoured; Cheriyal geographical-indication status; Mysore Ganjifa geographical-indication status |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Scope, reading discipline and boundaries

Five formats: prehistoric rock art; mural; miniature; modern; folk and tribal. Four reading questions in order: support, technique, patron, subject - style last. Three date-lines plus a fourth for living traditions: when made; when the phase ran; when found or protected; when institutionally recognised. Boundaries: buildings to architecture; carved images and attribute doctrine to sculpture; textual transmission to languages and manuscripts; dye and print trades to crafts; law, institutions and changeable status to heritage; Swadeshi politics to modern history; community conditions to society.

## Classical vocabulary

*Shadanga*, six limbs, from Vatsyayana's *Kamasutra*, third century AD: **Roopbheda** appearance, **Pramana** proportion and structure, **Bhava** expression, **Lavanya Yojana** aesthetic composition, **Sadrishya** resemblance, **Varnikabhanga** use of brush and colours. Ancient painting forms: **Lepya Chitra** framed drawings, **Lekhya Chitra** line drawings, **Dhuli Chitra** floor paintings, **Pata Chitra** on cloth or palm leaves. Styles in the *Mudrarakshasa*: **Cauka Pitaka** isolated framed, **Dighala Pitaka** long scrolls, **Yama Pitaka** isolated paintings.

## Rock art: three acts, three periods, two sites

Painting adds pigment - mainly Upper Palaeolithic and Mesolithic. Engraving or petroglyph removes rock - mainly Mesolithic and Neolithic: Konkan Geoglyphs, Kupgal Hill, Vikramkhol, Daraki-Chattan. Cupule is a pecked hollow, the simplest and oldest form - mainly Lower Palaeolithic: Daraki-Chattan, Bhimbetka, Kupgal Hill. Upper Palaeolithic 40,000-10,000 BC: quartzite shelter walls; ochre or *geru* with lime and water; red, white, yellow, green; large animals in white, dark red and green; red for hunters, green mostly for dancers. Mesolithic 10,000-4000 BC: red the main colour; smaller paintings; group hunting, grazing, riding. Chalcolithic 2500-700 BC: more green and yellow; battle scenes with horses and elephants; bows and arrows. Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi samples prove late-historical habitation. Narsinghgarh: spotted-deer skins drying, evidencing perfected tanning. Deccan sites dominated by cattle, suggesting agro-pastoralism. Dating: Carbon-14, thermoluminescence, OSL, uranium/thorium. Early discoverers: Cockburn, Anderson, Mitra, Ghosh; first discovery of rock paintings 1867-68 by Archibald Carlyle. Bhimbetka: south of Bhopal, Vindhyan Range, in Ratapani Wildlife Sanctuary; V. S. Wakankar, 1957, from a train between Bhopal and Itarsi; World Heritage 2003; one of seven hills; 642 shelters, nearly 400 marked; four periods, most Mesolithic; 29 animal species, chital commonest; 16 shades, white and light red commonest; x-ray style; the Bhimbetka Boar with a rhinoceros snout; Shelter 3 the Auditorium with a child's left handprint, Shelter 4 with over 400 figures, Shelter 8 with armour-clad soldiers and cavaliers, Shelter 9 in red, green and yellow on a deep white base. Other Mesolithic MP sites: Kharwar, Jaora, Kathotia, Lakhajoar. Lakhudiyar: "one lakh caves", Suyal River, Almora; finger-painted in red, white and black.

## Mural definition, technique and site list

Murals are artworks on walls or a solid structure, dated between the 10th century BC and the 10th century AD for the ancient bracket, with the practice continuing later. Sites: Ajanta, Ellora, Armamalai, Ravan Chhaya, Bagh, Tirumalaipuram, Chithannavasal or Sittanavasal, Badami Cave Temples; mostly caves or rock-cut chambers; mainly Hindu, Buddhist and Jain themes. Technique: painting on already dried surfaces; **fresco secco** named for Ajanta and Ellora; six colours recorded for the rock-cut tradition - white, red, yellow, black, green, blue; Ajanta pigments red ochre, yellow ochre, lapis lazuli for blue, lime white, lamp-soot black; Sittanavasal recorded as fresco technique with many mineral colours; **no distinct technique term for Bagh**. Badami is named with no described programme; Tirumalaipuram retains nothing significant.

## Ajanta

Oldest surviving murals of the subcontinent; carved 2nd century BC to 7th century AD in volcanic rock; approximately 29 caves, horse-shoe layout; inscribed by Buddhist monks, patronised by the Vakataka kings, especially Harisena. Caves 9 and 10 Shunga, the rest Gupta. Themes: Buddha's life, historical and Jataka; Bodhisattvas; kings, queens, dancers; animals; floral and geometric designs. Style: fluid lines, graceful figures, naturalistic expressions, symbolic gesture. Jatakas named: Vessantara, Sibi, Shaddanta, Mahajanaka; Jataka = previous births in human and animal form, each inculcating a virtue; Shibi offers his flesh for a pigeon; Matriposhaka's ungrateful man betrays the elephant. Cave 1 Bodhisattvas in *tribhanga*: Vajrapani power, Manjusri wisdom, Padmapani or Avalokiteshvara compassion with a lotus. The Dying Princess, Cave 16; The King Janaka also recorded. Caves 19 and 26: chaitya-hall Buddha preaching among devotees and celestial beings. The reception of a Persian embassy by Pulakeshin II. Recorded as images or sculptures, not paintings: Mahaparinirvana, the assault of Mara, seated Buddha in Dharmachakrapravartana mudra.

## Ellora and Bagh

Ellora: mostly 6th-9th centuries AD; Rashtrakutas and others; fresco secco; five caves, mostly Cave 16, the Kailasha Temple; all three religions; newer than Ajanta; smaller and more decorative; four of five recorded groups are ceiling work - divine couples and celestial beings; Vishnu's incarnations and courtly scenes; floral panels with Buddhas and Bodhisattvas; dancing figures, lotus medallions, flying couples; lotus flowers, celestial beings and Tirthankaras with attendants. Bagh: bank of the Baghni river, Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh; nine Buddhist caves; majority viharas; architecturally very similar to Ajanta; emphasis on interior murals; "Ajanta of Central India"; two recorded date ranges, 5th-6th and 5th-7th centuries, both mainly Gupta; Cave 4 = Rang Mahal = the most significant cave, with Jataka murals; ceiling panels with floral and geometric motifs; Buddhist themes in the light of contemporary lifestyle, also secular in nature; figures more tightly modelled, stronger outlines, more earthly and human; "more materialistic than spiritualistic" as a relative descriptive formula to be attributed and qualified; Hallisalasya dance recorded as a classical Indian dance form performed in royal courts, temples and ceremonial gatherings, in the Gupta period often depicted as a devotional or courtly dance in art and sculpture, attributed to the Bagh paintings generally. Closed pair: the two examples of Gupta-period mural painting are Ajanta and Bagh.

## The southern chain

Tamil wall painting from the 7th century AD; literary mention in *Silappadikaram* and *Manimekalai* in the 5th. Mahendravarman: first great patron; *Vichitra Chitta*, *Chettakri*, *Chitrakarppulli*; introduced rock-cut cave temples; wall paintings at Mamandur. Narasimhavarman II Rajasimha, 700-720 CE: Kailashnath Kanchi ambulatory, Shiva-Parvati legends; Panamalai, Parvati watching Shiva's dance; royal figures and a Jain Muni in the Jain caves of Annamalai Hill. Only two Pallava mural locations survive. Pandya patronage: Sittanavasal and Tirumalaipuram. Sittanavasal: 1st century BC to 10th century AD; *Arivar Koil*; Tirthankara sculpture and murals on walls, ceilings and pillars; recorded as "fresco technique with mineral colours", reported as the record's own phrase with no wet-plaster claim; lotus pond with green hue, fish among leaves and flowers, human figures, associated with *Samavasarana* - the first sermon after *keval gyan*, whose audience of celestial beings, humans, birds, animals and fish are the *Bhavyas*, within the five events of birth, renunciation, *keval gyan*, first sermon and Nirvana; *Ezhadippattam* with 17 polished rock beds and Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions, used for *Sallekhana* and *Kayotsarga* for over a thousand years since the 1st century BC; *Navach-chunai*, a submerged rock-cut temple. Said to link Ajanta, 4th-6th centuries, with the 11th-century Chola paintings at Thanjavur. Ravan Chhaya: Keonjhar, Odisha; half-opened-umbrella rock shelter; described as "ancient fresco paintings" in the record's own word; believed a royal hunting lodge; royal procession, 7th century AD. Armamalai: Shiva rock-cut cave, 8th-9th century murals, Pallava period later patronised by the Cholas, recorded as leading to the later "Chola frescoes" at Brihadisvara in the record's own word; originally a Jain monastery; Tirthankaras, monks, devotees; tales of the *Astathik Palakas* on walls and roof; evidences the Jain-to-Shaiva transition and the Ajanta-Bagh-to-South-India continuity.

## Four kingships

Chola: over a thousand temples until 1279; wall painting in only two temples; Vijaya Cholishwaram the first, with faint lines only; Brihadeswara the most outstanding centre and the biggest centre of wall painting in India after Ajanta - Shiva in Kailash, the devotee Sundarar, Tripurantaka, Nataraja, Dakshinamurti, Lingodbhava, and a portrait of Rajaraja Chola I with his guru Karuvur Devar; Rajaraja-period work with Nayaka-period superimpositions. Vijayanagara: largest state ever founded in south India; refused identification with one linguistic group; protectors of the Hindu religion; policy shaped art production; prosperity promoted migration and a cosmopolitan culture; Abdur Razzak and Domingo Paes record the wall paintings; Tirupparutikanram Jain temple with a beautiful damsel and Indra with Airavat; Lepakshi. Lepakshi: Andhra Pradesh; Veerbhadra temple; 16th century; Rang Mandapa with 70 carved pillars; hanging pillar; incomplete Kalyana Mandapa; giant naga hood over a linga; largest monolithic Nandi in the country; ceiling mural of Veerabhadra Swamy of more than 300 square feet, considered the largest single-figure fresco in India - a size claim in the record's own wording, carrying no technique claim; Ravana shaking Kailash, Shiva's marriage, Dakshinamoorthy, Arjuna's penance, Dashavatara, Arjuna winning Draupadi; seen as the progenitor of Kalamkari. Nayaka: Vijayanagara governors turned rulers; estates at Thanjavur, Madurai, Mysore, Gingee; not divine kings; policy of *bhoga*; Rama admired for physical charm; opulent art; Tirupparutikanram at Kanchipuram best preserved, *Adipurana* themes; Meenakshi Sundareshwar under Tirumala Nayaka 1623-1659, Shaivite and highly ornamental; Sri Ranganatha at Tiruchirappalli; Brihadeswara *Parikrama* under Raghunath Nayaka 1600-1633; Prasanna Venkataramana at Mysore under Raja Wodeyar 1578-1617; Shravanabelagola for the best Nayaka-period Jain wall paintings. Kerala: spice trade with Greeks, Romans, Persians and Arabs; Marco Polo and Ibn Batuta; the economic base led to temple architecture and wall painting; tradition evolved in the 16th century with the Pallimana Temple, Thrissur; Trivanchikulam, Triparayar, Padmanabhaswamy, Pundareekapuram; Guruvayur, "Dwarka of the South", with Krishna's childhood; richest paintings at Padmanabhapuram, Krishnapuram and Matancheri palaces; movement, rhythm and colour; prominent voluminous male figures; modelled muscular and highly ornamented bodies; a great deal of emotion.

## Northern walls

Rajasthani drivers: Mughal contact through matrimonial alliance and mansabdari plus exposure to Persian and Arabic traditions, as catalyst; the rise of the Marwari class, with Maharajas, nobles and the business community as patrons; religion as the most powerful inspiration, with Krishna as the deity of love and Jain community patronage. Harauti = Kota and Bundi, Hada Rajput country on the Delhi-to-Deccan route. Bundi: oldest and most beautiful wall paintings; Bundi Fort most extensive; earliest in Badal Mahal; early Rajasthani style with Mughal and Deccani influences; Rukmini's abduction, Sita's rescue, Nala-Damayanti, flute-playing Krishna, Ras-Leela; Adinatha Jain Temple with the earliest and most beautiful Jain wall paintings in Rajasthan. Kota: carved out of Bundi by Jahangir in 1614 for Madho Singh; outshone Bundi; Kota Fort - Rajmahal with Krishna's childhood and Vrindavan, Bada Mahal with royal hunt, durbar, Holi and Kaliya Mardan; noted for realism; predominance of red. Marwar: Rathores descended from the Rashtrakutas; Jaisalmer to Ajmer; house of Jodhpur or Marudesh; soldiers plus businessmen; wall painting from the 18th century; Jodhpur Fort, earliest in Phool Mahal; Raga-Ragini, Ragamalas, Krishna-Radha, Nayaka-Nayika Bheda; Teeja Mata Mandir Gas Mandi on Rama-Sita. Bikaner: Rao Bika, son of Rao Jodha; close military association with the Mughals; Chandra Mahal, then Anup Mahal and Shivabari Temple. Jaisalmer: Bhatti clan; Rang Mahal of Jaisalmer Fort; Krishna, Shiva-Parvati, Ganesh, Riddhi-Siddhi. Mewar: descent claimed from Kusha; state from at least 734 AD; mansabdar by 1614 but never submitted and refused matrimonial alliances; wall painting from the early 18th century; Pichola Lake Palace under Maharana Bhim Singh; Krishna Kumari's room among the best in Rajasthan, which does not mention her sacrifice; Govardhan Vilas, Mamaji Ki Haveli, Shiv Rati Ki Haveli. Amber: first to ally; Bharmal's daughter married Akbar in 1562; Man Singh the major patron; Jai Singh II founded Jaipur; Madho Singh 1777-1802 commissioned paintings in the Jaipur city palace. Pahari murals: Rajput migration into Himachal from Prayagraj, Ayodhya, Bundelkhand and Rajasthan, and into Jammu from MP, Bihar and Bengal; mansabdari under Akbar brought Mughal contact; Himachal under Raja Sansarchand, Gauri Shankar Temple at Sujanpur Tira, plus Dharamshala, Kangra, Kulu, Chamba; Jammu temple walls mainly Rama, Dogra dynasty, Gulab Singh. Ladakh: *Sadhanmala* explains most of the wall paintings and elaborates 312 *sadhanas*; *Hevajratantra* makes the five Dhyani Buddhas celestial beings for visualisation, with female counterparts; mandala as a mystic circle and instrument of *sadhana*, Dhyani Buddha at the centre, sometimes 16 Bodhisattvas and 8 goddesses; siddhas painted to motivate followers; halls as objects of concentration and meditation; Alchi with the three-storey Sumstek and images of Maitreya, Vairochana and Manjushri; Hemis with the *Dukhang*; Tabo in Spiti, Mahayana and Vajrayana; also Lamayuru, Mang Gyu, Saspol, Phiyang, Tsemo, Basgo, Shey, Thiktse, Spituk; parallel development in Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan and Arunachal, with Sinon, Namchi and Lachung in Sikkim.

## The miniature: definition and two beginnings

From Latin *minium*, red lead paint, verb *miniare*. Preconditions: not larger than 25 square inches; subject at not more than one-sixth of actual size. Conventions: profile; bulging eyes, pointed nose, slim waist; Rajasthani brown skin, Mughal fairer tones; divine beings such as Krishna blue; women with long black hair and eyes; men in traditional attire with turbans. Developed between the 8th and 12th centuries in the eastern and western regions. Pala: 750-1150; illustrated manuscripts on palm leaf or vellum; sinuous lines, subdued backgrounds, simple compositions, single figures; patronised by rulers supporting Buddhism, especially Vajrayana; painters Dhimman and Vitapala. Apabhramsa: Gujarat and the Mewar region; predominant in Western India 11th-15th centuries; Jain themes commonest, later Vaishnava; *Gita Govinda* and secular love introduced; palm leaf then paper; red, yellow and ochre with symbolic meaning, later bright colours and gold; fish-shaped bulging eyes, pointed nose, double chin, angular faces, stiff postures, rich detail; *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharya Katha*, 15th century. Prefer "Western Indian or Jain school" as the descriptive label.

## Transition, Sultanate and the three streams

14th century: arrival of Muslims brings a cultural renaissance; a style akin to the Deccan school emerges in the Vijayanagara South. Transition features: flat colour; dress and outlines demarcated in black; three-quarter face; detached appearance. Sultanate: synthesis of Persian elements from the artists' origins with indigenous elements; preference for illustrating manuscripts; *Nimatnama*, a book on cookery, under Nasir Shah of Mandu, Malwa Sultanate; Lodi Khuladar followed between Delhi and Jaunpur. Three major styles then dominate: Mughal, Rajput, Deccan; artists borrowed from Sultanate art but also developed their own individual styles.

## Mughal painting

Foreshortening added: objects drawn so they look closer and smaller than they really are. Babur: little time; said to have patronised Bihzad, who illustrated the Mughal family tree. Humayun: great patron; atelier disrupted by defeat by Sher Shah Suri and exile to Persia; acquired Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali at Shah Tahmasp's court; they brought Persian influence, made illustrated albums, and under Akbar created the *Tutinama*. Akbar: an entire department for illustration and scribing; the *Tasvir Khana*, salaried, allowing individual styles; paintings as a means of study and amusement; recruited talented Indian artists who had served previous rulers, the recorded source of "Indian influence"; three-dimensional figures; continued foreshortening; encouraged calligraphy; popular art transformed into court art; painters Abdus Samad, Mir Sayyid Ali, Daswanth, Basawan, Kesu Das; manuscripts *Tutinama*, *Razmnama*, *Baburnama*, *Hamzanama*, *Tarikh-i-Alfi*, *Anvar-i-Suhaili*, *Gulistan of Sadi*; the atelier assembled from Gwalior, Gujarat, Lahore, Kashmir and Malwa, working collaboratively. Jahangir: the zenith; a naturalist by nature preferring flora and fauna; the shift from illustrated manuscripts to albums with naturalism in individual portraiture; decorated margins sometimes as elaborate as the paintings; his own private workshop; famous naturalistic zebra, turkey and cock; Ustad Mansur titled *Nadir ul Asr*, expert in the most complex faces; *Ayar-i-Danish* illustrated in the reign; Abu'l Hasan 1569-1627, chief portrait painter, titled *Nadir-al-Zaman*, allegorical paintings with a subtle agenda reinforcing supremacy, legitimacy and divine right, Jahangir towering over a globe; his works *Jahangir with portrait of Akbar in his hand*, *Jahangir Standing on a Globe Shooting Poverty*, *Squirrels in a Plane Tree*, *Jahangir embracing Persian Safavid Emperor Shah Abbas*. Shah Jahan: rapid change of tenor; artificial elements; said to have reduced liveliness and brought unnatural stillness, inspired by the European style; charcoal replaced by pencil; increased gold and silver; brighter palettes. Aurangzeb: disinterest led to decline as artists moved to provincial courts; dispersal helped Rajasthan and Pahari centres.

## Deccan, stated within its evidence

One of three major medieval styles; a style akin to the Deccan school emerged in the Vijayanagara South; a Deccan Ragamala family exists; Bundi carries Deccani alongside Mughal influence; an influx of artists and artworks from the Deccan sultanates is recorded as decisive for Rajput painting's development; characterised by saturated colour, elongated figures, fantastic landscape and Persianate-Deccan court culture, and distinguished from Mughal painting despite shared Persianate roots. Not carried and never supplied: named courts, painters, manuscripts, dates or techniques.

## Rajasthani schools and Ragamala

Regional works also called Central Indian Painting: vibrant colours; epics, Puranas, folklore; differing from the Mughal style. Rajasthani School more or less synonymous with the Rajput School. Four recorded explanations of its development: arriving Mughal artists at Bikaner, Jodhpur and Kishangarh; a decisive influx from the Deccan sultanates; indigenous traditions predating Mughal influence; and the *ganga-jamni* Sultanate confluence. Mewar: resisted longest, agreeing under Shah Jahan; Sahibdin in the 17th century illustrating *Ragamala*, *Ramayana*, *Bhagavata Purana*; later court life and *tamasha* scenes; sub-styles Nathdwara, Deogarh, Shahpura. Amber-Jaipur: the Amber or Dhundar school at Bairat, palace walls and the Amer Palace mausoleum; folk-style with occasional Mughal-influenced clothing and headgear; pinnacle under Sawai Pratap Singh, builder of the Hawa Mahal, whose *suratkhana* made *Bhagwata Purana*, *Ramayana*, Ragamala and portraits. Marwar: Jodhpur, Bikaner, Jaisalmer; 15th-16th century court life in colourful attire; initially Mughal-influenced, predominantly Rajput after the 18th century; under Man Singh 1803-1843, *Shiva Purana*, *Natacharitra*, *Durgacharitra*, *Panchtantra*. Kishangarh, 17th-18th century, within Marwar: Sawant Singh and Bani Thani; Nihal Chand with prince Sawant Singh and poet Nagari Das; *Bani Thani* - a Radha-like figure with lotus-like elongated eyes, thin lips, pointed chin and a side profile defined by the *odhni*; many devotional and amorous Radha-Krishna works. Ragamala: based on the Garland of Ragas; an amalgamation of art, poetry and classical music; four families - Pahari, Rajasthan or Rajput, Deccan, Mughal; each raga personified by a colour, with a *nayaka* and *nayika* in a mood, the season and the time of day or night, and often the associated deity such as Bhairava or Bhairavi to Shiva and Sri to Devi; six principal ragas - Bhairava, Deepak, Sri, Malkaush, Megh, Hindola.

## Pahari schools

Sub-Himalayan states under Mughal influence; multiple schools across 22 princely states from Jammu to Almora; 17th-19th century; dynamic mythological and literary scenes. Three of the greatest painters: Nainsukh, Manaku, Sansar Chand. Basohli from the 17th century, Jammu and Kashmir: miniature paintings; lotus-shaped eyes, receding hairlines, vibrant primary colours; Mughal cloth techniques blended with local styles; first patron Raja Kirpal Pal, commissioning Bhanudatta's *Rasamanjari*, *Gita Govinda* and *Ramayana* drawings; most famous painter Devi Das; contrasting colours borrowed from Malwa. Mankot from the 17th century: closely resembles Basohli; vivid colours, bold subjects; portraiture general from the mid-17th century; later naturalism and muted colours. Guler from the 18th century, Himachal: name from "Galwa", cowherd; similarities with Mughal art; strong strokes with fine and sensitive expressions; *Srimad Bhagawata*, *Bihari Satasai*, *Gita Govinda*; well-modelled female figures with upturned nose and minutely done hair. Kangra from the mid-18th century: born of Mughal-trained artists migrating after the empire's decline, especially following Nadir Shah's invasion, and receiving Rajput patronage - the Guler-Kangra School; zenith under Raja Sansar Chand; sensuality and intelligence the other schools lacked; *Gita Govinda*, *Bhagwata Purana*, *Satsai* of Biharilal, *Nal Damayanti*; Krishna love scenes prominent; an other-worldly feel; Twelve months paintings on the effects of the months on human emotions, popular until the 19th century; parent school to Kullu, Chamba and Mandi; Sansar Chand Museum. Jammu or Dogra from the 17th century: remarkable resemblance to Kangra; *Shangri Ramayana* of the late 17th-early 18th centuries produced in Jammu, on a mythological theme. Kullu from the 17th century: similar to Basohli; *Shangri Ramayana* with Bal Kanda, Ayodhya Kanda and Uttar Kanda in dull, dark colours. Mandi from the 17th century: new style under Raja Sidh Sen, 1684-1727; mostly miniatures; rulers with exaggerated features; Shiva, Rama, Krishna; dark muted colours on dark backgrounds.

## South Indian miniatures, cards and manuscripts

South Indian miniatures emerged in the early medieval period, using gold heavily and focusing on divine figures rather than rulers; both Tanjore and Mysore originate from Vijayanagara painting and then Nayaka painting. Tanjore: probably developed from the defeat of the Thanjavur Nayakas by the Marathas and the fall of Vijayanagara; Maratha patronage in the 18th century; on glass and wooden planks, *Palagai padam*, instead of the north's cloth and vellum; brilliant colour patterns and liberal gold leaf; gemstones and cut glass on larger-than-life images, mostly Krishna; zenith under Maharaja Serfoji II; geographical-indication status awarded. Mysore: patronised by the rulers of Mysore, continuing into the British period; Hindu gods and goddesses; two or more figures with one predominating in size and colour; gesso paste of white lead powder, gambose and glue producing a sheen; muted colours contrasting with the background. Ganjifa: medieval card game; hand painted; popular in Mughal courts; each suit a different colour; referenced in the *Baburnama*; Mysore Ganjifa geographical-indication status in 2008. Illustrated manuscripts: pictorial translations of epic or literary verses with text in a demarcated space; precious artefacts often gifted to princesses as dowry; tradition began with the Pala rulers of Bengal and Bihar, notably under Ramapala in the 11th century, on palm leaves; Jain *shaastradaan* gifted manuscripts to monastery *bhandars*, early on palm leaf and later on paper in the 14th century, richly adorned with gold and lapis lazuli, depicting *Tirthipatas*, mandalas and secular stories; every Mughal successor contributed to enhancing their status; named manuscripts *Kalpasutra* and *Kalakacharyakatha* of the Svetambara sect, *Sangrahini Sutra* written in the 12th century, and *Uttaradhyana Sutra*, with details clipped in the record and not completed.

## Modern painting

Modern Indian painting blended Indian art with European styles and themes, giving rise to Company and Bazaar painting. Company painting, *Kampani Kalam*: colonial; hybrid of Rajput, Mughal and other Indian styles with European elements; Kolkata, Chennai, Delhi, Patna, Varanasi, Thanjavur; patrons East India Company officers, Mary Impey, Marquess Wellesley. Bazaar painting: colonial; European and Roman influence with minimal Indian content; Bengal and Bihar regions; British officials and patrons; remaining cells clipped. Raja Ravi Varma, 1848-1906: Kerala; "Father of Modern Indian Art"; South Indian painting with Western techniques of colour and style; *Ladies in the Moonlight*, *Shakuntala*, *Damayanti and Swan*; nationwide recognition for *Ravana kidnapping Sita and killing Jatayu*; the film *Rang Rasiya*. Bengal School: supposed to have arisen as a reaction against the existing styles of painting, in Abanindranath Tagore's work in the early 20th century; *Arabian Nights* series; Swadeshi values; reducing Western materialistic influence; *Bharat Mata*, 1905; eight features - Indian traditions and themes such as Mahakali, Shiva-Parvati and Krishna with the Gopis; influence of Ajanta with its rhythm, grace and harmony; linear delicacy mirroring Ajanta lines; softness and rhythm in figures; a wash technique with subdued colours; impact of Mughal and Rajasthani schools; exquisite use of light and shade; impressive and distinctly Indian literary, religious and historical subject matter; the painters rejected Ravi Varma's art as imitative and westernised; qualified by Japanese wash techniques and transnational networks. Nandalal Bose: Santiniketan; white-on-black Dandi March Gandhi sketch, iconic in the 1930s; illustrated the original document of the Constitution; sketched the Bharat Ratna and Padma emblems; *Shiva and Sati*. Rabindranath Tagore: dominant black lines; small-sized paintings; some art historians argue a link with his writings. Others: Asit Kumar Haldar, Manishi Dey, Mukul Dey, Sunayani Devi. Legible pairs: *Journey's End* - Abanindranath Tagore; *Tiller of the Soil (Indian Farmer)* - Nandalal Bose; other rows clipped and not completed. Sister Nivedita, Margaret Elizabeth Noble, 1867-1911: Irish-born educationist, author, social activist and thinker; women's education and empowerment; promoted science and art; awakened national consciousness; mentored young Bengal School artists; urged them away from aping Western art; her recorded assessment of *Bharat Mata* as "the first great masterpiece in a new style", with the wish to reprint it by tens of thousands. Indian Cubism: European-inspired; objects broken down and reassembled in abstract forms; balance of line and colour; M.F. Hussain's horse motif for fluidity of motion. Progressive Artists Group: after 1947; six founders - F.N. Souza, S.H. Raza, M.F. Hussain, K.H. Ara, H.A. Gade, S.K. Bakre; bold progressive themes with softer abstract ones; European Modernism without a uniform style.

## Folk and tribal traditions

Pichhwai - Rajasthan, mainly Nathdwara; Krishna as Sreenathji; temple hangings for the Pushtimarg Sampradaya; now a major export; Vallabhacharya credited with introducing it in the 16th century. Madhubani or Mithila - Bihar; by women; mentioned in the Ramayana and believed to begin in the Ramayana era; deities and symbolic motifs such as fish for luck and fertility; now on paper or cloth; bold colours, floral patterns, double borders, two-dimensional figures; recognition in 1970 with Jagdamba Devi; Baua Devi, Bharti Dayal, Ganga Devi, Mahasundari Devi, Sita Devi; geographical-indication status because the art remained confined to a specific area. Tikuli - Bihar; from *tikuli*, bindi; hardboard cut into shapes, enamel coats; Madhubani motifs. Pattachitra - Odisha; *patta* plus *chitra*; treated cloth; burnt coconut shell and lamp black; red or yellow brush outlines, colour fill, foliage and flowers, intricate framing, lacquer gloss; Jagannath and Vaishnava, sometimes Shakti and Shaiva; Raghurajpur; similar to old murals at Puri and Konark; on palm leaf, *talapattachitra*. Patua - Bengal; over a thousand years old; scrolls of *Mangal Kavyas*; Muslim Patuas travel and sing; now poster colours on stitched paper with social and political themes; Medinipur, Murshidabad, South 24 Parganas, Birbhum. Kalamkari - named after the *kalam*; treated, bleached, softened cotton; bamboo pen wrapped with cloth; black outlines then vegetable dyes; Srikalahasti and Machilipatnam; freehand mythological and Persian-influenced block-printed; dates to the Vijayanagara era; geographical-indication status. Kalighat - 19th-century Kolkata near the Kalighat Temple; hand-coloured on cloth; deities, epic scenes, later everyday life; influenced painters such as Jamini Roy. Warli - Gujarat-Maharashtra border; recorded as practised since 2500-3000 BC; resembles Bhimbetka murals; central *chaukat* with daily life, nature and festivals; Palaghata the fertility goddess; mud walls; triangles, circles, squares; white from gum and rice powder on red ochre; by women for auspicious occasions; today also on cloth with poster colours. Thangka - Sikkim, Himachal, Ladakh, Arunachal; originally a medium of reverence evoking the highest ideals of Buddhism; by monks and specific ethnic groups; cotton, natural dyes, symbolic colour, silk brocade frames. Manjusha - Bhagalpur, Bihar; also Angika art and snake painting; on boxes of jute and paper. Phad - Rajasthan; religious scroll; Pabuji and Devnarayan; vegetable colours on cloths of 15-30 ft; large-eyed figures; procession scenes. Cheriyal - Telangana; Nakashi scroll; epics and Puranic tales like comics with balladeers' music; up to 45 feet; geographical-indication status in 2007. Pithora - Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh tribal communities; religious and spiritual purpose; peace and prosperity at family occasions; animals, especially horses. Saura - Saura Tribe of Odisha; similar to Warli; ritual wall mural; white on red or yellow with plant and mineral colours; stick-like geometric figures; now in modern clothing designs. Paitkar - Jharkhand; ancient scroll tradition; Maa Manasa; rituals and life after death; nearing extinction. Gond - Madhya Pradesh mud walls for nearly 1400 years; also AP, Maharashtra, Chhattisgarh, Odisha; all natural elements sacred. Santhal - Jadu Patua, magic painters, of the Bengal-Bihar borders; never under Mughal, Rajput or British influence; hand-made paper sometimes backed with canvas; natural vegetable colours.

## Control layer

Attribution ladder, descending: recorded identification at the object; recorded patronage and date; recorded technique, material and formal property; attribution of a work to an individual artist. The **twenty** contested or bounded points each have a safe formulation, and they include the unresolved question of the 2026 Preliminary item's printed number. Survival rule: two Pallava locations, two Chola temples, nothing significant at Tirumalaipuram, faint lines at Vijaya Cholishwaram, Sittanavasal not at its best, and few surviving rock-cut cave murals - constraining claims about scale, not quality. Three loss mechanisms: fragile dry-surface technique; immovable support; overpainting by succession, as at Brihadeswara. Single current linkage, dated 28 July 2026: the State Museum, Hyderabad, is to restore its Ajanta Paintings Gallery of hand-painted canvas "eye copies", about 40 on display and the rest in store, depicting the Buddha's life, Bodhisattvas, Jataka tales, royal courts, merchants, musicians, dancers and everyday life including Padmapani and Vajrapani, in collaboration with the Noor International Microfilm Centre and the heritage department, using traditional methods; the replicas originate in an early-twentieth-century project of the Hyderabad archaeological department under Dr Ghulam Yazdani, begun before the originals deteriorated further. Nine demand shapes in the demand map; five reusable theses; mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20; and a risk list whose avoidance is worth more than any additional fact.
